A graph theoretic model for the derived categories of gentle algebras and their homological bilinear forms
Jesús Arturo Jiménez González, Andrzej Mróz

Abstract
We customize the existing models for the bounded derived category of gentle algebras to obtain simple graph theoretic tools to analyze indecomposable objects, Auslander-Reiten triangles, and their interaction with the associated homological bilinear form and the Coxeter transformation. We apply these tools to explore related new and classical derived invariants. We exhibit the non-negativity and Dynkin type of the homological quadratic form of a gentle algebra, classify indecomposable perfect complexes by means of its roots, describe the Coxeter polynomial and relate it with the Avella-Alaminos Geiss invariant. We also derive some consequences for Brauer graph algebras.



Journal:

2020 MSC
primary:
16G20 , 16E35 , 05E10 , 16G70 secondary:
16E20 , 17B22 , 05C76

1  Introduction

The computation and characterization of derived categories is an important task in many areas of mathematics, because of their heavy content of homological information. Some of the homological invariants that have attracted attention of representation theorists over the last decades involve Grothendieck groups, Hochschild (co-)homology and Auslander-Reiten quivers. Also a prominent example is the homological bilinear form, associated quadratic (Euler) form and the Coxeter transformation, classically defined for the algebras of finite global dimension, whose study helped to develop the representation theory of quivers and many other classes of algebras, especially in connection with representation types, root systems and tilting theory in the works of Gabriel
Gabriel [1972]
, Bongartz
Bongartz [1983]
, Dlab-Ringel
Dlab and Ringel [1976]
, Kac
Kac [1980]
, Reiten-Skowroński
Reiten and Skowroński [2003]
, Brüstle-de la Peña-Skowroński
Brüstle et al. [2011]
, Happel
Happel [1997]
, Lenzing-de la Peña
Lenzing and de la Peña [2008]
, Mróz-de la Peña
Mróz and de la Peña [2016]
, Zhang
Zhang [1989]
, to mention a few examples. Descriptions of such homological invariants are difficult in full generality, thus efforts are usually directed to classes of algebras of particular interest, gentle algebras in our case.

Before their formal introduction, the underlying combinatorics of gentle algebras appeared in several instances within representation theory: in the study of representations of the Lorentz group
Gelfand and Ponomarev [1968]
, self-reproducing systems
Nazarova and Roiter [1973]
, representations of groups
Bondarenko and Drozd [1982]
,
Bondarenko [1975]
,
Ringel [1975]
and skew-group algebras
Nazarova and Roiter [1969]
. These ideas have been systematized and generalized in the framework of matrix problems as clans
Crawley-Boevey [1989]
, bushes
Deng [2000]
, bundles of chains
Bondarenko [1991]
or bunches of chains
Burban and Drozd [2017]
, structures that have in common an initial combinatorial setting: a
disjoint union of (semi-) chains with an involution
. After the development of tilting theory in the 1980s, such structures were recognized independently as a particular class of well-behaved special biserial algebras, starting with the tilted classification of hereditary algebras of Dynkin type
\mathbb{A}

Assem and Happel [1981]
and the formal introduction of
gentle algebras
by Assem-Skowroński for type
\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}

Assem and Skowroński [1987]
, and soon after in the derived discrete
Bobiński et al. [2004]
,
Vossieck [2001]
and piecewise hereditary
Happel and Zacharia [2008]
classifications, see also
Happel [2001]
,
Reiten and den Bergh [2002]
. An early explicit connection between these two approaches can be found in Bekkert-Merklen’s description of indecomposable complexes of the derived category of gentle algebras
Bekkert and Merklen [2003]
following techniques presented in
Bondarenko [1975]
. Many homological aspects of gentle algebras came to light with the help of repetitive algebras and Happel’s embedding functor, cf.
Ringel [1997]
, from their closedness up to derived equivalence
Schröer and Zimmermann [2003]
, the description of their derived categories and almost split triangles in terms of
string combinatorics

Bobiński [2011]
,
Arnesen et al. [2016]
, to the construction of purely combinatorial derived invariants
Avella-Alaminos and Geiss [2008]
that describe part of the tubular structure of the derived category (the latter also with help of clans). Recently, graded versions of gentle algebras were related to Fukaya categories of Riemann surfaces for considerations in homological mirror symmetry
Haiden et al. [2017]
,
Lekili and Polishchuk [2020]
and to cluster combinatorics as Jacobian algebras for certain quivers with potentials
Labardini-Fragoso [2009]
. These connections prompted extensive research in the area and, together with developments on symmetric special biserial algebras and Brauer graph algebras in terms of
ribbon graphs

Marsh and Schroll [2014]
,
Schroll [2015]
, motivated the construction of geometric models for the derived category of (graded) gentle algebras
Haiden et al. [2017]
,
Opper et al. [2018]
,
Palu et al. [2019]
successfully applied for the characterization of derived equivalence within these algebras
Lekili and Polishchuk [2020]
,
Amiot et al. [2023]
. The presentation of the geometric model in
Opper et al. [2018]
is based on the notion of
marked ribbon graphs
and their embeddings into marked oriented surfaces, where the reformulation of a gentle algebra as disjoint union of chains with an involution (having no fixed point) was exploited again. In this geometric framework, the surface, its curves and their intersections serve as model for the derived category of a gentle algebra.

Here we propose a reformulation of the geometric model to study the derived category of a gentle algebra purely in terms of marked ribbon graphs, which may be considered as a discrete version of existing surface presentations.
Our exposition makes no direct use of newly developed geometric methods and considerably simplifies parts of the classical string combinatorics. Our “graph model” is based on three technical results dealing with the presentation of string complexes as reduced walks of the marked ribbon graph and the corresponding presentation of bands (Propositions
3.21
and
3.37
, compare to
Bekkert and Merklen [2003]
), and the construction of Auslander-Reiten triangles, which simplifies the well-known construction of Bobiński
Bobiński [2011]
(Proposition
6.4
); see also Remark
3.39
. The presentation is intended to provide simple tools for the construction and analysis of Auslander-Reiten components and the homological bilinear form of gentle algebras. Although the homological form is easy to compute for concrete examples (its Gram matrix in an appropriate basis is the Cartan matrix of the algebra), the description of its properties in a unified manner is challenging work. With the proposed graph model and the use of the
incidence matrix
of a graph as connection to linear aspects of quadratic forms as in
Jiménez González and Mróz [2026]
, we explore the definiteness of the homological bilinear form, related new and classical derived invariants, and its interrelation with the classification of indecomposable objects of the derived category. The results of
Jiménez González and Mróz [2026]
form a combinatorial framework for our present study, and they may be viewed as yet another path leading to combinatorics of gentle algebras (this time from algebraic graph theory and the theory of integral quadratic forms), see also
Jiménez González [2018]
.

In the paper we work with finite dimensional (bound quiver) algebras over a field
k
, see Section
3
for the details. Direct inspiration for our main results below are the following well-known facts concerning a specific class of gentle algebras, proved when
k
is algebraically closed. Namely, by Assem-Skowroński
Assem and Skowroński [1987]
, derived hereditary algebras of type
\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}
(thus having non-negative Euler form of corank 1 and Dynkin type
\mathbb{A}
) are precisely the 1-cycle gentle algebras
A=kQ/I
such that the bound quiver
(Q,I)
satisfies the
clock condition
(cf. Remark
4.7
). Whereas, if a 1-cycle gentle algebra
A
does not satisfy the clock condition, then by the results of Vossieck
Vossieck [2001]

A
is derived discrete and not derived hereditary (cf.
Schröer and Zimmermann [2003]
,
Assem and Happel [1981]
). However, in this case and if the global dimension of
A
is finite,
A
has non-negative Euler form of corank at most one and Dynkin type
\mathbb{A}
or
\mathbb{D}
by Bobiński-Geiss-Skowroński
Bobiński et al. [2004]
(see Subsections
2.1
and
4.1
for detailed definitions).

The following first main result of the paper
establishes a relationship between the marked ribbon graph and the Euler form of a gentle algebra. It generalizes some aspects of the above results
Assem and Skowroński [1987]
,
Bobiński et al. [2004]
(cf. also
Assem and Happel [1981]
) to all gentle algebras by providing the non-negativity, Dynkin type and corank of the Euler form and a related simple derived invariant.
From now on
k
denotes an arbitrary fixed field (not necessarily algebraically closed). We deal with gentle bound quivers of arbitrary global dimension.

Theorem A

(Theorem
4.8
)
.

Let
(Q,I)
be a gentle bound quiver, and
(G,\leq)
the associated marked ribbon graph. Then the Cartan matrix
C_{A}
of the gentle algebra
A:=kQ/I
and the incidence matrix
\mathrm{Inc}(G)
of
G
are related by the identity

C_{A}+C_{A}^{\rm{tr}}=\mathrm{Inc}(G)\mathrm{Inc}(G)^{\rm{tr}}.

In particular, the Euler form
q_{A}:K_{0}({\rm per}\,A)\to\mathbb{Z}
of
A
is non-negative. Moreover:

(a)

(Q,I)
satisfies the
multi-clock condition

(
that is, every closed walk in
Q
contains even number of relations of length two from
I
, see Definition
4.6
) if and only if the graph
G
is bipartite.

(b)

The corank of the Euler form is given by the formula

\mathrm{crk}(q_{A})=|Q_{1}|-|Q_{0}|+{\nabla}_{A},

where
{\nabla}_{A}\in\{0,1\}
and
{\nabla}_{A}=1
if and only if
(Q,I)
satisfies the multi-clock condition. In particular,
{\nabla}_{A}
is a derived equivalence invariant.

(c)

With the exception of certain degenerate cases, the Dynkin type
\mathrm{Dyn}(q_{A})
of
q_{A}
is
\mathbb{A}
,
\mathbb{D}
or
\mathbb{C}
, and
\mathrm{Dyn}(q_{A})=\mathbb{A}
if and only if
(Q,I)
satisfies the multi-clock condition.

We refer to the full version (Theorem
4.8
) of the theorem for more precise statements on the Dynkin type, as well as some additional claims, among others, for the distinguished case of finite global dimension and a certain graph “Koszul dual” to
(G,\leq)
. The proof is given in Subsection
4.4
.
We present in Subsection
4.5
several applications of Theorem A. Among other things, we provide there a collection of graph theoretic invariants for gentle algebras in the spirit of the results of Antipov-Zvonareva
Antipov and Zvonareva [2022]
and Opper-Zvonareva
Opper and Zvonareva [2022]
for Brauer graph algebras (see Corollary
4.34
); a characterization of the properties of the Euler form for derived discrete algebras (see Corollaries
4.36
and
4.37
) which recover and extend some of the results of Bobiński-Geiss-Skowroński
Bobiński et al. [2004]
; and we show that the Euler form of a Brauer graph algebra is non-negative, and we interrelate its definiteness with the representation type (see Theorem
4.39
, Corollary
4.41
and Remark
4.43
).

The following second main result of the paper may be viewed as an analogue of classical results for algebras of finite global dimension providing a classification of indecomposable modules in terms of positive roots of the Euler (or Tits) form, cf.
Gabriel [1972]
,
Bongartz [1983]
,
Dlab and Ringel [1976]
,
Kac [1980]
.

Theorem B

(Theorem
5.1
)
.

Let
(Q,I)
be a gentle bound quiver and
A=kQ/I
. The following inclusions hold:

\mathcal{R}_{q}(0)\cup\mathcal{R}_{q}(1)\ \subseteq\ \mathrm{P}(A)\ \subseteq\ \mathcal{R}_{q}(0)\cup\mathcal{R}_{q}(1)\cup\mathcal{R}_{q}(2),

where
\mathrm{P}=\mathrm{P}(A):=[{\rm ind}({\rm per}\,A)]\subseteq K_{0}({\rm per}\,A)
denotes the subset of all the classes of indecomposable perfect complexes in the Grothendieck group
K_{0}({\rm per}\,A)
of the perfect category,
q=q_{A}:K_{0}({\rm per}\,A)\to\mathbb{Z}
is the Euler form of
A
and
\mathcal{R}_{q}(d)=q^{-1}(d)
is the set of all
d
-roots of
q
for
d\in\mathbb{Z}
. Moreover,

\mathcal{R}_{q}(0)\cup\mathcal{R}_{q}(1)=\mathrm{P}
if and only if
(Q,I)
satisfies the multi-clock condition.

We refer to the full version (Theorem
5.1
) of the theorem for more precise statements, including a complete characterization of indecomposable perfect complexes whose classes in the Grothendieck group are 0-, 1-, or 2-roots, respectively, by means of walks in the associated marked ribbon graph, see also Corollary
5.14
. The proof of the theorem and some applications are given in Subsections
5.2
and
5.3
, respectively. We show that, analogously as in the classical situation of representations of quivers
Gabriel [1972]
,
Kac [1980]
, in case the Euler form
q_{A}
is positive, the set
\mathrm{P}(A)
is finite and it carries an additional structure of a root system in the sense of Bourbaki
Bourbaki [1968]
, see Theorem
5.16
.

The following third main result provides a complete description of the Coxeter polynomial
\psi_{A}(z)\in\mathbb{Z}[z]
of a gentle algebra
A
in terms of Avella-Alaminos Geiss invariant of
A
(shortly, AAG invariant; see
Avella-Alaminos and Geiss [2008]
and
3.4
). Recall that
\psi_{A}(z)
is the characteristic polynomial of the automorphism of the Grothendieck group
K_{0}({\rm per}\,A)
induced by the Auslander-Reiten translation on the perfect category (see
6.3
for the details). The Coxeter polynomial is a widely studied (mainly for algebras of finite global dimension) derived invariant which encodes certain interesting data on the module categories and derived categories, see for instance
Lenzing and de la Peña [2008]
,
Happel [1997]
,
Ringel [1994]
,
de la Peña [2015]
,
Zhang [1989]
,
Simson [2022]
.

Theorem C

(Theorem
6.17
)
.

Let
(Q,I)
be a gentle bound quiver. The Coxeter polynomial
\psi_{A}(z)
of the gentle algebra
A=kQ/I
with AAG invariant
\varphi_{A}
is given by

\psi_{A}(z)=(z-1)^{|Q_{1}|-|Q_{0}|}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}n>0\\
m\geq 0\end{subarray}}(z^{n}-(-1)^{n+m})^{\varphi_{A}(n,m)}.

(1.1)

Observe that the theorem shows in particular that the Coxeter polynomial of a gentle algebra is cyclotomic (that is, it is a product of cyclotomic polynomials) and the formula (
1.1
) provides its cyclotomic decomposition as those studied in
de la Peña [2014]
,
de la Peña [2015]
,
Mróz and de la Peña [2016]
. The proof of Theorem C is provided in Subsection
6.3
. It exploits, among others, the interplay between the Cartan matrix and the marked ribbon graph from Theorem A and the compact formula for the Auslander-Reiten translation given in Proposition
6.4
.

To sum up this introductory part, one of the basic ideas of the paper is to replace string combinatorics (or a geometry of curves in the geometric model) with the analysis of walks in marked ribbon graphs, equipped with methods related to integral quadratic forms. This approach provides simple tools to perform computations in the derived category of gentle algebras and it allows us to prove new general results for this class. It seems to us that these methods can be extended to related classes like skew-gentle algebras and (skew-)Brauer graph algebras. In particular, we believe that this may lead to a construction of further combinatorial derived invariants, cf.
Avella-Alaminos and Geiss [2008]
,
Amiot et al. [2023]
,
Opper and Zvonareva [2022]
.

The authors thank Grzegorz Bobiński and Grzegorz Zwara for inspiring discussions, especially around Theorem
4.39
on Brauer graph algebras (cf. Remark
4.43
).

2  Preliminary notions

Our base commutative ring
R
is either the integer numbers
R=\mathbb{Z}
or an arbitrary field
R=k
, and
R
-modules (abelian groups or vector spaces) are supposed to have finite bases, that is, they are free and finitely generated. The set of
n\times m
matrices with coefficients in
R
is denoted by
\mathbb{M}_{n,m}(R)
; more generally, given two finite sets
X
and
Y
, we consider the set of
X\times Y
-matrices
\mathbb{M}_{X,Y}(R):=\{f:X\times Y\to R:f\ \text{a function}\}
and we define addition and multiplication of such matrices in a natural way. Any morphism of
R
-modules with fixed bases is identified with its corresponding matrix. Given a natural number
n\geq 1
(resp. a finite set
X
), by
\{\mathbf{e}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
(resp.
\{\mathbf{e}_{i}\}_{i\in X}
) we denote the canonical basis of the
R
-module
R^{n}
(resp.
R^{X}
), that is, the coefficients of
\mathbf{e}_{i}
are given by
(\mathbf{e}_{i})_{j}=\delta_{i,j}
for each
i,j
. We often identify
R^{X}
with
R^{|X|}
under an arbitrarily fixed ordering in
X
and we view the elements of
R^{|X|}
as column vectors. Similarly we identify
\mathbb{M}_{X,Y}(R)
with
\mathbb{M}_{|X|,|Y|}(R)
. Given a matrix
M\in\mathbb{M}_{n,m}(\mathbb{Z})
, the rank of
M
is denoted by
\mathrm{rk}(M)
. By
\mathrm{crk}(M):=m-\mathrm{rk}(M)
we denote the (right)
corank
of
M
. Clearly,
\mathrm{crk}(M)
is the rank of the (right) null space
\ker(M)
of
M
.

2.1. Integral quadratic forms

Let
\langle-,-\rangle:K^{2}\to\mathbb{Z}
be an integral bilinear form where
K
is a free finitely generated abelian group. If
\beta:=\{\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{n}\}
is a
\mathbb{Z}
-linear basis of
K
, then the
Gram matrix
of
\langle-,-\rangle
with respect to
\beta
is the matrix
M\in\mathbb{M}_{n,n}(\mathbb{Z})
given by

M=M_{\langle-,-\rangle,\beta}=[\langle\beta_{i},\beta_{j}\rangle]_{i,j=1}^{n}.

(2.1)

The
rank
,
corank
and
determinant
of
\langle-,-\rangle
are those of
M
, which do not depend on the choice of basis of
K
. Two integral bilinear forms
\langle-,-\rangle:K^{2}\to\mathbb{Z}
and
\langle-,-\rangle^{\prime}:(K^{\prime})^{2}\to\mathbb{Z}
are called
equivalent
(or isometric), if there is an isomorphism of groups
f:K\to K^{\prime}
such that
\langle x,y\rangle=\langle f(x),f(y)\rangle^{\prime}
for all
x,y
in
K
. Note that rank, corank and determinant are invariant under equivalence of integral bilinear forms.

Definition 2.2
.

Let
K
be a free finitely generated abelian group.

(a)

An
integral quadratic form
on
K
is a function
q:K\to\mathbb{Z}
satisfying
q(x+x)=4q(x)
for all
x
in
K
, and such that the mapping
q(x,y):=q(x+y)-q(x)-q(y)
is bilinear. The (symmetric) integral bilinear form
q(-,-):K^{2}\to\mathbb{Z}
is called
polarization
of
q
. Note that
q(x)=\frac{1}{2}q(x,x)
by the first condition, and by the latter we have
q(tx)=t^{2}q(x)
for all
t\in\mathbb{Z}
and
x\in K
. The
rank

\mathrm{rk}(q)
,
corank

\mathrm{crk}(q)
and
determinant

\det(q)
of
q
are those of its polarization
q(-,-)
, and the
radical
of
q
is the set
\mathrm{rad}(q):=\{x\in K\mid q(-,x)\equiv 0\}
.

(b)

Given two integral quadratic forms
q:K\to\mathbb{Z}
and
q^{\prime}:K^{\prime}\to\mathbb{Z}
, by the
direct sum
of
q
and
q^{\prime}
we mean the quadratic form
q\oplus q^{\prime}:K\oplus K^{\prime}\to\mathbb{Z}
defined by
(q\oplus q^{\prime})(x,x^{\prime}):=q(x)+q^{\prime}(x^{\prime})
for
x\in K
and
x^{\prime}\in K^{\prime}
.

(c)

Two integral quadratic forms
q:K\to\mathbb{Z}
and
q^{\prime}:K^{\prime}\to\mathbb{Z}
are
equivalent
if so are their polarizations, that is, if there is an isomorphism of groups
f:K\to K^{\prime}
such that
q=q^{\prime}\circ f
. In this case we write
q\sim q^{\prime}
.

(d)

An integral quadratic form
q
is
non-negative
(respectively,
positive
) if
q(x)\geq 0
for all
x\in K
(respectively, if
q(x)>0
for all
0\neq x\in K
). The form
q
is
irreducible
if whenever
q=aq^{\prime}
for an integral quadratic form
q^{\prime}
and an integer
a\in\mathbb{Z}
then
a=\pm 1
.

(e)

For
d\in\mathbb{Z}
, the set
\mathcal{R}_{q}(d):=\{x\in K\mid q(x)=d\}
is called set of
d
-roots
of an integral quadratic form
q:K\to\mathbb{Z}
.

It can be easily verified that the radical of
q:K\to\mathbb{Z}
is the (right or left) null space of the (symmetric) Gram matrix of
q(-,-)
with respect to any basis of
K
. Therefore, the corank of
q
is the rank of its radical. It can also be shown that if
q
is non-negative then
\mathrm{rad}(q)=\mathcal{R}_{q}(0)
, and
q
is positive if and only if
\mathrm{crk}(q)=0
(equivalently,
\det(q)\neq 0
), cf.
[
Simson, 2011
, Proposition 2.8]
.
Furthermore, given an integral bilinear form
\langle-,-\rangle:K^{2}\to\mathbb{Z}
, the mapping
q:K\to\mathbb{Z}
defined by
q(x):=\langle x,x\rangle
is an integral quadratic form with
q(x,y)=\langle x,y\rangle+\langle y,x\rangle
for all
x,y\in K
.

Note that, besides the numerical invariants rank, corank and determinant, the non-negativity (positivity) property of an integral quadratic form is preserved under equivalence (in particular, under change of basis). Below we list some notions of interest of an integral quadratic form
q:K\to\mathbb{Z}
that depend on a given fixed basis
\beta:=\{\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{n}\}
of the abelian group
K
. We set
q_{ij}=q_{ij}^{\beta}:=q(\beta_{i},\beta_{j})
and
q_{i}=q_{i}^{\beta}:=q(\beta_{i})=\frac{1}{2}q_{ii}
for all
1\leq i,j\leq n
. Observe that then

q\left(\sum_{i}x_{i}\beta_{i}\right)=\frac{1}{2}x^{\rm{tr}}Mx=\sum_{i}q_{i}x_{i}^{2}+\sum_{i<j}q_{ij}x_{i}x_{j}

(2.3)

for any
x=[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]^{\rm{tr}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
, where
M=M_{q(-,-),\beta}=[q_{ij}]_{i,j=1}^{n}
.

•

The form
q
is
connected
if for any
i\neq j
in
\{1,\ldots,n\}
there is a sequence
(i_{0},\ldots,i_{r})
with
r>0
,
i_{0}=i
,
i_{r}=j
and such that
q_{{i_{t-1}}{i_{t}}}\neq 0
for
t=1,\ldots,r
.

•

The form
q
is
unitary
or a
unit form
(respectively,
semi-unitary
) if
q_{i}=1
(resp.
q_{i}\in\{0,1\}
) for all
i
, cf.
Barot and de la Peña [1999]
.

•

The form
q
is
Cox-regular
(resp.
semi-Cox-regular
) if
q_{i}>0
(resp.
q_{i}\geq 0
) for each
1\leq i\leq n
, and
\frac{q_{ij}}{q_{i}}\in\mathbb{Z}
, if
q_{i}>0
, and
\frac{q_{ij}}{q_{j}}\in\mathbb{Z}
, if
q_{j}>0
, for all
i\neq j
. Recall that
q
is Cox-regular if and only if the Gram matrix
M_{q(-,-),\beta}=[q_{ij}]_{i,j=1}^{n}
is a (symmetrized) quasi-Cartan matrix
Barot et al. [2006a]
, cf.
Kasjan and Simson [2015]
,
Roiter [1978]
and
[
Makuracki and Mróz, 2019
, Proposition 2.7]
.

Observe that (semi-)unitary forms are (semi-)Cox-regular, and that the connectedness between non-negative unitary forms is preserved under equivalence, cf.
[
Jiménez González, 2021
, Lemma 2.1(e)]
. However, this is not the case among Cox-regular quadratic forms. Moreover, equivalences do not preserve neither unitary nor Cox-regular forms, cf.
Jiménez González and Mróz [2026]
,
Mróz and Zając [2022]
.
For certain reasons (related mainly to Lie theory) one usually restricts equivalences to certain classes of isomorphisms. We recall the following classification results for certain classes of Cox-regular forms.
Let
q:K\to\mathbb{Z}
be a non-negative connected Cox-regular form, considered in a fixed basis
\beta
of
K
, and let
r:=\mathrm{rk}(q)\geq 1
and
c:=\mathrm{crk}(q)\geq 0
be the rank and corank of
q
, respectively.

(D1)

If
q
is a unit form then by
Barot and de la Peña [1999]
there exists a unique (simply-laced) Dynkin graph
D_{r}\in\{\mathbb{A}_{n_{a}},\mathbb{D}_{n_{d}},\mathbb{E}_{6},\mathbb{E}_{7},\mathbb{E}_{8}\}_{n_{a}\geq 1,n_{d}\geq 4}
with
r
vertices such that
q\sim q_{D_{r}}\oplus\zeta^{c}
, where
q_{D_{r}}:\mathbb{Z}^{r}\to\mathbb{Z}
is the unit form associated to
D_{r}
and
\zeta^{c}:\mathbb{Z}^{c}\to\mathbb{Z}
denotes the zero form, see
Barot and de la Peña [1999]
for details, cf.
Simson [2016]
,
Simson and Zając [2017]
and
[
Jiménez González and Mróz, 2026
, Theorem 4.3]
. The Dynkin graph
D_{r}
is called the
Dynkin type
of
q
and it is denoted by
\mathrm{Dyn}(q):=D_{r}
.

(D2)

If
q
is irreducible, non-unitary, and the coefficients
q_{ij}=q_{ij}^{\beta}
and
q_{i}=q_{i}^{\beta}
of
q
in the basis
\beta
satisfy:
1\leq q_{i}\leq 2
and
\frac{q_{ij}}{q_{i}q_{j}}\in\mathbb{Z}
for all
i,j
, then

q
is said to have
Dynkin type

\mathbb{C}_{r}
, written
\mathrm{Dyn}(q)=\mathbb{C}_{r}
(note that
r\geq 2
in this case). It is shown in
[
Jiménez González and Mróz, 2026
, Theorem 5.19]
that in this case the equivalence
q\sim q_{\mathbb{D}_{r}}\oplus\zeta^{c}
holds, provided
r\geq 4
. We stress that it should not be confused with Dynkin type
\mathbb{D}
since simply-laced Dynkin types are defined only for unit forms. In particular, the class of Cox-regular forms of Dynkin type
\mathbb{C}
is not closed under equivalences. However, it is closed under so-called Gabrielov equivalence
\sim_{G}
(of interest in Lie theory and singularity theory) and a complete
\sim_{G}
-classification of forms of type
\mathbb{C}
is provided in
[
Jiménez González and Mróz, 2026
, Section 5]
. We refer to
Kasjan and Simson [2015]
,
Mróz and Zając [2022]
,
Barot et al. [2006b]
,
Pérez and Rivera [2019]
,
Simson [2022]
,
Mróz and Zając [2025]
for more details on Cox-regular forms, their certain other classifications and a broader context.

2.2. Bidirected graphs

For convenience we use the following notion of graph by means of “glued” half-edges, cf.
Opper et al. [2018]
.

Definition 2.4
.

A
graph

G=(V,H,\mathrm{z},\iota)
consists of sets
V
and
H
together with a function
\mathrm{z}:H\to V
and an involution
\iota:H\to H
without fixed points. The elements of
V
and
H
are called
vertices
and
half-edges
of
G
, respectively, and the vertex
\mathrm{z}(h)
is said to be
incident
to the half-edge
h
. The set of
edges

E(G)
of
G
is the set of
\iota
-orbits on
H
, that is,
E(G):=H/\iota=\{\{h,\iota h\}\}_{h\in H}
. An edge
\{h,\iota h\}
with
\mathrm{z}(h)=\mathrm{z}(\iota h)
is called a
loop
. We also write
V(G):=V
and
H(G):=H
.

Throughout the paper we will assume that all graphs are finite (that is, so are the sets
V
and
H
) and that they have no isolated vertices (that is,
\mathbf{Im}(\mathrm{z})=V
).
Some of the usual concepts related to graphs can be expressed according to this definition as follows.
Two graphs
G=(V,H,\mathrm{z},\iota)
and
G^{\prime}=(V^{\prime},H^{\prime},\mathrm{z}^{\prime},\iota^{\prime})
are
isomorphic
if there are bijections
f_{V}:V\to V^{\prime}
and
f_{H}:H\to H^{\prime}
such that
\mathrm{z}^{\prime}\circ f_{H}=f_{V}\circ\mathrm{z}
and
\iota^{\prime}\circ f_{H}=f_{H}\circ\iota
. A
subgraph
of a graph
G=(V,H,\mathrm{z},\iota)
consists of subsets
V^{\prime}\subseteq V
and
H^{\prime}\subseteq H
such that
\mathrm{z}
and
\iota
are well defined on their restrictions to
H^{\prime}
and
V^{\prime}
, denoted by
\mathrm{z}^{\prime}:H^{\prime}\to V^{\prime}
and
\iota^{\prime}:H^{\prime}\to H^{\prime}
. In that case
G^{\prime}:=(V^{\prime},H^{\prime},\mathrm{z}^{\prime},\iota^{\prime})
is itself a graph. A non-empty subgraph
G^{\prime}
of
G
is called
proper
if
V^{\prime}\neq V
,
full
if any
h\in H
with
\mathrm{z}(h)
and
\mathrm{z}(\iota h)
in
V^{\prime}
belongs to
H^{\prime}
, and a
component
of
G
if any
h\in H
with
\mathrm{z}(h)
or
\mathrm{z}(\iota h)
in
V^{\prime}
belongs to
H^{\prime}
. A graph is called
connected
if it contains no proper component. Observe that
G
is disconnected if and only if there is a (non-trivial) partition
H=H^{1}\cup H^{2}
with
\iota H^{\epsilon}=H^{\epsilon}=\mathrm{z}^{-1}(\mathrm{z}H^{\epsilon})
for
\epsilon=1,2
. If
G
is a connected graph with

t={\rm cyc}(G):=|E(G)|-|V(G)|+1

(2.5)

then
t\geq 0
and we say that
G
is a
t
-
cycle
graph. Observe that
G
is a
0
-cycle graph if and only if
G
is a tree.

Consider the set of
ordered edges

\vec{E}(G)
of
G
, that is, the subset of
H\times H
given by

\vec{E}(G):=\{(h^{\prime},h^{\prime\prime})\in H\times H\mid h^{\prime\prime}=\iota h^{\prime}\}.

For an ordered edge
i=(h^{\prime},h^{\prime\prime})\in\vec{E}(G)
denote by
{i}^{-1}:=(h^{\prime\prime},h^{\prime})\in\vec{E}(G)
the
inverse
of
i
and by
\overline{i}=\{h^{\prime},h^{\prime\prime}\}\in E(G)
the corresponding (unordered) edge of
G
. We use the notation
\mathbf{t}^{h}(i):=h^{\prime}\in H
and
\mathbf{t}(i):=\mathrm{z}(h^{\prime})\in V
for the (half-edge and vertex)
target
of
i
, and
\mathbf{s}^{h}(i):=h^{\prime\prime}\in H
and
\mathbf{s}(i):=\mathrm{z}(h^{\prime\prime})\in V
for the corresponding
source
of
i
. Thus we may visualize the ordered edge
i=(h^{\prime},h^{\prime\prime})
as follows:

\textstyle{{\bullet}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbf{t}^{h}(i)=h^{\prime}}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\bullet}}
\scriptstyle{\mathbf{s}^{h}(i)=h^{\prime\prime}}

\scriptstyle\mathbf{t}(i)=\mathrm{z}(h^{\prime})

\scriptstyle\mathbf{s}(i)=\mathrm{z}(h^{\prime\prime})

(2.6)

By
walk
of
G
we mean an alternating sequence

\omega=(v_{0},i_{1},v_{1},\ldots,v_{\ell-1},i_{\ell},v_{\ell})

of vertices
v_{t}
and ordered edges
i_{t}
of
G
such that
\mathbf{t}(i_{t})=v_{t-1}
and
\mathbf{s}(i_{t})=v_{t}
for
t=1,\ldots,\ell\geq 0
(observe that we write walks “from right to left”). The integer
\ell(\omega):=\ell\geq 0
is called
length
of
\omega
, and we use the compact notation
\omega=i_{1}\cdots i_{\ell}
or
\omega={}^{v_{0}}i_{1}\cdots i_{\ell}^{v_{\ell}}
if
\ell(\omega)>0
. A walk
\omega
of length zero starting at vertex
v
is called
trivial
and is denoted by
\mathbf{1}_{v}:=(v)
. Extend the target (resp. source) functions to non-trivial walks by taking
\mathbf{t}^{h}(\omega):=\mathbf{t}^{h}(i_{1})
and
\mathbf{t}(\omega):=\mathbf{t}(i_{1})
(resp.
\mathbf{s}^{h}(\omega):=\mathbf{s}^{h}(i_{\ell})
and
\mathbf{s}(\omega):=\mathbf{s}(i_{\ell})
), and take
\mathbf{t}(\mathbf{1}_{v})=v=\mathbf{s}(\mathbf{1}_{v})
. We say that a non-trivial walk
\omega
as above is:

•

closed
if
\mathbf{s}(\omega)=\mathbf{t}(\omega)
,

•

open
if
\mathbf{s}(\omega)\neq\mathbf{t}(\omega)
,

•

reduced
if
i_{t}\neq{i}^{-1}_{t+1}
for
t=1,\ldots,\ell-1
,

•

self-crossing
at
v_{t}
if
v_{t}=v_{s}
for some
0\leq t,s\leq\ell
,
s\neq t
,

•

and
\omega
has
no repeated edges
, if
\overline{i}_{t}\neq\overline{i}_{s}
for all
s\neq t
.

The
inverse
of the walk
\omega
is given by
{\omega}^{-1}:={i}^{-1}_{\ell}\cdots{i}^{-1}_{1}
if
\ell>1
and
{\mathbf{1}}^{-1}_{v}:=\mathbf{1}_{v}
. A pair of walks
\omega
and
\omega^{\prime}
is said to be
concatenable
if
\mathbf{s}(\omega)=\mathbf{t}(\omega^{\prime})
, and their concatenation is denoted by
\omega\omega^{\prime}
which is a walk of length
\ell(\omega)+\ell(\omega^{\prime})
. If
\omega
is a closed walk we denote by
\omega^{r}
the
r
-fold concatenation of
\omega
with itself, and say that
\omega
is
primitive
if it is not the
r
-fold concatenation of a walk of strictly smaller length. For an arbitrary walk
\omega
and
0\leq t\leq\ell(\omega)
we will often use the decomposition

\omega=\omega_{[t]}\,{}_{[t]}\omega\qquad\qquad\text{with $\ell(\omega_{[t]})=t$ and $\ell(_{[t]}\omega)=\ell(\omega)-t$.}

Definition 2.7
.

By
bidirected graph

(G,\sigma)
we mean a graph
G
together with a function
\sigma:H\to\{\pm 1\}
called a
bidirection
of
G
. If such function satisfies
\sigma(\iota h)=-\sigma(h)
for all
h\in H
we say that it is a
direction
of
G
, and the pair
(G,\sigma)
is called a
directed graph
.
By the
(edge-vertex) incidence matrix
of
(G,\sigma)
we mean the matrix
\mathrm{Inc}(G,\sigma)\in\mathbb{M}_{E(G),V(G)}(\mathbb{Z})
whose transposed rows are defined by

\mathrm{Inc}(G,\sigma)^{\rm{tr}}\mathbf{e}_{i}:=\sigma(h)\mathbf{e}_{\mathrm{z}(h)}+\sigma(h^{\prime})\mathbf{e}_{\mathrm{z}(h^{\prime})},\;\text{ for $i=\{h,h^{\prime}\}\in E(G)$}.

(2.8)

Viewing a graph
G
as a bidirected graph
(G,\sigma^{+})
with constant
\sigma^{+}\equiv+1
, we define its
incidence matrix
as
\mathrm{Inc}(G):=\mathrm{Inc}(G,\sigma^{+})
.

A bidirection
\sigma
of
G
induces a function
\sigma:\vec{E}(G)\to\{\pm 1\}
by taking

\sigma(i):=(-1)\sigma(\mathbf{t}^{h}(i))\sigma(\mathbf{s}^{h}(i)).

(2.9)

A positive ordered edge
i
(that is, one with
\sigma(i)=+1
) is called a (direct)
arrow
if
\sigma(\mathbf{s}^{h}(i))=1
, and an
inverse arrow
if
\sigma(\mathbf{s}^{h}(i))=-1
. A negative ordered edge
i
is called a
bidirected arrow
; in this case
\sigma(\mathbf{s}^{h}(i))=\sigma(\mathbf{t}^{h}(i))=:\epsilon
, and if
\epsilon=+1
(resp.
\epsilon=-1
) then we call
i
a
two-tail arrow
(resp.
two-head arrow
). The sign of a walk
\omega
is given by
\sigma(\omega):=\prod_{t=1}^{\ell(\omega)}\sigma(i_{t})
, setting
\sigma(\omega):=+1
if
\omega
is trivial. A bidirected graph
(G,\sigma)
is
balanced
if all closed walks in
G
are positive, that is, each of them contains an even number of negative (bidirected) arrows. Otherwise, that is, when
G
contains a negative closed walk, we say
(G,\sigma)
is
unbalanced
. Sometimes, whenever it can be done without ambiguity, we also use letters
i
,
j
etc. to denote unordered edges, and we apply analogous notation and terminology for unordered edges, that is, the sign of an unordered edge
i=\{h,h^{\prime}\}\in E(G)
is
\sigma(i):=-\sigma(h)\sigma(h^{\prime})
and we say that
i
is
bidirected
if
\sigma(i)=-1
, analogously for
two-tail
(resp.
two-head
)
edges
.

In representation theory, directed graphs are called
quivers
and are denoted by
Q=(Q_{0},Q_{1},\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t})
where
Q_{0}
is the set of vertices and
Q_{1}
is the set of (direct) arrows of
Q
, and
\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t}:Q_{1}\to Q_{0}
are the source and target functions as above (cf.
Assem et al. [2006]
). In the sequel we often use (indexed) Greek letters to denote arrows in quivers and natural numbers (or Latin letters
i
,
j
) to denote their vertices. Note that the ordered edges of
Q
are given by
\vec{E}(Q)=Q_{1}\cup{Q}^{-1}_{1}
. We say that a walk
\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{\ell}
of
Q
is a
path
if
\alpha_{t}\in Q_{1}
for
t=1,\ldots,\ell\geq 0
.

3  Combinatorial aspects of gentle algebras

By
k
-algebra we mean an associative algebra with unity over an arbitrary field
k
. Unless otherwise stated, all
k
-algebras are finite dimensional. For a
k
-algebra
A
consider the category of finite dimensional left
A
-modules
\mathrm{mod}\,A
, and the full subcategory
{\rm proj}\,A
of
\mathrm{mod}\,A
determined by projective
A
-modules. We fix a complete set
\{S_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
of representatives of isomorphism classes of simple
A
-modules, and take for
i=1,\ldots,n
a projective cover
P_{i}
and an injective envelope
E_{i}
of
S_{i}
, respectively. By
global dimension

{\rm gl.dim}(A)\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
of
A
we mean as usual the maximum of lengths of (minimal) projective resolutions of simples
\{S_{i}\}
, cf.
Assem et al. [2006]
.

We denote by
{\mathcal{D}}^{b}(A):={\mathcal{D}}^{b}(\mathrm{mod}\,A)
the bounded derived category of (bounded complexes of) finite dimensional
A
-modules, see
Happel [1988]
. Recall that
{\mathcal{D}}^{b}(A)
has a structure of triangulated category with translation functor
[1]:{\mathcal{D}}^{b}(A)\to{\mathcal{D}}^{b}(A)
acting as the usual shift of complexes (to the left). The full triangulated subcategory
{\rm per}\,A
of
{\mathcal{D}}^{b}(A)
determined by complexes quasi-isomorphic to bounded complexes whose terms are in
{\rm proj}\,A
is called the
perfect category
of
A
, and its objects are called
perfect complexes
. It is well known that
{\rm per}\,A
(resp.
{\mathcal{D}}^{b}(A)
) is triangle equivalent with the homotopy category
\mathcal{K}^{b}({\rm proj}\,A)
(resp.
\mathcal{K}^{-,b}({\rm proj}\,A)
) of bounded complexes with terms in
{\rm proj}\,A
(resp. right bounded complexes with terms in
{\rm proj}\,A
and whose cohomology is bounded).
Two algebras
A
and
A^{\prime}
are
derived equivalent
(written
A\cong_{\rm der}A^{\prime}
) if their derived categories
{\mathcal{D}}^{b}(A)
and
{\mathcal{D}}^{b}(A^{\prime})
are triangle equivalent. Recall that
{\rm per}\,A={\mathcal{D}}^{b}(A)
precisely when
A
has finite global dimension, and that in general
A\cong_{\rm der}A^{\prime}
if and only if the categories
{\rm per}\,A
and
{\rm per}\,A^{\prime}
are triangle equivalent, see
Rickard [1989]
.

The
path algebra

kQ
of a quiver
Q
is the possibly infinite dimensional
k
-algebra whose underlying vector space has as basis the paths of
Q
, and its product is determined on this basis by the concatenation of concatenable paths, and zero otherwise, see
Assem et al. [2006]
. Denote by
R_{Q}
the two-sided ideal of
kQ
generated by the arrows of
Q
. A pair
(Q,I)
is a
bound quiver
if
I
is a two-sided ideal of
kQ
with
I\subseteq R_{Q}^{2}
. Moreover, if
R^{m}_{Q}\subseteq I
for some
m\geq 2
, then
I
is an
admissible ideal
and
(Q,I)
is an
admissible bound quiver
. The quotient
k(Q,I):=kQ/I
is a
bound quiver algebra
, which is finite dimensional if and only if
I
is an admissible ideal. It is well-known that if
k
is algebraically closed, then every (finite dimensional)
k
-algebra is Morita equivalent to an admissible bound quiver algebra (Gabriel
Gabriel [1972]
). A
relation
of the ideal
I
(or of the bound quiver
(Q,I)
) is an element of
I
given as a
k
-linear combination of parallel paths (that is, with common source and common target) of length at least two. Observe that every admissible ideal
I
is generated as bimodule by a finite set of relations. A relation of
I
is
monomial
if it is a single path, and
I
is a
monomial ideal
if it can be generated by finitely many monomial relations (in this case, the bound quiver
(Q,I)
and bound quiver algebra
k(Q,I)
are also called monomial). If
I
is a monomial ideal, then it has a unique minimal monomial generating set, see
Green [1999]
.

Given a bound quiver
(Q,I)
, a path of
Q
not belonging to the ideal
I
is called
permitted
, and it is a
maximal permitted path
if it is not properly contained in any permitted path (note that all paths of length at most
1
are permitted). Observe that a monomial bound quiver algebra
k(Q,I)
is isomorphic to the
k
-vector space generated by all permitted paths of
(Q,I)
.

If
A
is an admissible bound quiver algebra,
A=k(Q,I)
, we call
{\rm gl.dim}(Q,I):={\rm gl.dim}(A)
the global dimension of
(Q,I)
, and take
P_{i}:=A\mathbf{1}_{i}
,
S_{i}:=P_{i}/\mathrm{rad}(P_{i})
and
E_{i}:=D(\mathbf{1}_{i}A)
for each vertex
i\in Q_{0}
, where
D(-):=\mathrm{Hom}_{k}(-,k)
is the standard duality. We usually identify the set of vertices
Q_{0}
with the set
\{1,\ldots,|Q_{0}|\}
. In the paper, all
k
-algebras are bound quiver algebras.

3.1. Gentle bound quivers

In this subsection we collect definitions and mostly well-known properties of gentle bound quivers. For a quiver
Q
and a vertex
i
in
Q
we denote by
\deg^{+}(i)
(resp.
\deg^{-}(i)
) the number of arrows
\alpha\in Q_{1}
such that
\mathbf{s}(\alpha)=i
(resp.
\mathbf{t}(\alpha)=i
), and call
\deg(i):=\deg^{+}(i)+\deg^{-}(i)
the
degree
of
i
.

Definition 3.1
.

An admissible bound quiver
(Q,I)
is called a
gentle bound quiver
if the ideal
I
is generated by paths of length two, and the following conditions hold:

(a)

\deg^{+}(i)\leq 2
and
\deg^{-}(i)\leq 2
for any vertex
i
of
Q
;

(b)

for any arrow
\beta
of
Q
there is at most one arrow
\alpha
such that
\alpha\beta\notin I
, and at most one arrow
\gamma
such that
\beta\gamma\notin I
;

(c)

for any arrow
\beta
of
Q
there is at most one arrow
\alpha
such that
0\neq\alpha\beta\in I
, and at most one arrow
\gamma
such that
0\neq\beta\gamma\in I
.

Here we always assume that
Q
is connected and has at least one arrow. By
gentle algebra
we mean the bound quiver algebra
k(Q,I)=kQ/I
of a gentle bound quiver.

Alternatively, an admissible bound quiver
(Q,I)
with ideal
I
generated by paths of length two is gentle if there are functions
S,T:Q_{1}\to\{\pm 1\}
satisfying the following conditions for any pair of arrows
\alpha,\beta\in Q_{1}
:

(1)

if
\mathbf{s}(\alpha)=\mathbf{s}(\beta)
and
\alpha\neq\beta
, then
S(\alpha)\neq S(\beta)
;

(2)

if
\mathbf{t}(\alpha)=\mathbf{t}(\beta)
and
\alpha\neq\beta
, then
T(\alpha)\neq T(\beta)
;

(3)

if
\mathbf{t}(\alpha)=\mathbf{s}(\beta)
, then
\beta\alpha\in I
if and only if
T(\alpha)=S(\beta)
.

The functions
S,T
are called
string functions
for
(Q,I)
, cf.
Bobiński [2011]
,
Geiss and Reiten [2005]
. We extend them to the set of non-trivial walks of
Q
by taking
S(\alpha^{-1}):=T(\alpha)
,
T(\alpha^{-1}):=S(\alpha)
for
\alpha\in Q_{1}
, and

S(\omega):=S(\xi_{\ell})
,
T(\omega):=T(\xi_{1})
for a walk
\omega=\xi_{1}\cdots\xi_{\ell}
in
Q
with
\xi_{t}\in Q_{1}\cup{Q}^{-1}_{1}
for
t=1,\ldots,\ell\geq 1
.

Following
Avella-Alaminos and Geiss [2008]
, if
(Q,I)
is a gentle bound quiver, then a maximal permitted path is a
non-trivial permitted thread
of
(Q,I)
. Moreover, the path
\mathbf{1}_{i}
is a
trivial permitted thread
if either
\deg(i)=1
, or
\deg(i)=2
and there are arrows
\alpha,\beta
with
\mathbf{s}(\alpha)=\mathbf{t}(\beta)=i
such that
\alpha\beta\notin I
. For the sake of precision, we also consider
split permitted thread
which is a pair of paths
(\eta^{\prime\prime},\eta^{\prime})
such that
\eta^{\prime\prime}\eta^{\prime}
is a permitted thread, and the vertex
\mathbf{s}(\eta^{\prime\prime})=\mathbf{t}(\eta^{\prime})
is called the
center
of
(\eta^{\prime\prime},\eta^{\prime})
. The set of (split) permitted threads of
(Q,I)
is denoted by
\mathcal{P}_{Q,I}
(respectively, by
\mathcal{SP}_{Q,I}
). A path of
Q
is called
forbidden
if it contains no permitted subpath of length two, and it is called a
non-trivial forbidden thread
if it is not properly contained in any forbidden path. Moreover, the path
\mathbf{1}_{i}
is a
trivial forbidden thread
if either
\deg(i)=1
, or
\deg(i)=2
and there are arrows
\alpha,\beta
with
\mathbf{s}(\alpha)=\mathbf{t}(\beta)=i
such that
\alpha\beta\in I
. By
split forbidden thread
we mean a pair of paths
(\theta^{\prime\prime},\theta^{\prime})
such that
\theta^{\prime\prime}\theta^{\prime}
is a forbidden thread, and the vertex
\mathbf{s}(\theta^{\prime\prime})=\mathbf{t}(\theta^{\prime})
is called the
center
of
(\theta^{\prime\prime},\theta^{\prime})
. The set of (split) forbidden threads of
(Q,I)
is denoted by
\mathcal{F}_{Q,I}
(respectively, by
\mathcal{SF}_{Q,I}
). A closed forbidden path
\omega=\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{\ell}
without repeated arrows is called a
full cycle
if
\alpha_{\ell}\alpha_{1}\in I
, and a
almost-full cycle
at
\mathbf{s}(\omega)
if
\alpha_{\ell}\alpha_{1}\notin I
.

The following technical facts are known and follow easily from Definition
3.1
, see for instance
Bekkert and Merklen [2003]
and
Opper et al. [2018]
. We provide a sketch of proof for an illustration,
see also Example
3.4
.

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
(Q,I)
be a gentle bound quiver.

(a)

Every non-trivial permitted path of
(Q,I)
can be extended to a unique permitted thread.

(b)

Every vertex of
Q
is center of exactly two split permitted threads.

Proof.

If
\alpha_{1}
is an arrow of
Q
such that
\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}
and
\alpha_{1}\alpha^{\prime}_{2}
are permitted paths of
(Q,I)
, then
\mathbf{t}(\alpha_{2})=\mathbf{s}(\alpha_{1})=\mathbf{t}(\alpha^{\prime}_{2})
and
T(\alpha_{2})\neq S(\alpha_{1})\neq T(\alpha^{\prime}_{2})
, which implies that
\alpha_{2}=\alpha^{\prime}_{2}
. Similarly, if
\alpha_{0}\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha^{\prime}_{0}\alpha_{1}
are permitted paths of
(Q,I)
then
\alpha_{0}=\alpha^{\prime}_{0}
. This shows that
\alpha_{1}
is contained in at most one (non-trivial) permitted thread, cf.
[
Bekkert and Merklen, 2003
, Lemma 3]
. And at least one such permitted thread exists since
I
is admissible. By analogous arguments the claim holds for any permitted path of length greater than 1.

For (b) recall that it was observed in
[
Opper et al., 2018
, 1.3]
(cf.
[
Avella-Alaminos and Geiss, 2008
, 2.2]
) that every vertex of
Q
appears exactly two times along all permitted threads (this includes the situation when some permitted thread
\eta
passes 2 times through a given vertex
i
, that is,
\eta
self-crosses at
i
). In our terminology this means precisely that every vertex of
Q
is a center of exactly two split permitted threads. ∎

Remark 3.3
.

By Lemma
3.2
(a) it follows that every arrow
\alpha
of
Q
belongs to a unique permitted thread
\eta
and the position of
\alpha
in
\eta
is unique (since obviously permitted paths have no repeated arrows). Hence we will usually index non-trivial permitted threads with Greek letters
\eta^{\alpha},\eta^{\beta},\ldots
and their constituent arrows will be denoted by the corresponding indexed letters
\eta^{\alpha}=\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}\cdots\alpha_{\ell^{\alpha}}
and
\eta^{\beta}=\beta_{1}\beta_{2}\cdots\beta_{\ell^{\beta}}
. Observe that with this notation all monomial relations of length two in
(Q,I)
are of the form
\alpha_{r}\beta_{s}
or
\alpha_{r}\alpha_{s}
with
s\neq r+1
(to be precise, these relations form the unique minimal monomial generating set for
I
). We indicate these relations with dotted lines in diagrams.

Example 3.4
.

(Q,I)

The ideal
I
is generated by relations
\alpha_{1}\alpha_{3}
and
\alpha_{2}^{2}
. Observe that
\alpha_{2}
is a full cycle, and
(Q,I)
has infinite global dimension, cf. Lemma
3.5
.

\eta^{\alpha}=\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}\alpha_{3}&&\eta^{\alpha}=\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}&&\eta^{\alpha}=\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}\alpha_{3}\\
\eta^{\beta}=\beta_{1}\beta_{2}&&\eta^{\beta}=\beta_{1}\beta_{2}\beta_{3}&&\eta^{\beta}=\beta_{1}\beta_{2}\beta_{3}\\
\eta^{\gamma}=\gamma_{1}&&\eta^{\gamma}=\gamma_{1}&&\eta^{\gamma}=\mathbf{1}_{3}\\
\eta^{\delta}=\mathbf{1}_{5}&&\eta^{\delta}=\mathbf{1}_{4}&&\eta^{\delta}=\mathbf{1}_{4}\\
\end{array}

Forbidden threads have similar properties to permitted threads in case of finite global dimension.
To see this we recall the following known fact.

Lemma 3.5

(
[
Bobiński, 2017
, Proposition 2.1]
)
.

A gentle bound quiver
(Q,I)
has finite global dimension if and only if it contains no full cycle.

Lemma 3.6
.

Let
(Q,I)
be a gentle bound quiver of finite global dimension.

(a)

Every non-trivial forbidden path of
(Q,I)
can be extended to a unique forbidden thread.

(b)

Every vertex of
Q
is center of exactly two split forbidden threads.

(c)

For each vertex
i
of
Q
there exists at most one almost-full cycle at
i
in
(Q,I)
and it has length at least 2.

Proof.

Since
(Q,I)
has no full cycle by Lemma
3.5
, then every non-trivial forbidden path can be extended to a forbidden thread. The uniqueness of such thread follows the lines of the proof of Lemma
3.2
(a). The claim (b) similarly follows by arguments as in Lemma
3.2
(b). To see (c) assume that there are two different almost-full cycles
\omega=\alpha_{1}\ldots\alpha_{\ell}
and
\omega^{\prime}=\alpha^{\prime}_{1}\ldots\alpha^{\prime}_{\ell^{\prime}}
at
i\in Q_{0}
, for some arrows
\alpha_{t},\alpha_{t^{\prime}}\in Q_{1}
, for
1\leq t\leq\ell
,
1\leq t^{\prime}\leq\ell^{\prime}
. By definition
\omega
and
\omega^{\prime}
are closed forbidden paths without repeated arrows with
\mathbf{s}(\omega)=\mathbf{t}(\omega)=\mathbf{s}(\omega^{\prime})=\mathbf{t}(\omega^{\prime})=i
and
\alpha_{\ell}\alpha_{1},\alpha^{\prime}_{\ell^{\prime}}\alpha^{\prime}_{1}\notin I
. Gentleness of
(Q,I)
easily implies that
\{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{\ell}\}\cap\{\alpha^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\alpha^{\prime}_{\ell^{\prime}}\}=\emptyset
and
\alpha^{\prime}_{\ell^{\prime}}\alpha_{1},\alpha_{\ell}\alpha^{\prime}_{1}\in I
. This means that
\omega^{\prime}\omega
is a full cycle – contradiction with Lemma
3.5
. Finally, if
\omega=\alpha_{1}
is an almost-full cycle of length 1 then it means that
\alpha_{1}^{2}\notin I
, hence
\alpha^{n}_{1}\notin I
for each
n\geq 1
, which is impossible since
I
is admissible (
kQ/I
has finite dimension).
∎

3.2. Marked ribbon graphs

A graph
G=(V,H,\mathrm{z},\iota)
together with a permutation
\mathrm{p}
of the set of half-edges
H
whose orbits coincide with the inverse images
\mathrm{z}^{-1}(\alpha)
for any vertex
\alpha
of
G
is called a
ribbon graph
, cf.
[
Opper et al., 2018
, Definition 1.2]
. Ribbon graphs have a wide range of applications, especially in connection to orientable surfaces. They were used in the analysis of Brauer graph algebras
Marsh and Schroll [2014]
and in connection to trivial extension of gentle algebras
Schroll [2015]
, see Subsection
4.5
below. The latter connection makes use of a function
\mathrm{m}:V\to H
satisfying
\mathrm{z}(\mathrm{m}(\alpha))=\alpha
for each vertex
\alpha\in V
, called a
marking
of the ribbon graph
(G,\mathrm{p})
, cf.
[
Opper et al., 2018
, Definition 1.5]
(recall our assumption
V=\mathbf{Im}(\mathrm{z})
). Observe that the marking
\mathrm{m}
partitions the set of half-edges
H
of
G
into linearly ordered sets (a disjoint union of chains joint by the involution
\iota
), which is a formulation better adjusted to our purposes:

Definition 3.7
.

Let
G=(V,H,\mathrm{z},\iota)
be a graph. A pair
(G,\leq)
is called a
marked ribbon graph
if
\leq
is a partial order on the set of half-edges
H
of
G
such that
h\leq h^{\prime}
or
h^{\prime}\leq h
if and only if
\mathrm{z}(h)=\mathrm{z}(h^{\prime})
for
h,h^{\prime}\in H
. Here we further assume that
G
is a connected graph having a vertex of degree at least two.

Remark 3.8
.

If
(G,\mathrm{p})
is a connected ribbon graph with marking
\mathrm{m}
, then for a vertex
\alpha\in V(G)
we take

\mathrm{z}^{-1}(\alpha):=\{h^{\alpha}_{0},h^{\alpha}_{1},\ldots,h^{\alpha}_{\ell^{\alpha}-1},h^{\alpha}_{\ell^{\alpha}}\}\subseteq H(G),

where
h^{\alpha}_{0}
is the marked half-edge
\mathrm{m}(\alpha)
, and such that
\mathrm{p}(h^{\alpha}_{r})=h^{\alpha}_{r-1}
for
r=1,\ldots,\ell^{\alpha}
and
\mathrm{p}(h^{\alpha}_{0})=h^{\alpha}_{\ell^{\alpha}}
. For
h,h^{\prime}\in H(G)
, define
h\leq h^{\prime}
precisely when there is a vertex
\alpha
of
G
such that
h=h^{\alpha}_{r}
and
h^{\prime}=h^{\alpha}_{r^{\prime}}
with
0\leq r^{\prime}\leq r\leq\ell^{\alpha}
. Then
(G,\leq)
is a marked ribbon graph, and the set
\mathrm{m}(V(G))
consists of the maximal elements of
H(G)
with respect to
\leq
. Clearly, the permutation
\mathrm{p}
and the marking
\mathrm{m}
can be recovered from the partial order
\leq
.

In the following definition we construct the marked ribbon graph associated with a gentle bound quiver as in
Opper et al. [2018]
, in a slightly more precise form. We also provide a converse operation below (Definition
3.11
). These constructions establish a formal foundation for our graph theoretic model.

Definition 3.9
.

For a gentle bound quiver
(Q,I)
define a pair
\triangle(Q,I):=(G,\leq)
with
G
a graph and
\leq
a relation on the set of half-edges of
G
as follows. The graph
G=(V,H,\mathrm{z},\iota)
has as set of vertices the permitted threads of
(Q,I)
,
V:=\mathcal{P}_{Q,I}
, as set of half-edges the split permitted threads of
(Q,I)
,
H:=\mathcal{SP}_{Q,I}
, the function
\mathrm{z}
is given by
\mathrm{z}(\eta^{\prime\prime},\eta^{\prime}):=\eta^{\prime\prime}\eta^{\prime}
, and the involution
\iota(\eta^{\prime\prime},\eta^{\prime})
is the unique element in
\mathcal{SP}_{Q,I}-\{(\eta^{\prime\prime},\eta^{\prime})\}
having the same center as
(\eta^{\prime\prime},\eta^{\prime})
, cf. Lemma
3.2
(b). The relation
\leq
on
H
is given by
(\eta^{\prime\prime}_{0},\eta^{\prime}_{0})\leq(\eta^{\prime\prime},\eta^{\prime})
if and only if
\eta^{\prime\prime}_{0}\eta^{\prime}_{0}=\eta^{\prime\prime}\eta^{\prime}
and
\ell(\eta^{\prime}_{0})\leq\ell(\eta^{\prime})
.

We easily verify that given a (connected) gentle bound quiver
(Q,I)
the pair
\triangle(Q,I)
is a (connected) marked ribbon graph having a vertex of degree at least two.
Although the above definition is somehow cryptic, the construction is straightforward once we know the permitted threads of
(Q,I)
. Indeed, the vertices of
G
may be viewed as the indices
\alpha,\beta,\ldots
of the permitted threads, the edges of
G
are in bijection with the vertices of
Q
(by taking the center of the respective split permitted thread), and the linear orders
\leq
record the vertices of
Q
as found along each permitted thread of
(Q,I)
, compare with
[
Opper et al., 2018
, Definition 1.8]
. These vertices are written graphically (from right to left) as
\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}123}}}}}{{\alpha}}}
for a permitted a thread
{}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}}\alpha_{1}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}}\alpha_{2}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}}
. Note that
i
is a loop of
G
at vertex
\alpha
exactly when the permitted thread
\eta^{\alpha}
self-crosses at vertex
i
. Let us consider some examples illustrating these conventions.

Example 3.10
.

The following table exhibits three “small” gentle bound quivers together with the associated marked ribbon graphs.

†
†

NiceArray (nicematrix)

Definition 3.11
.

For a marked ribbon graph
(G,{\leq})
define a bound quiver
\triangledown(G,\leq):=(Q,I)
as follows. The quiver
Q
has as set of vertices the edges of
G
,
Q_{0}:=E(G)=H(G)/\iota
where
\iota
is the involution of
G
, and as set of arrows
Q_{1}:=\{(h^{\prime},h)\in H(G)\times H(G)\mid\text{ $h$ is direct predecessor of $h^{\prime}$ with respect to $\leq$}\}
, with
\mathbf{s}(h^{\prime},h):=\{h,\iota h\}
and
\mathbf{t}(h^{\prime},h):=\{h^{\prime},\iota h^{\prime}\}
. The two-sided ideal
I
is generated by all paths of length two
(h^{\prime}_{2},h_{2})(h^{\prime}_{1},h_{1})
such that
h^{\prime}_{1}=\iota h_{2}
.

We easily verify that given a (connected) marked ribbon graph
(G,{\leq})
the pair
\triangledown(G,\leq)
is a (connected) gentle bound quiver having at least one arrow.

Remark 3.12
.

For a vertex
\alpha
of a marked ribbon graph
(G,\leq)
we have a linear order on the half-edges of
G
that are incident to
\alpha
,

\mathrm{z}^{-1}(\alpha)=\{h^{\alpha}_{0}>h^{\alpha}_{1}>\ldots>h^{\alpha}_{t-1}>h^{\alpha}_{t}>\ldots>h^{\alpha}_{\ell^{\alpha}-1}>h^{\alpha}_{\ell^{\alpha}}\},

(3.13)

with
h^{\alpha}_{t}
direct predecessor of
h^{\alpha}_{t-1}
, cf. Remark
3.8
. Analogously to the conventions in Remark
3.3
, we fix the following notation for the arrows and permitted threads of the gentle bound quiver
\triangledown(G,\leq)
. If
\ell^{\alpha}>0
, then we denote by
\alpha_{t}
the arrow
(h^{\alpha}_{t-1},h^{\alpha}_{t})
for
t=1,\ldots,\ell^{\alpha}
, and by
\eta^{\alpha}
the path
\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{\ell^{\alpha}}
of
\triangledown(G,\leq)=(Q,I)
. By construction, every non-trivial path
\alpha_{t}\cdots\alpha_{t^{\prime}}
with
1\leq t\leq t^{\prime}\leq\ell^{\alpha}
is permitted, and
\eta^{\alpha}
is a maximal permitted path hence a (non-trivial) permitted thread. If
\ell^{\alpha}=0
, then we set
\eta^{\alpha}:=\mathbf{1}_{\{h_{0}^{\alpha},\iota h_{0}^{\alpha}\}}
and we check directly that it is a trivial permitted thread. Note that every permitted thread in
\triangledown(G,\leq)
can be found in the way.

3.4
),

Note that the construction of Definition
3.11
when applied to the graph
G
with two vertices joint by a single edge, which is not included in the definition of marked ribbon graph (
3.7
), produces a quiver
Q
with one vertex and no arrow, which we do not consider to be gentle (Definition
3.1
) mainly because it has no permitted threads.

It follows from
Opper et al. [2018]
,
Amiot et al. [2023]
that the constructions of Definitions
3.9
and
3.11
are inverse to each other, see also
[
Palu et al., 2019
, Theorem 4.10]
. For convenience, we provide a brief explicit proof.

Lemma 3.14
.

Let
(G,{\leq})
be a marked ribbon graph and
(Q,I)
a gentle bound quiver. Then

(a)

(G,\leq)
and
\triangle(\triangledown(G,\leq))
are isomorphic as marked ribbon graphs,

(b)

(Q,I)
and
\triangledown(\triangle(Q,I))
are isomorphic as bound quivers.

Proof.

Taking
(G^{\prime},\leq^{\prime}):=\triangle(\triangledown(G,\leq))
and
(Q^{\prime},I^{\prime}):=\triangledown(\triangle(Q,I))
we consider the following mappings:

\textstyle{V(G)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{V}}
\textstyle{V(G^{\prime})}
\textstyle{\alpha\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\eta^{\alpha}}

\textstyle{H(G)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{H}}
\textstyle{H(G^{\prime})}
\textstyle{h^{\alpha}_{t}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(\eta^{\alpha}_{[t]},{}_{[t]}\eta^{\alpha})}

\textstyle{Q_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g_{0}}
\textstyle{Q^{\prime}_{0}}
\textstyle{i\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\{h_{i},\iota h_{i}\}}

\textstyle{Q_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g_{1}}
\textstyle{Q^{\prime}_{1}}
\textstyle{\alpha_{r}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{((\eta^{\alpha}_{[r-1]},{}_{[r-1]}\eta^{\alpha}),(\eta^{\alpha}_{[r]},{}_{[r]}\eta^{\alpha})),}

where
\{h_{i},\iota h_{i}\}
is the pair of split permitted threads of
(Q,I)
with center
i
as in Lemma
3.2
(b). By Remark
3.12
the mappings
f_{V}
and
f_{H}
are well-defined bijections and a routine check shows that they define a graph isomorphism between
G
and
G^{\prime}
(cf.
2.2
) such that
f_{H}
is an isomorphism between the partially ordered sets
(H(G),\leq)
and
(H(G^{\prime}),\leq^{\prime})
, hence it commutes with marked ribbon graph structures. Similarly, by Lemma
3.2
and Remark
3.3
the mappings
g_{0}
and
g_{1}
are well-defined bijections
and
g=(g_{0},g_{1}):Q\to Q^{\prime}
is an isomorphism of quivers (that is,
\mathbf{t}^{\prime}\circ g_{1}=g_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}
and
\mathbf{s}^{\prime}\circ g_{1}=g_{0}\circ\mathbf{s}
) such that the induced mapping
g:kQ\to kQ^{\prime}
sends the generating monomial relations of
I
to those of
I^{\prime}
. ∎

Lemma 3.15
.

If
(Q,I)
is a gentle bound quiver with marked ribbon graph
(G,\leq)
, then there is a
1-1
correspondence between string functions of
(Q,I)
and directions of the graph
G
. In particular, there exist precisely
2^{|Q_{0}|}
pairs of string functions for
(Q,I)
.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.14
we can assume that
(Q,I)=\triangledown(G,\leq)
as in Definition
3.11
. If
S,T
are string functions of
(Q,I)
, define a bidirection
\sigma^{S,T}
of
G
by taking, if
\mathrm{z}^{-1}(\alpha)=\{h^{\alpha}_{0}>h^{\alpha}_{1}>\ldots>h^{\alpha}_{\ell^{\alpha}}\}
for a vertex
\alpha
of
G
,

\sigma^{S,T}(h^{\alpha}_{t}):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l l}S(\alpha_{t}),&\text{if $0<t\leq\ell^{\alpha}$},\\
(-1)T(\alpha_{1}),&\text{if $0=t<\ell^{\alpha}$},\\
(-1)\sigma^{S,T}(\iota h^{\alpha}_{t}),&\text{if $0=t=\ell^{\alpha}$}.\end{array}\right.

(3.16)

Recall that the permitted thread corresponding to
\alpha
is
\eta^{\alpha}=\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{\ell^{\alpha}}
where
\mathbf{s}(\alpha_{t})=\{h^{\alpha}_{t},\iota h^{\alpha}_{t}\}
and
\mathbf{t}(\alpha_{t})=\{h^{\alpha}_{t-1},\iota h^{\alpha}_{t-1}\}
(see Remark
3.12
and Definition
3.11
). Take
h^{\beta}_{s}:=\iota h^{\alpha}_{t}
for some vertex
\beta
. The bidirection
\sigma^{S,T}
is well-defined, because
\ell^{\alpha}=0
implies that the permitted thread
\eta^{\beta}
is non-trivial (
\ell^{\beta}>0
), since
G
is connected and has a vertex of degree at least two. That
\sigma^{S,T}
is in fact a direction (that is,
\sigma(h^{\alpha}_{t})\neq\sigma(h^{\beta}_{s})
, see Definition
2.7
) follows from definition if
\ell^{\alpha}\ell^{\beta}=0
, and from the properties of string functions if
\ell^{\alpha}\ell^{\beta}>0
: if
st>0
, then
\sigma^{S,T}(h^{\alpha}_{t})=S(\alpha_{t})
and
\sigma^{S,T}(h^{\beta}_{s})=S(\beta_{s})
, and since
\mathbf{s}(\alpha_{t})=\{h^{\alpha}_{t},h^{\beta}_{s}\}=\mathbf{s}(\beta_{s})
we have
S(\alpha_{t})\neq S(\beta_{s})
. The case
st=0
can be shown similarly.

Now take an arbitrary direction
\sigma
of
G
and define two functions
S^{\sigma},T^{\sigma}:Q_{1}\to\{\pm 1\}
as

S^{\sigma}(\alpha_{t}):=\sigma(h^{\alpha}_{t})
and
T^{\sigma}(\alpha_{t}):=-\sigma(h^{\alpha}_{t-1})
for an arrow
\alpha_{t}=(h^{\alpha}_{t-1},h^{\alpha}_{t})
of
Q
.

(3.17)

To show that
S^{\sigma},T^{\sigma}
are string functions recall that
\mathbf{s}(\alpha_{t})=\{\check{\alpha_{t}},\iota\check{\alpha_{t}}\}
,
\mathbf{t}(\alpha_{t})=\{\hat{\alpha_{t}},\iota\hat{\alpha_{t}}\}
where we set

\hat{\alpha_{t}}:=h^{\alpha}_{t-1}
and
\check{\alpha_{t}}:=h^{\alpha}_{t}
for any arrow
\alpha_{t}=(h^{\alpha}_{t-1},h^{\alpha}_{t})
as above (see Definition
3.11
). Then for any pair of arrows
\alpha_{t}
and
\beta_{s}
of
Q
we have:

•

if
\alpha_{t}\neq\beta_{s}
and
\mathbf{s}(\alpha_{t})=\mathbf{s}(\beta_{s})
, then
\check{\alpha_{t}}=\iota\check{\beta_{s}}
and
S^{\sigma}(\alpha_{t})=\sigma(\check{\alpha_{t}})=\sigma(\iota\check{\beta_{s}})=-\sigma(\check{\beta_{s}})=-S^{\sigma}(\beta_{s})
;

•

if
\alpha_{t}\neq\beta_{s}
and
\mathbf{t}(\alpha_{t})=\mathbf{t}(\beta_{s})
, then
\hat{\alpha_{t}}=\iota\hat{\beta_{s}}
and
T^{\sigma}(\alpha_{t})=-\sigma(\hat{\alpha_{t}})=-\sigma(\iota\hat{\beta_{s}})=\sigma(\hat{\beta_{s}})=-T^{\sigma}(\beta_{s})
;

•

if
\mathbf{s}(\alpha_{t})=\mathbf{t}(\beta_{s})
and
\check{\alpha_{t}}=\iota\hat{\beta_{s}}
(that is,
\alpha_{t}\beta_{s}\in I
), then
S^{\sigma}(\alpha_{t})=\sigma(\check{\alpha_{t}})=\sigma(\iota\hat{\beta_{s}})=-\sigma(\hat{\beta_{s}})=T^{\sigma}(\beta_{s})
;

•

if
\mathbf{s}(\alpha_{t})=\mathbf{t}(\beta_{s})
and
\check{\alpha_{t}}=\hat{\beta_{s}}
(that is,
\alpha_{t}\beta_{s}\notin I
), then
S^{\sigma}(\alpha_{t})=\sigma(\check{\alpha_{t}})=\sigma(\hat{\beta_{s}})=-T^{\sigma}(\beta_{s})
. In this case we necessarily have
\beta=\alpha
and
s=t+1
, since
\alpha_{t}=(h^{\alpha}_{t-1},h^{\alpha}_{t})
and
\beta_{s}=(h^{\beta}_{s-1},h^{\beta}_{s})
with
h^{\alpha}_{t}=\check{\alpha_{t}}=\hat{\beta_{s}}=h^{\beta}_{s-1}
, see (
3.13
).

This shows that
S^{\sigma},T^{\sigma}
are string functions of
(Q,I)
.
Clearly, the constructions
(S,T)\mapsto\sigma^{S,T}
and
\sigma\mapsto(S^{\sigma},T^{\sigma})
are inverse to each other,
which concludes the proof of the main statement. To see the final claim recall that
|Q_{0}|=|E(G)|
and clearly, there exist
2^{|E(G)|}
directions in
G
.
∎

For a gentle bound quiver
(Q,I)
of finite global dimension an analogous construction of Definition
3.9
, replacing “permitted” by “forbidden” notions (cf. Lemma
3.6
), yields also a marked ribbon graph together with a bidirection of the graph. We give notation and brief details for convenience.

Definition 3.18
.

For a gentle bound quiver
(Q,I)
of finite global dimension define a triple
(\tilde{G},\tilde{\leq},\tilde{\sigma})
with
(\tilde{G},\tilde{\sigma})
a bidirected graph and
\tilde{\leq}
a relation on the set of half-edges of
\tilde{G}
as follows. The graph
\tilde{G}=(V,H,\mathrm{z},\iota)
has as set of vertices the forbidden threads of
(Q,I)
,
V:=\mathcal{F}_{Q,I}
, as set of half-edges the split forbidden threads of
(Q,I)
,
H:=\mathcal{SF}_{Q,I}
, the function

\mathrm{z}
is given by
\mathrm{z}(\theta^{\prime\prime},\theta^{\prime}):=\theta^{\prime\prime}\theta^{\prime}
, and the involution
\iota(\theta^{\prime\prime},\theta^{\prime})
is the unique element in
\mathcal{SF}_{Q,I}-\{(\theta^{\prime\prime},\theta^{\prime})\}
having the same center as
(\theta^{\prime\prime},\theta^{\prime})
, cf. Lemma
3.6
(b). The relation
\tilde{\leq}
on
H
is given by
(\theta^{\prime\prime}_{0},\theta^{\prime}_{0})\leq(\theta^{\prime\prime},\theta^{\prime})
if and only if
\theta^{\prime\prime}_{0}\theta^{\prime}_{0}=\theta^{\prime\prime}\theta^{\prime}
and
\ell(\theta^{\prime}_{0})\leq\ell(\theta^{\prime})
. The bidirection
\tilde{\sigma}:H\to\{\pm 1\}
is defined, for the linearly ordered half-edges

\mathrm{z}^{-1}(\alpha)=\{\tilde{h}^{\alpha}_{0}>\tilde{h}^{\alpha}_{1}>\ldots>\tilde{h}^{\alpha}_{\ell^{\alpha}}\}
incident to each vertex
\alpha\in V
, by setting
\tilde{\sigma}(\tilde{h}^{\alpha}_{t}):=(-1)^{\ell^{\alpha}-t}
for
t=0,\ldots,\ell^{\alpha}
. We call the triple
(\tilde{G},\tilde{\leq},\tilde{\sigma})
the
forbidden marked ribbon graph
of
(Q,I)
, and denote it by
\blacktriangleup(Q,I)
.

We verify that
(\tilde{G},\tilde{\leq})
(called the
dual marked ribbon graph
of
(Q,I)
) constructed in the first part of Definition
3.18
is a well-defined (connected) marked ribbon graph. Recall that in the geometric model of a gentle bound quiver
(Q,I)
, the marked ribbon graph
\triangle(Q,I)
(as in Definition
3.9
) is in some sense uniquely embedded into an orientable surface with boundary, where the edges of the graph determine a dissection of the surface (called admissible dissection in
Opper et al. [2018]
,
Amiot et al. [2023]
, or cellular dissection in
Palu et al. [2019]
). In the case of finite global dimension, the dual marked ribbon graph
(\tilde{G},\tilde{\leq})
corresponds to a so-called lamination or dual dissection of
(G,\leq)
in the surface, and the gentle bound quiver
\triangledown(\tilde{G},\tilde{\leq}^{\rm op})
is isomorphic to the Koszul dual of
(Q,I)
, see
[
Palu et al., 2019
, Definition 4.4]
and
[
Opper et al., 2018
, Proposition 1.25]
, where
\tilde{\leq}^{\rm op}
denotes the order on
H(\tilde{G})
inverse to
\tilde{\leq}
.
In comparison to graphs in the geometric model we consider here the graph
\tilde{G}
with additional structure – the bidirection
\tilde{\sigma}
. In Sections
4
and
6
we will use the bidirected graph
(\tilde{G},\tilde{\sigma})
to combinatorially bypass the explicit inversion of Cartan matrices in the analysis of Euler forms.

3.3. Walks and indecomposable perfect complexes

In this subsection we apply the results of Bekkert-Merklen
Bekkert and Merklen [2003]
(see also
Bobiński [2011]
,
Arnesen et al. [2016]
) to describe the indecomposable perfect complexes of a gentle bound quiver
(Q,I)
in terms of walks of the corresponding marked ribbon graph
(G,\leq)
. Recall that all non-trivial permitted paths of
(Q,I):=\triangledown(G,\leq)
are of the form
\alpha_{s+1}\cdots\alpha_{t}=(h^{\alpha}_{s},h^{\alpha}_{s+1})\cdots(h^{\alpha}_{t-1},h^{\alpha}_{t})
for some vertex
\alpha\in V(G)
, see Remark
3.12
. Consider the (unique) ordered edges
i
and
j
of
G
with
\mathbf{s}^{h}(i)=h^{\alpha}_{s}
and
\mathbf{t}^{h}(j)=h^{\alpha}_{t}
, and observe that the target and source of the path
\alpha_{s+1}\cdots\alpha_{t}
are the unordered edges
\overline{i},\overline{j}\in Q_{0}:=E(G)
respectively, and that
j\neq{i}^{-1}
. Thus the situation around the vertex
\alpha
of
G
may be visualized as follows (we use the convention of (
2.6
), and the red arrows symbolize the corresponding arrows of
Q
):

\scriptstyle{h_{s+1}^{\alpha}}
\textstyle{\cdots}
\scriptstyle{h_{t-1}^{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\iota h_{s}^{\alpha}}
\textstyle{{\alpha}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h_{s}^{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{h_{t}^{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\iota h_{t}^{\alpha}}
\textstyle{i}
\textstyle{j}

\scriptstyle\begin{array}[]{c}\\
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\alpha_{t}}\end{array}

\scriptstyle\begin{array}[]{c}\\
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\alpha_{s+1}}\end{array}

In what follows we will use the notation

\langle i,j\rangle:=\alpha_{s+1}\cdots\alpha_{t}.

Conversely, a pair of ordered edges
i
and
j
of
G
is concatenable if and only if the half-edges
\mathbf{s}^{h}(i)
and
\mathbf{t}^{h}(j)
are comparable with the partial order
\leq
, that is,
\mathbf{s}^{h}(i)=h^{\alpha}_{s}
and
\mathbf{t}^{h}(j)=h^{\alpha}_{t}
for some vertex
\alpha\in V(G)
. If
s=t
then
j={i}^{-1}
and the walk
ij
is not reduced. If
s<t
(that is,
h^{\alpha}_{s}>h^{\alpha}_{t}
) we take
\deg(ij):=1
and
\langle i,j\rangle:=\alpha_{s+1}\cdots\alpha_{t}
as above; if
s>t
we take
\deg(ij)=-1
and
\langle i,j\rangle:={\langle{j}^{-1},{i}^{-1}\rangle}^{-1}
. If
\omega=i_{1}i_{2}\cdots i_{L}i_{L+1}
is a reduced walk (
L\geq 0
) we take the
degree
of
\omega
as

\deg(\omega):=\sum_{t=1}^{L}\deg(i_{t}i_{t+1}).

(3.19)

In particular,
\deg(i_{1})=0
and
\deg(\omega^{-1})=-\deg(\omega)
.
Moreover, we have the following fact:

Remark 3.20
.

The construction
\langle-,-\rangle
provides a bijection between the set of reduced walks of
G
of length two and the set of non-trivial permitted paths of
(Q,I)
together with their inverses (the so-called homotopy letters of
(Q,I)
, see discussion below).

As usual, the morphism between indecomposable projective (left) modules
\textstyle{P_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{P_{j}}
given by right multiplication with a path is denoted by same symbol as the path (where we write
P_{i}
instead of
P_{\overline{i}}
, recall that
Q_{0}=E(G)
).

For a reduced walk
\omega=i_{1}i_{2}\cdots i_{L}i_{L+1}
of
(G,\leq)
,
L\geq 0
, consider the following “unfolded diagram” similarly as in
Arnesen et al. [2016]
,

\textstyle{P_{i_{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\langle i_{1},i_{2}\rangle}
\textstyle{P_{i_{2}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\langle i_{2},i_{3}\rangle}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\langle i_{L-1},i_{L}\rangle}
\textstyle{P_{i_{L}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\langle i_{L},i_{L+1}\rangle}
\textstyle{P_{i_{L+1}}}

where
\textstyle{P_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\langle i,j\rangle}
\textstyle{P_{j}}
denotes the morphism
\textstyle{P_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\langle i,j\rangle}
\textstyle{P_{j}}
if
\deg(ij)=1
or
\textstyle{P_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\langle i,j\rangle}^{-1}}
\textstyle{P_{j}}
if
\deg(ij)=-1
. We refer to
[
Arnesen et al., 2016
, Section 2]
for description of the transition of an unfolded diagram to the corresponding “folded” complex. Here we illustrate the procedure with a couple of representative examples. Consider the following marked ribbon graph
(G,\leq)
with corresponding gentle bound quiver (to simplify the notation for walks we draw the edges of
G
together with a fixed order, cf. (
2.6
)),
