\scriptstyle{\left[\begin{smallmatrix}\delta_{1}\\
\beta_{3}\end{smallmatrix}\right]}
\textstyle{\oplus\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\left[\begin{smallmatrix}\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}\alpha_{3}&0\\
0&\gamma_{1}\end{smallmatrix}\right]}
\textstyle{\oplus}
\textstyle{P_{5}}
\textstyle{P_{3}.}

A reduced walk
\omega=i_{1}i_{2}\cdots i_{L}i_{L+1}
of
(G,\leq)
is a
belt
if
i_{1}=i_{L+1}
,
\omega_{[L]}
is a non-trivial (
L\geq 1
) primitive closed walk and
\deg(\omega)=0=\deg(i_{L}i_{1}i_{2})
. Such walks (as the example
\omega^{\prime}
above) produce an additional family of complexes as follows. For an indecomposable automorphism
\mu
of a
k
-vector space
K
consider the complex,

\textstyle{P_{4}\otimes_{k}K\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta_{3}\otimes\mathbf{1}}
\scriptstyle{\delta_{1}\otimes\mathbf{1}}
\textstyle{P_{6}\otimes_{k}K\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}\alpha_{3}\otimes\mu}
\textstyle{P_{3}\otimes_{k}K\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{P_{5}\otimes_{k}K\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma_{1}\otimes\mathbf{1}}
\textstyle{P_{3}\otimes_{k}K}

\textstyle{P_{6}\otimes_{k}K}
\textstyle{P_{4}\otimes_{k}K\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\left[\begin{smallmatrix}\delta_{1}\otimes\mathbf{1}\\
\beta_{3}\otimes\mathbf{1}\end{smallmatrix}\right]}
\textstyle{\oplus\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\left[\begin{smallmatrix}\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}\alpha_{3}\otimes\mu&\gamma_{1}\otimes\mathbf{1}\end{smallmatrix}\right]}
\textstyle{P_{3}\otimes_{k}K.}
\textstyle{P_{5}\otimes_{k}K}

The following description of the indecomposable perfect complexes over a gentle algebra is a reformulation of the classification of Bekkert-Merklen
Bekkert and Merklen [2003]
(cf.
Bobiński [2011]
,
Arnesen et al. [2016]
) in terms of walks of the corresponding marked
ribbon graph.

Proposition 3.21
.

Let
(Q,I)
be a gentle bound quiver with marked ribbon graph
(G,\leq)
. Let
A:=kQ/I
.

(a)

If
x=(m,\omega)
with
m\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\omega=i_{1}i_{2}\cdots i_{L}i_{L+1}
a non-trivial reduced walk of
G
, then there is an indecomposable perfect complex
X^{\bullet}_{x}
over
A
given by the following unfolded diagram,

\textstyle{P_{i_{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\langle i_{1},i_{2}\rangle}
\textstyle{P_{i_{2}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\langle i_{2},i_{3}\rangle}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\langle i_{L-1},i_{L}\rangle}
\textstyle{P_{i_{L}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\langle i_{L},i_{L+1}\rangle}
\textstyle{P_{i_{L+1}},}

where the module
P_{i_{1}}
is positioned at cohomological degree
m
.

(b)

If
x=(m,\omega,\mu)
with
m\in\mathbb{Z}
,
\omega=i_{1}i_{2}\cdots i_{L}i_{L+1}
a belt of
(G,\leq)
and
\mu
an indecomposable automorphism of a finite dimensional
k
-vector space
K
, then there is an indecomposable perfect complex
X^{\bullet}_{x}
over
A
given by the following unfolded diagram,

\textstyle{P_{i_{1}}\otimes_{k}K\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\langle i_{1},i_{2}\rangle\otimes\mu}
\textstyle{P_{i_{2}}\otimes_{k}K\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\langle i_{2},i_{3}\rangle\otimes\mathbf{1}}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\langle i_{L-1},i_{L}\rangle\otimes\mathbf{1}}
\textstyle{P_{i_{L}}\otimes_{k}K\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\langle i_{L},i_{L+1}\rangle\otimes\mathbf{1}}
\textstyle{P_{i_{L+1}}\otimes_{k}K,}

where the module
P_{i_{1}}
is positioned at cohomological degree
m
.

(c)

Every indecomposable perfect complex over
A
is isomorphic to
X^{\bullet}_{x}
for some
x
as in
(
a
)
or
(
b
)
.

Observe that by construction
X^{\bullet}_{m,\omega}[1]\cong X^{\bullet}_{m-1,\omega}
for any
m,\omega
as in (a), and
X^{\bullet}_{m,\omega,\mu}[1]\cong X^{\bullet}_{m-1,\omega,\mu}
for any
m,\omega,\mu
as in (b). For convenience, we set
X^{\bullet}_{m,\omega}
to be the zero complex for any
m\in\mathbb{Z}
and a trivial walk
\omega
. Our proof of Proposition
3.21
relies on Bekkert-Merklen Theorem
Bekkert and Merklen [2003]
for which we recall some terminology. If
(Q,I)
is a gentle bound quiver, a non-trivial walk
\omega
in
Q
is called a
direct homotopy letter
if it is a permitted path in
(Q,I)
, and an
inverse homotopy letter
if
{\omega}^{-1}
is a permitted path in
(Q,I)
. If
\omega
is any non-trivial reduced walk of
Q
, then there is a decomposition
\omega=\sigma_{1}(\omega)\cdots\sigma_{L}(\omega)
with
\sigma_{t}(\omega)
a homotopy letter for
t=1,\ldots,L>0
. Such decomposition is unique if
L
is minimal, in which case we say that
\sigma_{1}(\omega)\cdots\sigma_{L}(\omega)
is the
homotopy presentation
of
\omega
. The
degree
of
\omega
is the difference
\deg(\omega)
of the number of direct homotopy letters minus the number of inverse letters in the homotopy presentation of
\omega
. To be precise, given string functions
S,T
of
(Q,I)
and concatenable homotopy letters
\omega^{\prime},\omega^{\prime\prime}
then
\omega^{\prime}\omega^{\prime\prime}
is a homotopy presentation if and only if

S(\omega^{\prime})T(\omega^{\prime\prime})=\deg(\omega^{\prime})\deg(\omega^{\prime\prime}),

cf.
[
Bobiński, 2011
, p. 645]
. Indeed, if
\deg(\omega^{\prime})\deg(\omega^{\prime\prime})=-1
and
S(\omega^{\prime})T(\omega^{\prime\prime})=1
then
\omega^{\prime}\omega^{\prime\prime}
in not a reduced walk, and if
\deg(\omega^{\prime})\deg(\omega^{\prime\prime})=1
and
S(\omega^{\prime})T(\omega^{\prime\prime})=-1
then
\omega^{\prime}\omega^{\prime\prime}
itself is a homotopy letter. A
non-trivial homotopy string
of
(Q,I)
is a non-trivial reduced walk of
Q
. We consider as
trivial homotopy strings
the symbols
\mathbf{1}_{v,\epsilon}
with
v\in Q_{0}
and
\epsilon\in\{\pm 1\}
, and take
\deg(\mathbf{1}_{v,\epsilon})=0
and
{\mathbf{1}}^{-1}_{v,\epsilon}:=\mathbf{1}_{v,-\epsilon}
. A
homotopy band
of
(Q,I)
is primitive closed homotopy string
\omega=\sigma_{1}(\omega)\cdots\sigma_{L}(\omega)
such that
\deg(\omega)=0
and
S(\omega)T(\omega)=-1=\deg(\sigma_{1}(\omega))\deg(\sigma_{L}(\omega))
for any string functions
T,S
of
(Q,I)
. Denote by
\mathfrak{w}_{\geq t}(G)
the set of reduced walks of a graph
G
having length at least
t
.

Lemma 3.22
.

Let
(Q,I)
be a gentle bound quiver with marked ribbon graph
(G,\leq)
.

(a)

Assume that
i,j
and
j_{0},k
are two concatenable pairs of ordered edges of
G
with
j\neq{i}^{-1}
and
k\neq{j}^{-1}_{0}
.

(a1)

The walks
\langle i,j\rangle
and
\langle j_{0},k\rangle
of
Q
are concatenable if and only if
j_{0}={j}^{\pm}
.

(a2)

If
j_{0}=j^{\epsilon}
for
\epsilon=\pm
then
S(\langle i,j\rangle)=\epsilon\delta T(\langle j^{\epsilon},k\rangle)
for any string functions
S,T
of
(Q,I)
, where
\delta=\deg(\langle i,j\rangle)\deg(\langle j^{\epsilon},k\rangle)
. That is,
\langle i,j\rangle\langle j_{0},k\rangle
is a homotopy presentation if and only if
j_{0}=j
.

(b)

The mapping
\triangledown:\mathfrak{w}_{\geq 2}(G)\longrightarrow\mathfrak{w}_{\geq 1}(Q)
given by
\omega:=i_{1}\cdots i_{L+1}\mapsto\triangledown(\omega):=\langle i_{1},i_{2}\rangle\langle i_{2},i_{3}\rangle\cdots\langle i_{L},i_{L+1}\rangle
is a bijection, whose inverse is denoted by
\triangle
.

(c)

\triangledown({\omega}^{-1})={\triangledown(\omega)}^{-1}
and
\deg(\triangledown(\omega))=\deg(\omega)
, for any
\omega\in\mathfrak{w}_{\geq 2}(G)
.

(d)

\triangledown(\omega)
is a homotopy band of
(Q,I)
if and only if
\omega
is a belt of
(G,\leq)
, for any
\omega\in\mathfrak{w}_{\geq 2}(G)
.

Proof.

Note that the pair
(\langle i,j\rangle,\langle j_{0},k\rangle)
is concatenable if and only if
\mathbf{s}(\langle i,j\rangle)=\overline{j}=\overline{j_{0}}=\mathbf{t}(\langle j_{0},k\rangle)
, that is, if and only if
j_{0}={j}^{\pm}
. To show (a2) let
\sigma
be a direction of the edges of
G
with corresponding string functions
S,T
as in Lemma
3.15
. A direct computation yields

S(\langle i,j\rangle)=\deg(\langle i,j\rangle)\sigma(\mathbf{t}^{h}(j))\qquad\text{and}\qquad T(\langle j^{\epsilon},k\rangle)=-\epsilon\deg(\langle j^{\epsilon},k\rangle)\sigma(\mathbf{s}^{h}(j))=\epsilon\deg(\langle j^{\epsilon},k\rangle)\sigma(\mathbf{t}^{h}(j)).

Hence we have
S(\langle i,j\rangle)=\epsilon\deg(\langle i,j\rangle)\deg(\langle j^{\epsilon},k\rangle)T(\langle j^{\epsilon},k\rangle)
. Due to (a2) and our description of homotopy letters via
\langle-,-\rangle
(Remark
3.20
), claim (b) is a reformulation of the existence and uniqueness of homotopy presentations for non-trivial reduced walks of
Q
.

Claim (c) follows by construction since
\langle{j}^{-1},{i}^{-1}\rangle={\langle i,j\rangle}^{-1}
and definitions of degree. To show (d) take
\omega=i_{1}\cdots i_{L}i_{L+1}\in\mathfrak{w}_{\geq 2}(G)
. Assume first that
\triangledown(\omega)
is a homotopy band. Then
\deg(\omega)=0=\deg(i_{L}i_{1}i_{2})
by (c), and using (a) we get
i_{L+1}=i_{1}
. If
\omega^{\prime}:=\omega_{[L]}=i_{1}\cdots i_{L}
is not primitive then
\omega^{\prime}=\widetilde{\omega}^{r}
for some
r>1
and some walk
\widetilde{\omega}=j_{1}\cdots j_{\ell}
with
j_{1}=i_{1}=i_{L+1}
and
j_{\ell}=i_{L}
. Then

\triangledown(\omega)=\triangledown(\omega^{\prime})\langle i_{L},i_{L+1}\rangle=[\triangledown(\widetilde{\omega})\langle j_{\ell},j_{1}\rangle\triangledown(\widetilde{\omega})\cdots\triangledown(\widetilde{\omega})\langle j_{\ell},j_{1}\rangle\triangledown(\widetilde{\omega})]\langle i_{L},i_{L+1}\rangle=[\triangledown(\widetilde{\omega})\langle j_{\ell},j_{1}\rangle]^{r},

in contradiction with
\triangledown(\omega)
being primitive. This shows that
\omega
is a belt. Conversely, assume that
\omega
is a belt and observe that
\triangledown(\omega)
is a closed walk with
S(\triangledown(\omega))=-T(\triangledown(\omega))
for any string functions
S,T
by (a), and that the conditions on the degree in the definition of a band follow using (c). Assume that the closed walk
\triangledown(\omega)
is not primitive. That is, using (b), we get a walk
\widetilde{\omega}=j_{1}\cdots j_{\ell}
in
G
such that
\triangledown(\omega)=\triangledown(\widetilde{\omega})^{r}
for some
r>1
. Since
\triangledown(\widetilde{\omega})
is closed then
j_{\ell}={j_{1}}^{\pm}
, and since
T(\triangledown(\widetilde{\omega}))=T(\triangledown(\widetilde{\omega})^{r})=T(\triangledown(\omega))=-S(\triangledown(\omega))=-S(\triangledown(\widetilde{\omega}))
, then
j_{\ell}=j_{1}
and the following is a homotopy presentation of
\triangledown(\widetilde{\omega})^{r}
,

[\langle j_{1},j_{2}\rangle\cdots\langle j_{\ell-2},j_{\ell-1}\rangle]\langle j_{\ell-1},j_{\ell}\rangle[\langle j_{1},j_{2}\rangle\cdots\langle j_{\ell-2},j_{\ell-1}\rangle]\langle j_{\ell-1},j_{\ell}\rangle\cdots\langle j_{\ell-1},j_{\ell}\rangle[\langle j_{1},j_{2}\rangle\cdots\langle j_{\ell-2},j_{\ell-1}\rangle]\langle j_{\ell-1},j_{\ell}\rangle,

that is,
\omega=\triangle(\triangledown(\widetilde{\omega})^{r})=[(j_{1}\cdots j_{\ell-1})\cdots(j_{1}\cdots j_{\ell-1})]j_{\ell}=\omega^{\prime r}j_{\ell}=\omega^{\prime r}i_{L+1}
where
\omega^{\prime}:=j_{1}\cdots j_{\ell-1}
, in contradiction with
\omega_{[L]}
being primitive. This completes the proof.
∎

Proof of Proposition
3.21
.

Fix string functions
S,T
of
(Q,I)
and a corresponding direction
\sigma
of
G
as in Lemma
3.15
. We extend the bijection
\triangledown
of Lemma
3.22
(b) to include trivial strings by taking
\triangledown^{S,T}(i):=\mathbf{1}_{\overline{i},-1}
if
i
is a direct arrow of
(G,\sigma)
and
\triangledown^{S,T}(i):=\mathbf{1}_{\overline{i},+1}
if
i
is an inverse arrow (and
\triangledown^{S,T}(\omega):=\triangledown(\omega)
if
\ell(\omega)>1
). This establishes a bijection
\triangledown^{S,T}
(which we sometimes denote also by
\triangledown
) between the set of reduced walks
\mathfrak{w}_{\geq 1}(G)
and the set of homotopy strings of
(Q,I)
. For a homotopy string
\omega^{\prime}
and an integer
m\in\mathbb{Z}
, the description of a corresponding indecomposable perfect complex
\widehat{X}^{\bullet}_{m,\omega^{\prime}}
was given by Bekkert and Merklen in
[
Bekkert and Merklen, 2003
, Theorem 3]
, see details in
[
Bobiński, 2011
, Proposition 3.1]
. Our folded presentation for a walk
\omega
of
(G,\leq)
coincides with the corresponding presentation given in
Arnesen et al. [2016]
, so that

\textstyle{(m,\omega)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X^{\bullet}_{m,\omega}=\widehat{X}^{\bullet}_{m,\triangledown^{S,T}(\omega)}.}

(3.23)

This shows claim (a). By Lemma
3.22
(d), the restriction of the extended function
\triangledown
to the belts of
(G,\leq)
is precisely the set of bands of
(Q,I)
, and claim (b) follows similarly. The last claim (c) follows also from
[
Bekkert and Merklen, 2003
, Theorem 3]
by the bijectivity of
\triangledown^{S,T}
.
∎

The terms homotopy string and band were introduced in
Bobiński [2011]
. Note that if
L=0
(that is,
\omega=i_{1}
) then
X^{\bullet}_{m,\omega}
is the stalk complex
P_{i_{1}}
concentrated in degree
m
. A particular consequence of
[
Bekkert and Merklen, 2003
, Theorem 3]
is that the set of isomorphism classes of indecomposable perfect complexes of a gentle algebra
A=kQ/I
, denoted by
{\rm ind}^{0}({\rm per}\,A)
, partitions into two subsets

{\rm ind}^{0}({\rm per}\,A)={\rm ind}^{0}_{\rm st}({\rm per}\,A)\cup{\rm ind}^{0}_{\rm bd}({\rm per}\,A),

(3.24)

where
{\rm ind}^{0}_{\rm st}({\rm per}\,A)
denotes the classes of the complexes of Proposition
3.21
(a), called
string complexes
, and
{\rm ind}^{0}_{\rm bd}({\rm per}\,A)
denotes the classes of the complexes of Proposition
3.21
(b), called
band complexes
of
A
. Then there is a bijection

\textstyle{[\mathbb{Z}\times\mathfrak{w}_{\geq 1}(G)]/\sim\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{1-1}
\textstyle{{\rm ind}^{0}_{\rm st}({\rm per}\,A),}

(3.25)

where
(m,\omega)\sim(m^{\prime},\omega^{\prime})
when
(m,\omega)=(m^{\prime},\omega^{\prime})
, or
m^{\prime}=m+\deg(\omega)
and
\omega^{\prime}={\omega}^{-1}
.

3.4. Faces of a marked ribbon graph and AAG-invariants

Let
(G,\mathrm{p})
be a ribbon graph with
G=(V,H,\mathrm{z},\iota)
a finite graph. Following
Opper et al. [2018]
, see also
Opper and Zvonareva [2022]
, a (not necessarily reduced) primitive closed walk
\omega=i_{1}\cdots i_{L}i_{L+1}
(
L\geq 0
) is called a
face
of
(G,\mathrm{p})
if the half-edge
\mathbf{s}^{h}(i_{t})
is direct successor of the half-edge
\mathbf{t}^{h}(i_{t+1})
in the cyclic orderings determined by the permutation
\mathrm{p}
(that is,
\mathbf{s}^{h}(i_{L+1})=\mathrm{p}(\mathbf{t}^{h}(i_{1}))
and
\mathbf{s}^{h}(i_{t})=\mathrm{p}(\mathbf{t}^{h}(i_{t+1}))
for
t=1,\ldots,L
). For convenience, faces are considered up to cyclic permutations. A marking
\mathrm{m}
of a ribbon graph
(G,\mathrm{p})
cuts the faces of
(G,\mathrm{p})
into exactly
m:=|V(G)|
walks that can be given as follows in terms of our definition of marked ribbon graph as disjoint union of chains with an involution having no fixed point, see Definition
3.7
and Remark
3.8
.

Fix a marked ribbon graph
(G,\leq)
with corresponding gentle bound quiver
(Q,I):=\triangledown(G,\leq)
(cf. Definition
3.11
). A walk
\omega=i_{1}\cdots i_{L}i_{L+1}
of
G
with
L\geq 0
is called an
anti-walk
of
(G,\leq)
if the half-edge
\mathbf{s}^{h}(i_{t})
is direct successor of
\mathbf{t}^{h}(i_{t+1})
with respect to the partial order
\leq
for
t=1,\ldots,L
, and
\omega
is maximal with this property. One can show directly that:

•

anti-walks are reduced,

•

the mapping
\triangledown
of Lemma
3.22
(b) gives a bijection between the set of anti-walks of a marked ribbon graph
(G,\leq)
, denoted by
\mathcal{F}_{G,\leq}
, and the set of forbidden threads
\mathcal{F}_{Q,I}
of the gentle quiver
(Q,I)=\triangledown(G,\leq)
(in case
i
is an anti-walk of length 1 we set
\triangledown(i):=\mathbf{1}_{\overline{i}}
, cf. the proof of Proposition
3.21
; note that in this case
\mathbf{1}_{\overline{i}}
is a forbidden thread),

•

the degree (
3.19
) of an anti-walk
\omega=i_{1}\cdots i_{L}i_{L+1}
is

\deg(\omega)=L=\ell(\triangledown(\omega)),

(3.26)

•

if
\omega
is an anti-walk with target vertex
\alpha=\mathbf{t}(\omega)
then the half-edge
\mathbf{t}^{h}(\omega)
is the maximal element
\mathrm{m}(\alpha)
of
\mathrm{z}^{-1}(\alpha)
, and for every vertex
\alpha\in V(G)
there is a unique anti-walk with
\mathbf{t}(\omega)=\alpha
, that we denote by
\overleftarrow{\alpha}:=\omega
,

•

similarly, the source half-edge
\mathbf{s}^{h}(\omega)
is the minimal element of
\mathrm{z}^{-1}(\beta)
where
\beta
is the source vertex of
\omega
.

Then the target and source functions restricted to anti-walks are bijections in the following commutative diagram,

\textstyle{V(G)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{F}_{G,\leq}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\triangledown}
\scriptstyle{\mathbf{t}}
\scriptstyle{\mathbf{s}}
\textstyle{V(G)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{P}_{Q,I}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Phi_{1}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{F}_{Q,I}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Phi_{2}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{P}_{Q,I}}

(3.27)

where
\Phi_{1}
and
\Phi_{2}
are the bijections defined in
[
Bobiński, 2017
, 2.2]
. In particular, if
\eta\in\mathcal{P}_{Q,I}
is non-trivial then
\Phi_{1}(\eta)=\theta\in\mathcal{F}_{Q,I}
, where
\theta
is the unique forbidden thread such that
\mathbf{t}(\theta)=\mathbf{t}(\eta)
and either
\ell(\theta)=0
or
\ell(\theta)>0
and the terminating arrows of
\theta
and
\eta
differ. Whereas if
\mathbf{1}_{i}
is a trivial permitted thread then in case there is an arrow
\alpha_{1}
such that
\mathbf{t}(\alpha_{1})=i
, then
\Phi_{1}(\mathbf{1}_{i})
is the (unique) forbidden thread whose terminating arrow is
\alpha_{1}
. If there is no such
\alpha_{1}
then
\Phi_{1}(\mathbf{1}_{i})=\mathbf{1}_{i}
. The bijection
\Phi_{2}
acts dually (with respect to the source function). The composition

\xi:=\mathbf{s}\circ\mathbf{t}^{-1}:V(G)\to V(G)

(3.28)

in diagram (
3.27
) is a permutation of the set of vertices of
G
such that
\overleftarrow{\alpha}
and
\overleftarrow{\xi(\alpha)}
are concatenable walks for any vertex
\alpha
. Note that the concatenation of anti-walks
\omega=\overleftarrow{\alpha^{0}}\overleftarrow{\alpha^{1}}\cdots\overleftarrow{\alpha^{a}}
, with
\alpha^{t}:=\xi^{t}(\alpha)
and
a:=\min\{t\geq 0\mid\alpha^{t+1}=\alpha\}
, is a face of
(G,\leq)
(by a face of
(G,\leq)
we mean a face of the underlying ribbon graph). Moreover, the set
\mathcal{O}=\{\triangledown(\overleftarrow{\alpha^{0}}),\triangledown(\overleftarrow{\alpha^{1}}),\ldots,\triangledown(\overleftarrow{\alpha^{a}})\}
is an orbit of the bijection
\Phi:=\Phi_{1}\Phi_{2}:\mathcal{F}_{Q,I}\to\mathcal{F}_{Q,I}
, and it is easy to show that if we take

\deg^{\circ}(i_{1}\cdots i_{L+1}):=\sum_{t=1}^{L}\deg(i_{t}i_{t+1})+\deg(i_{L+1}i_{1}),

where
\deg(ij)
is as in (
3.19
) if
j\neq{i}^{-1}
and
\deg(ij):=-1
if
j={i}^{-1}
, then using (
3.26
) we get

\ell(\omega)=\deg^{\circ}(\omega)+2|\mathcal{O}|=\sum_{\widetilde{\omega}\in\mathcal{O}}\ell(\widetilde{\omega})+|\mathcal{O}|.

(3.29)

Even though every orbit in
\mathcal{F}_{Q,I}/\Phi
can be found in this way, in general there might be faces of
(G,\leq)
that are not concatenation of anti-walks. We call them
full faces
of
(G,\leq)
, cf. Example
3.36
below. It can be shown that for any full face
\omega=i_{1}\cdots i_{L+1}
,
L\geq 0
:

•

\omega
is reduced,

•

the corresponding walk
\triangledown(i_{1}\cdots i_{L+1}i_{1})
(see Lemma
3.22
(b)) is a full cycle of
(Q,I)
,

•

every full cycle of
(Q,I)
may be found in the above way,

•

we have

\ell(\omega)=\deg^{\circ}(\omega)=L+1=\ell(\triangledown(i_{1}\cdots i_{L+1}i_{1})).

(3.30)

In particular, the gentle bound quiver
(Q,I)
has finite global dimension if and only if every ordered edge of
G
can be extended to an anti-walk, see Lemma
3.5
. We call
\deg^{\circ}(\omega)
the
closed degree
of a closed walk
\omega
.

The closed degree and the length of a closed walk are invariant under cyclic permutations, and when applied to faces of
(G,\leq)
these numbers are related to the
Avella-Alaminos Geiss invariant
(AAG invariant, for short) of the gentle bound quiver
(Q,I)
as follows. Let
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{Q,I}
be the set of arrows of
Q
that are not subpaths of any forbidden thread (that is, those that belong to full cycles) and take
\Phi^{\prime}:\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{Q,I}\to\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{Q,I}
as
\Phi^{\prime}(\alpha_{t})=\beta_{s}
if
\alpha_{t}\beta_{s}\in I
. Then the AAG invariant
\varphi_{Q,I}
of the gentle bound quiver
(Q,I)
is given by

\varphi_{Q,I}(n,m):=|\{\mathcal{O}\in\mathcal{F}_{Q,I}/\Phi\cup\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{Q,I}/\Phi^{\prime}\mid(n_{\mathcal{O}},m_{\mathcal{O}})=(n,m)\}|,\qquad\text{for $n,m\geq 0$},

(3.31)

where
(n_{\mathcal{O}},m_{\mathcal{O}}):=(|\mathcal{O}|,\sum_{\omega\in\mathcal{O}}\ell(\omega))
if
\mathcal{O}\in\mathcal{F}_{Q,I}/\Phi
, and
(n_{\mathcal{O}},m_{\mathcal{O}}):=(0,|\mathcal{O}|)
if
\mathcal{O}\in\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{Q,I}/\Phi^{\prime}
, see details in
Bobiński [2017]
,
Avella-Alaminos and Geiss [2008]
. Note that equivalently to
\varphi_{Q,I}
, one can consider the finite multi-set
\{(n_{\mathcal{O}},m_{\mathcal{O}})\mid\mathcal{O}\in\mathcal{F}_{Q,I}/\Phi\cup\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{Q,I}/\Phi^{\prime}\}
. As usual, we also say that
\varphi_{Q,I}
is the AAG invariant of the gentle algebra
kQ/I
, and we use freely the notation
\varphi_{kQ/I}
.

Theorem 3.32

(
[
Avella-Alaminos and Geiss, 2008
, Theorem A]
)
.

If two gentle algebras
A
and
A^{\prime}
are derived equivalent, then
\varphi_{A}(n,m)=\varphi_{A^{\prime}}(n,m)
for each
n,m\geq 0
.

By the discussion above, there is a bijection between the set of (chosen representatives of the cyclic permutation equivalence classes of) faces
\mathrm{Fa}(G,\leq)
of the marked ribbon graph
(G,\leq)
and the set of orbits
\mathcal{F}_{Q,I}/\Phi\cup\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{Q,I}/\Phi^{\prime}
, and using the identities (
3.29
) and (
3.30
) we get

\varphi_{Q,I}(n,m)=\left|\left\{\omega\in\mathrm{Fa}(G,\leq)\mid\left(\frac{\ell(\omega)-\deg^{\circ}(\omega)}{2},\frac{\ell(\omega)+\deg^{\circ}(\omega)}{2}\right)=(n,m)\right\}\right|.

(3.33)

We will apply equation (
3.33
) in Corollary
4.34
to reformulate the derived invariance of
\varphi_{Q,I}
in graph theoretical terms.

Note that the orbits in
V(G)
of the permutation (
3.28
) correspond to the marked points in the geometric model of gentle algebras, as found along the boundary components of the corresponding (oriented) marked surface, see
Opper et al. [2018]
. In Subsection
6.3
we will see how the bijection (
3.28
) relates the AAG invariant to the Coxeter polynomial of the gentle bound quiver
(Q,I)
.

3.5. Non-perfect complexes

Fix a gentle bound quiver
(Q,I)
with marked ribbon graph
(G,\leq)
and set
A:=kQ/I
. Recall that all indecomposable perfect complexes over
A
are given by reduced walks and belts of
(G,\leq)
, see Proposition
3.21
. Here we provide analogous description of indecomposable non-perfect complexes, that is, objects of the class
{\rm ind}({\mathcal{D}}^{b}(A))\setminus{\rm ind}({\rm per}\,A)
. This approach may be viewed as a gentle algebra analogue of the techniques of
Green walks
in Brauer (ribbon) graphs, see
Green [1974]
and
Schroll [2017]
. Note that via the triangle equivalence
{\mathcal{D}}^{b}(A)\cong\mathcal{K}^{-,b}({\rm proj}\,A)
indecomposable non-perfect complexes correspond to
{\rm ind}(\mathcal{K}^{-,b}({\rm proj}\,A))\setminus{\rm ind}(\mathcal{K}^{b}({\rm proj}\,A))
. We apply the classification of
Bekkert and Merklen [2003]
following conventions established in
Arnesen et al. [2016]
.

Definition 3.34
.

Let
\omega=i_{1}i_{2}\cdots i_{L}i_{L+1}
be a reduced walk of
(G,\leq)
, with
L\geq 1
.

•

The walk
\omega
is called
left resolvable
if
i_{1}
belongs to a full face of
(G,\leq)
and
\deg(i_{1}\cdots i_{t})\geq 0
for all
t=1,\ldots,L+1
.

•

Left resolvable walk
\omega
is
primitive
if there is no
s\in\{2,\ldots,L\}
such that
i_{1}\cdots i_{s}
is a subwalk of a power of a full face, and

i_{s}\cdots i_{L+1}
is left resolvable.

Fix
m\in\mathbb{Z}
and a primitive left resolvable walk
\omega=i_{1}\cdots i_{L+1}
. Then there is a unique face
\phi=j_{1}\cdots j_{K}
in
(G,\leq)
such that
j_{1}=i_{1}
and the composition
\phi\omega
is defined. Consider the complex
X^{\bullet}_{m,-,\omega}
given by the following left-infinite unfolded diagram

\textstyle{\cdots P_{j_{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\langle j_{1},j_{2}\rangle}
\textstyle{P_{j_{2}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\langle j_{2},j_{3}\rangle}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\langle j_{K-1},j_{K}\rangle}
\textstyle{P_{j_{K}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\langle j_{K},j_{1}\rangle}
\textstyle{P_{j_{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\langle j_{1},j_{2}\rangle}
\textstyle{P_{j_{2}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\langle j_{2},j_{3}\rangle}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\langle j_{K-1},j_{K}\rangle}
\textstyle{P_{j_{K}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\langle j_{K},i_{1}\rangle}
\textstyle{P_{i_{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\langle i_{1},i_{2}\rangle}
\textstyle{P_{i_{2}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\langle i_{2},i_{3}\rangle}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\langle i_{L-1},i_{L}\rangle}
\textstyle{P_{i_{L}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\langle i_{L},i_{L+1}\rangle}
\textstyle{P_{i_{L+1}},}

(3.35)

with
P_{i_{1}}
positioned at cohomological degree
m
. Observe that the homotopy letters
\langle j_{t},j_{t+1}\rangle
and
\langle j_{K},j_{1}\rangle
are direct of length 1 by the properties of a full face. One may view (
3.35
) as the diagram of the infinite word
\cdots\phi\phi\phi\omega
, analogously as for finite walks in Subsection
3.3
. We consider the obvious dual definitions of (
primitive
)
right resolvable
walk and the associated complex
X^{\bullet}_{m,+,\omega}
given by right-infinite unfolded diagram dual to (
3.35
). A reduced walk
\omega
of
(G,\leq)
is called (
primitive
)
two-sided resolvable
if it is both (primitive) left resolvable and (primitive) right resolvable and we consider the associated complex
X^{\bullet}_{m,\mp,\omega}
given by two-sided infinite unfolded diagram (compare with
[
Arnesen et al., 2016
, 2.3]
). In each of the cases we position
P_{i_{1}}
at cohomological degree
m
. Denote by
\mathfrak{lw}(G,\leq)

(
resp.
\mathfrak{wr}(G,\leq)
,
\mathfrak{lwr}(G,\leq):=\mathfrak{lw}(G,\leq)\cap\mathfrak{wr}(G,\leq))
the set of all primitive left

(
resp. right, two-sided
)
resolvable walks of
(G,\leq)
.

.

)):

The graph
(G,\leq)
has three faces:
{3}^{-1}1
(being the concatenation of anti-walks
\overleftarrow{\alpha}={3}^{-1}
and
\overleftarrow{\beta}=1
) and two full faces
2{1}^{-1}
and
3{2}^{-1}
. The walk
\omega:={1}^{-1}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}>}}2_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}<}}{3}^{-1}
is primitive two-sided resolvable and the associated two-sided infinite unfolded diagram with the corresponding complex
X^{\bullet}_{m,\mp,\omega}
for fixed
m\in\mathbb{Z}
look as follows

\textstyle{\cdots P_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta_{1}}
\textstyle{P_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{2}}
\textstyle{P_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta_{1}}
\textstyle{P_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{2}}
\textstyle{P_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta_{1}}
\textstyle{P_{2}}
\textstyle{P_{3}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{1}}
\textstyle{P_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta_{2}}
\textstyle{P_{3}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{1}}
\textstyle{P_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta_{2}}
\textstyle{P_{3}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{1}}

462.771pt\raise-17.41666pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{P_{3}}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 400.62341pt\raise-34.83331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 471.521pt\raise-34.83331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

Observe that here
X^{\bullet}_{m,-,\omega}\cong X^{\bullet}_{m,+,{\omega}^{-1}}
, since
\deg(\omega)=0
.

The classification of indecomposable non-perfect complexes provided by Bekkert-Merklen
Bekkert and Merklen [2003]
can be reformulated in terms of walks in the marked ribbon graph as follows. We give a sketch of the proof leaving the details to the reader.

Proposition 3.37
.

Let
(Q,I)
be a gentle bound quiver and
(G,\leq)
the associated marked ribbon graph. Then for every
\omega\in\mathfrak{lw}(G,\leq\nolinebreak)

(
resp.
\omega\in\mathfrak{wr}(G,\leq)
,
\omega\in\mathfrak{lwr}(G,\leq))
the complex

X^{\bullet}_{m,\epsilon,\omega}
for
\epsilon=-

(
resp.
\epsilon=+
,
\epsilon=\mp)
is an indecomposable object of
\mathcal{K}^{-,b}({\rm proj}\,A)
for
A=kQ/I
. Moreover, the construction of
X^{\bullet}_{m,\epsilon,\omega}
induces the following bijection with the set of the isomorphism classes of indecomposable non-perfect complexes over
A

\textstyle{[\mathbb{Z}\times\{-\}\times\mathfrak{lw}(G,\leq)]\ \cup\ ([\mathbb{Z}\times\{\mp\}\times\mathfrak{lwr}(G,\leq)]/\sim)\ \ \ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ \ }
\scriptstyle{1-1}
\textstyle{\ \ {\rm ind}^{0}(\mathcal{K}^{-,b}({\rm proj}\,A))\setminus{\rm ind}^{0}(\mathcal{K}^{b}({\rm proj}\,A)),}

(3.38)

where
(m,\mp,\omega)\sim(m^{\prime},\mp,\omega^{\prime})
when
(m,\omega)=(m^{\prime},\omega^{\prime})
, or
m^{\prime}=m+\deg(\omega)
and
\omega^{\prime}={\omega}^{-1}
.

Proof.

By applying Lemma
3.22
and the properties of a full face from Subsection
3.4
we check directly that a reduced walk
\omega
of
(G,\leq)
is primitive left (resp. right, two-sided) resolvable if and only if the walk
\triangledown(\omega)
of
Q
is a primitive left (resp. right, two-sided) resolvable homotopy string in the sense of
[
Arnesen et al., 2016
, Definition 2.6]
. Moreover,
for every
\omega\in\mathfrak{lw}(G,\leq\nolinebreak)

(
resp.
\omega\in\mathfrak{wr}(G,\leq)
,
\omega\in\mathfrak{lwr}(G,\leq))
the complex

X^{\bullet}_{m,\epsilon,\omega}
for
\epsilon=-

(
resp.
\epsilon=+
,
\epsilon=\mp)
is precisely the complex associated with the (respectively shifted) “infinite homotopy string” denoted by
{}^{\infty}\triangledown(\omega)
(resp.
\triangledown(\omega)^{\infty}
,
{}^{\infty}\triangledown(\omega)^{\infty}
) in
[
Arnesen et al., 2016
, 2.3]
.
Thus the hypothesis follows from
[
Bekkert and Merklen, 2003
, Theorem 3]
, see also
[
Arnesen et al., 2016
, Theorem 2.9]
. With regard to the parametrization (
3.38
) we only note that
\omega
is right resolvable if and only if
\omega^{-1}
is left resolvable thus it is enough to take primitive left resolvable walks as representatives, cf. Example
3.36
. Moreover, it easily follows by construction that
X^{\bullet}_{m,\mp,\omega}\cong X^{\bullet}_{m+\deg(\omega),\mp,\omega^{-1}}
, for each
m\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\omega\in\mathfrak{lwr}(G,\leq)
. ∎

Remark 3.39
.

As seen in Propositions
3.21
and
3.37
, the reduced walks and belts of a marked ribbon graph
(G,\leq)
are compact expressions of the indecomposable complexes in the bounded derived category of the gentle algebra
A=kQ/I
with bound quiver
(Q,I)=\triangledown(G,\leq)
, cf. Lemma
3.14
. A description of the morphisms between two such indecomposable objects was given by Arnesen-Laking-Pauksztello
Arnesen et al. [2016]
for algebraically closed fields. Translating such description to our graphical setting yields certain maximally common subwalks, or “overlaps”, which simplify the string approach of
Arnesen et al. [2016]
by unifying so-called graph/quasi-graph maps and single/double maps into non-trivial and trivial “overlaps”, respectively. We believe, however, that a precise formulation and necessary details for these claims deserve a separate paper, especially in the context of tilting and silting theory. For the main goals of the present paper it is more important to provide a description of Auslander-Reiten triangles, see Proposition
6.4
below.

4  Derived invariants

In this section we formulate and prove the first main result of the paper, Theorem
4.8
(Theorem A from the introduction) and provide several applications, see Subsection
4.5
. We start by recalling basic notions and facts related Grothendieck groups, homological bilinear and quadratic forms and other derived invariants.

4.1. Grothendieck groups and the Euler form

Let
A
be an arbitrary (finite dimensional)
k
-algebra and let
\{S_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
,
\{P_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
and
\{E_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
be complete sets of representatives of isomorphism classes of simple, indecomposable projective and indecomposable injective left
A
-modules respectively, with
P_{i}
a projective cover of
S_{i}
and
E_{i}
and injective envelope of
S_{i}
(cf. Section
3
). By
K_{0}(\mathrm{mod}\,A)
and
K_{0}({\mathcal{D}}^{b}(A))
we denote the Grothendieck groups of the category
\mathrm{mod}\,A
of finite dimensional
A
-modules and the derived category
{\mathcal{D}}^{b}(A)={\mathcal{D}}^{b}(\mathrm{mod}\,A)
of
\mathrm{mod}\,A
, defined by means of short exact sequences and the distinguished triangles, respectively (see
Assem et al. [2006]
and
Happel [1988]
for the details). Recall that there is an isomorphism
K_{0}(\mathrm{mod}\,A)\overset{\cong}{\rightarrow}K_{0}({\mathcal{D}}^{b}(A))
of abelian groups induced by the canonical embedding of
\mathrm{mod}\,A
into
{\mathcal{D}}^{b}(A)
. The inverse mapping is given by

[X^{\bullet}]\ \,\mapsto\ \,\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}(-1)^{i}[X^{i}],

(4.1)

where
[X^{\bullet}]
denotes the class in
K_{0}({\mathcal{D}}^{b}(A))
of a complex
X^{\bullet}
from
{\mathcal{D}}^{b}(A)
and
[X^{i}]
, the class in
K_{0}(\mathrm{mod}\,A)
of the
A
-module
X^{i}
of
X^{\bullet}
of cohomological degree
i
. The groups
K_{0}(\mathrm{mod}\,A)
and
K_{0}({\mathcal{D}}^{b}(A))
are freely generated by the corresponding classes
\{[S_{i}]\}_{i=1}^{n}
of simple modules. Given an
A
-module
X
(resp. a complex
X^{\bullet}
), the coordinate vector in
\mathbb{Z}^{n}
of the class
[X]\in K_{0}(\mathrm{mod}\,A)
(resp.
[X^{\bullet}]\in K_{0}({\mathcal{D}}^{b}(A))
) in the basis
\{[S_{i}]\}_{i=1}^{n}
is called the
dimension vector
of
X
(resp. of
X^{\bullet}
) and it is denoted by
\underline{\mathrm{dim}}(X)\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
(resp.
\underline{\mathrm{dim}}(X^{\bullet})\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
). Observe that
\underline{\mathrm{dim}}(X)
belongs to
\mathbb{N}^{n}
, while
\underline{\mathrm{dim}}(X^{\bullet})
may have negative coefficients, cf. (
4.1
) and
Happel [1988]
.

Similarly, we consider the Grothendieck groups
K_{0}({\rm proj}\,A)
and
K_{0}({\rm per}\,A)
of the categories
{\rm proj}\,A
of projective
A
-modules and
{\rm per}\,A
of perfect complexes, respectively (cf. Section
3
). Also in this case there is a natural isomorphism
K_{0}({\rm proj}\,A)\overset{\cong}{\rightarrow}K_{0}({\rm per}\,A)
induced by the canonical embedding of
{\rm proj}\,A
into
\mathcal{K}^{b}({\rm proj}\,A)
. The groups
K_{0}({\rm proj}\,A)\cong K_{0}({\rm per}\,A)
are freely generated by the corresponding classes
\{[P_{i}]\}_{i}
of indecomposable projective modules. Given an
A
-module
X
in
{\rm proj}\,A
(resp. a perfect complex
X^{\bullet}
), the coordinate vector in
\mathbb{Z}^{n}
of the class
[X]\in K_{0}({\rm proj}\,A)
(resp.
[X^{\bullet}]\in K_{0}({\rm per}\,A)
) in the basis
\{[P_{i}]\}_{i=1}^{n}
is denoted by
\underline{\mathrm{dim}}^{\rm p}(X)\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
(resp.
\underline{\mathrm{dim}}^{\rm p}(X^{\bullet})\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
).

Recall that the mapping
P\mapsto P/\mathrm{rad}(P)
induces an isomorphism

K_{0}({\rm proj}\,A)\overset{\cong}{\rightarrow}K_{0}(\mathrm{mod}\,A)

(4.2)

which sends the basis elements
[P_{i}]
to
[S_{i}]
for each
i
. However, recall that the categories
{\rm per}\,A\cong\mathcal{K}^{b}({\rm proj}\,A)
and
{\mathcal{D}}^{b}(A)
are not equivalent in general.

In what follows we use the bilinear and quadratic forms, which are usually defined on
K_{0}(\mathrm{mod}\,A)
for algebras of finite global dimension (cf.
Assem et al. [2006]
,
Happel [1988]
). For our purposes it is useful to consider the following slightly more general notions (cf.
Ladkani [2017]
,
Bocian and Skowroński [2005]
).

Definition 4.3
.

Let
A
be a
k
-algebra. The
homological bilinear form

\langle-,-\rangle_{A}:K_{0}({\rm per}\,A)^{2}\to\mathbb{Z}
of
A
is given by

\langle[X^{\bullet}],[Y^{\bullet}]\rangle_{A}=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}(-1)^{i}{\rm dim}_{k}\mathrm{Hom}_{{\mathcal{D}}^{b}(A)}(X^{\bullet},Y^{\bullet}[i]).

Its associated quadratic form
q_{A}(x):=\langle x,x\rangle_{A}
is called the
homological quadratic form
of
A
, or simply
Euler form
.

Remark 4.4
.

The Gram matrix of the bilinear form
\langle-,-\rangle_{A}
in the basis
P=\{[P_{i}]\}_{i=1}^{n}
of indecomposable projectives is the
Cartan matrix

C_{A}:=M_{\langle-,-\rangle_{A},P}=[{\rm dim}_{k}\mathrm{Hom}_{A}(P_{i},P_{j})]_{i,j}
of a
k
-algebra
A
(cf. (
2.1
)). Note that for bound quiver algebras we have
C_{A}\mathbf{e}_{i}=\underline{\mathrm{dim}}(P_{i})
and
C_{A}^{\rm{tr}}\mathbf{e}_{i}=\underline{\mathrm{dim}}(E_{i})
for each
i=1,\ldots,n
, see for instance
Barot [2015]
. If two algebras
A
and
A^{\prime}
are derived equivalent then their bilinear forms, resp. Euler forms are equivalent; in particular, the rank
\mathrm{rk}(q_{A})
, the corank
\mathrm{crk}(q_{A})
and the determinant of the Cartan matrix are derived equivalence invariants, see
Happel [1988]
and
[
Bocian and Skowroński, 2005
, Proposition 1.5]
. Moreover, if
A
has finite global dimension
{\rm gl.dim}(A)
, then it follows from
Happel [1988]
that:

(a)

There is a triangle equivalence of categories
\mathcal{K}^{b}({\rm proj}\,A)
and
{\mathcal{D}}^{b}(A)
, that is,
{\rm per}\,A={\mathcal{D}}^{b}(A)
. In particular, the identity mapping
P\mapsto P
induces an isomorphism of groups
K_{0}({\rm proj}\,A)\overset{\cong}{\rightarrow}K_{0}(\mathrm{mod}\,A)
(alternative to (
4.2
)).

(b)

The Cartan matrix
C=C_{A}
is
\mathbb{Z}
-invertible and it is the matrix of the isomorphism
K_{0}({\rm proj}\,A)\overset{\cong}{\rightarrow}K_{0}(\mathrm{mod}\,A)
from (a) in the bases of indecomposable projectives and simples, respectively.

(c)

The (perfect) bilinear form
\langle-,-\rangle_{A}
is the (classical) Euler characteristic of
A
and its Gram matrix in the basis of simple
A
-modules is
(C^{-1})^{\rm{tr}}\cdot C\cdot C^{-1}=(C^{-1})^{\rm{tr}}
, cf.
[
Assem et al., 2006
, III.3]
.

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
(Q,I)
be an admissible bound quiver with
Q
connected. Then the Euler form
q_{A}:K_{0}({\rm per}\,A)\to\mathbb{Z}
of the algebra
A:=kQ/I
in the basis of indecomposable projectives is connected.

Proof.

Recall that the indecomposable projective
A
-modules are of the form
P_{i}:=A\mathbf{1}_{i}
, for
i\in Q_{0}=\{1,\ldots,n\}
(cf. the beginning of Section
3
).
Observe that for each arrow
\alpha\in Q_{1}
with
j:=\mathbf{t}(\alpha)
and
i:=\mathbf{s}(\alpha)
there is a non-zero
A
-homomorphism
\bar{\alpha}:P_{j}\to P_{i}
induced by the right composition by
\alpha
(cf.
[
Bobiński, 2011
, p. 644]
). This means that the corresponding coefficient of
q=q_{A}
in the basis of indecomposable projectives
q_{ji}=q_{ij}=q([P_{i}],[P_{j}])=\langle[P_{i}],[P_{j}]\rangle_{A}+\langle[P_{j}],[P_{i}]\rangle_{A}={\rm dim}_{k}\mathrm{Hom}_{A}(P_{i},P_{j})+{\rm dim}_{k}\mathrm{Hom}_{A}(P_{j},P_{i})
is non-zero (cf. (
2.3
)). Now take arbitrary
i^{\prime}\neq j^{\prime}
in
\{1,\ldots,n\}
. Since
Q
is connected then there exists a walk
\omega=\xi_{1}\cdots\xi_{\ell}
in
Q
with
\xi_{t}\in Q_{1}\cup{Q}^{-1}_{1}
for
t=1,\ldots,\ell\geq 1
such that
\mathbf{s}(\omega)=i^{\prime}
and
\mathbf{t}(\omega)=j^{\prime}
. Thus by the above observations the walk
\omega
induces the sequence of non-zero coefficients
q_{i_{t-1}i_{t}}\neq 0
for
i=1,\ldots,\ell
, where
i_{t}=\mathbf{s}(\xi_{t})
,
i_{\ell}=i^{\prime}
and
i_{0}=j^{\prime}
. This shows that
q_{A}
in the basis of projectives is connected, see Subsection
2.1
.
∎

It should be noted that the Euler form
q_{A}
of the algebra
A=kQ/I
of a connected admissible bound quiver
(Q,I)
may be disconnected in the basis of simple
A
-modules.

4.2. Main result

To formulate the main result we need to establish some notation.
Fix a gentle bound quiver
(Q,I)
. Given a closed walk
\omega=\xi_{1}\cdots\xi_{\ell}
in
Q
with
\xi_{t}\in Q_{1}\cup{Q}^{-1}_{1}
for
t=1,\ldots,\ell\geq 1
, assume that
\omega
has no repeated edges, that is
\xi_{i}\neq\xi_{j}
and
\xi_{i}\neq{\xi}^{-1}_{j}
for all
i\neq j
. In particular, this means that
\omega
is reduced. By
{\rm nr}_{+}(\omega)
we denote the number of monomial relations of length two
\rho\in I
that are either a subpath of
\omega
, or of the form
\rho=\xi_{\ell}\xi_{1}
with
\xi_{1},\xi_{\ell}\in Q_{1}
(we call them
clockwise relations
on
\omega
, cf.
Assem and Skowroński [1987]
). Similarly, denote by
{\rm nr}_{-}(\omega)
the number of monomial relations of length two
\rho\in I
that are either a subpath of
{\omega}^{-1}
or of the form
\rho={\xi}^{-1}_{1}{\xi}^{-1}_{\ell}
with
{\xi}^{-1}_{1},{\xi}^{-1}_{\ell}\in Q_{1}
(we call them
counterclockwise relations
on
\omega
). We set
{\rm nr}(\omega):={\rm nr}_{+}(\omega)+{\rm nr}_{-}(\omega)
and we say that
\omega
contains
{\rm nr}(\omega)
relations. Note that
{\rm nr}(\omega)={\rm nr}({\omega}^{-1})={\rm nr}(\omega^{\prime\prime}\omega^{\prime})
for each decomposition
\omega=\omega^{\prime}\omega^{\prime\prime}
for subwalks
\omega^{\prime}
,
\omega^{\prime\prime}
.

Definition 4.6
.

Let
(Q,I)
be a gentle bound quiver and
A:=k(Q,I)=kQ/I
the associated gentle algebra. We say that
(Q,I)
(or
A
) satisfies the
multi-clock condition
, if for each non-trivial closed walk
\omega
in
Q
without repeated edges, the number
{\rm nr}(\omega)
of relations in
\omega

is even. If
A
satisfies the multi-clock condition then we set
{\nabla}_{A}:=1
, otherwise we set
{\nabla}_{A}:=0
.

Remark 4.7
.

We say that an admissible bound quiver
(Q,I)
or the associated algebra
kQ/I
is
t
-cycle if so is the underlying graph of
Q
(we set
{\rm cyc}(Q,I)={\rm cyc}(Q)=t
, cf. (
2.5
)).
If
(Q,I)
is a 1-cycle gentle bound quiver satisfying the
clock condition
in the sense of
Assem and Skowroński [1987]
(that is,
{\rm nr}_{+}(\omega)={\rm nr}_{-}(\omega)
for the unique up to inverse and rotation non-trivial closed walk
\omega
of
Q
without repeated edges)
then
(Q,I)
satisfies the multi-clock condition. Obviously, each 0-cycle gentle bound quiver also satisfies the multi-clock condition, cf.
Assem and Happel [1981]
.

The following main result of this section is a more precise version of Theorem A. We use the notation and the terminology of Section
2
and Definitions
3.9
and
3.18
. Recall that a graph
G
is
bipartite
if there is a partition
V(G)=V^{\prime}\cup V^{\prime\prime}
such that the end-vertices of each edge of
G
belong to different parts of the partition. Alternatively, a graph is bipartite if and only if all its closed walks have even length, if and only if all its cycles have even length (a cycle is a non-trivial reduced closed walk in which only the first and last vertices are equal).

Theorem 4.8
.

Let
(Q,I)
be a gentle bound quiver,
(G,\leq)=\triangle(Q,I)
the associated marked ribbon graph and
(\tilde{G},\tilde{\leq},\tilde{\sigma})=\blacktriangleup(Q,I)
the associated forbidden marked ribbon graph
(
the latter in case
{\rm gl.dim}(Q,I)<\infty)
. Then the Cartan matrix
C_{A}
of the gentle algebra
A:=kQ/I
satisfies

\begin{array}[]{rll}C_{A}+C_{A}^{\rm{tr}}&=&\mathrm{Inc}(G)\mathrm{Inc}(G)^{\rm{tr}}\quad\text{and}\\
C_{A}^{-{\rm{tr}}}+C_{A}^{-1}&=&\mathrm{Inc}(\tilde{G},\tilde{\sigma})\mathrm{Inc}(\tilde{G},\tilde{\sigma})^{\rm{tr}}\ \ (\text{if}\ \ {\rm gl.dim}(A)<\infty).\end{array}

(4.9)

In particular, the Euler form
q_{A}
of
A
is non-negative. Moreover:

(a)

The following conditions are equivalent.

(a1)

(Q,I)
satisfies the multi-clock condition.

(a2)

There exist string functions
S,T:Q_{1}\to\{\pm 1\}
such that
S\equiv-T
,

(a3)

The graph
G
is bipartite.

(a4)

The bidirected graph
(\tilde{G},\tilde{\sigma})
is balanced.

(
The condition
(a4)
is well-defined only in case
{\rm gl.dim}(A)<\infty)
.

(b)

The rank and corank of the Euler form are given by the formulae

\begin{array}[]{l}r=r_{A}:=\mathrm{rk}(q_{A})\ \ =|V(G)|-{\nabla}_{A}\ =\ 2|Q_{0}|-|Q_{1}|-{\nabla}_{A}\ =\ |V(\tilde{G})|-{\nabla}_{A},\\
c=c_{A}:=\mathrm{crk}(q_{A})=|E(G)|-|V(G)|+{\nabla}_{A}\ =\ |Q_{1}|-|Q_{0}|+{\nabla}_{A}\ =\ |E(\tilde{G})|-|V(\tilde{G})|+{\nabla}_{A}\end{array}

(4.10)

(
the last equalities in both rows hold only in case
{\rm gl.dim}(A)<\infty)
. In particular, the binary parameter
{\nabla}_{A}
of Definition
4.6
is a derived equivalence invariant.

(c)

The Euler form
q=q_{A}
in the basis of indecomposable projectives
P:=\{[P_{i}]\}_{i\in Q_{0}}
is connected, Cox-regular and:

(c1)

The diagonal coefficients of
q
are given by:

q_{i}=q_{i}^{P}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l l}2,&\text{if there is a permitted path self-crossing at $i$},\\
1,&\text{otherwise},\\
\end{array}\right.

(4.11)

for each vertex
i\in Q_{0}
. In particular,
q
is a unit form if and only if
(Q,I)
has no self-crossing permitted path.

(c2)

If
q
is a unit form then

\mathrm{Dyn}(q)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathbb{D}_{r},&\text{if }\ {\nabla}_{A}=0\ \text{ and }\ 2|Q_{0}|-|Q_{1}|\geq 4,\\
\mathbb{A}_{r},&\text{otherwise}.\\
\end{array}\right.

(4.12)

(c3)

If
q
is not unitary then
{\nabla}_{A}=0
and

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathrm{Dyn}(q)=\mathbb{C}_{r},&\text{if }\ 2|Q_{0}|-|Q_{1}|\geq 2,\\
\mathrm{Dyn}(\frac{1}{2}q)=\mathbb{A}_{1},&\text{if }\ 2|Q_{0}|-|Q_{1}|=1,\\
\end{array}\right.

(4.13)

where
\frac{1}{2}q
in the latter denotes the
(
unit
)
form given by
x\mapsto\frac{1}{2}q(x)
.

(d)

If
{\rm gl.dim}(A)<\infty
then the Euler form
q=q_{A}
in the basis of simples
S:=\{[S_{i}]\}_{i\in Q_{0}}
is semi-Cox-regular and:

(d1)

The diagonal coefficients of
q
are given by:

q_{i}=q_{i}^{S}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l l}0,&\text{if there is a $($unique$)$ almost-full cycle at $i$ of odd length},\\
2,&\text{if there is a $($unique$)$ almost-full cycle at $i$ of even length},\\
1,&\text{otherwise},\\
\end{array}\right.

(4.14)

for each vertex
i\in Q_{0}
. In particular,
q
is a unit form if and only if
(Q,I)
has no
almost-full cycles. Moreover,

\bullet

if
q
is connected then there is no almost-full cycle of odd length,

\bullet

if there is no almost-full cycle of odd length and
2|Q_{0}|-|Q_{1}|\geq 3
then
q
is connected.

(d2)

If
q
is connected and unitary then the formula (
4.12
) of
(
c
2)
for the Dynkin type holds.

(d3)

If
q
is connected and not unitary then
{\nabla}_{A}=0
and the formula (
4.13
) of
(
c
3)
for the Dynkin type holds.

Observe that even though the Dynkin types of the Euler form
q
in both bases
P
and
S
are described by the same formula, it may happen that
q
is unitary in the basis
P
and non-unitary in
S
and vice-versa. In particular, it may happen that
q
in the basis
P
has Dynkin type
\mathbb{D}
while in the basis
S
it has Dynkin type
\mathbb{C}
etc., cf. comments (D2) in Subsection
2.1
. Moreover, it should be noted that the Euler form of an admissible bound quiver algebra in general does not need to be (semi-)Cox-regular and it is quite surprising that this property is fulfilled for gentle algebras (see claims (c) and (d)). Cox-regularity of the Euler form of a gentle algebra seems to be a reason of some of the good properties of its roots discussed in Section
5
.

The proof of the theorem will be given in Subsection
4.4
. In the next subsection we discuss some preparatory facts required for the proof.

4.3. Incidence quadratic forms

Let
(G,\sigma)
be a bidirected graph.
In
Jiménez González and Mróz [2026]
we studied the following integral quadratic form associated with
(G,\sigma)
.

Definition 4.15
.

The
incidence (quadratic) form
of
(G,\sigma)
is the mapping
q=q_{G,\sigma}:\mathbb{Z}^{E(G)}\to\mathbb{Z}
defined as

q(x):=\frac{1}{2}x^{\rm{tr}}\mathcal{I}\mathcal{I}^{{\rm{tr}}}x=\frac{1}{2}||\mathcal{I}^{{\rm{tr}}}x||^{2},

(4.16)

for
x\in\mathbb{Z}^{E(G)}
, where
\mathcal{I}=\mathrm{Inc}(G,\sigma)\in\mathbb{M}_{E(G),V(G)}(\mathbb{Z})
is the incidence matrix of
(G,\sigma)
as in Definition
2.7
.

We also consider the incidence form of a graph
G
, given by
q_{G}:=q_{G,\sigma^{+}}
, where
\sigma^{+}
is the constant bidirection
\sigma^{+}\equiv+1
. Recall that the incidence matrix of
G
is defined as
\mathrm{Inc}(G)=\mathrm{Inc}(G,\sigma^{+})
(cf. Definition
2.7
).

Lemma 4.17
.

Let
G

(
resp.
(G,\sigma))
be a connected graph
(
resp. bidirected graph) and let
c_{G}:=\mathrm{crk}(\mathrm{Inc}(G))

(
resp.
c_{G,\sigma}:=\mathrm{crk}(\mathrm{Inc}(G,\sigma)))
be the corank of the incidence matrix of
G

(
resp. of
(G,\sigma))
. Then

(a)

c_{G,\sigma}\in\{0,1\}
and
c_{G,\sigma}=1
if and only if
(G,\sigma)
is balanced,

(b)

c_{G}\in\{0,1\}
and
c_{G}=1
if and only if
G
is bipartite.

Proof.

Claim (a) follows from
[
Jiménez González and Mróz, 2026
, Proposition 3.12]
. To show (b) note that all edges of
G
are negative with respect to the constant bidirection
\sigma^{+}
(cf.
2.2
). Therefore the bidirected graph
(G,\sigma^{+})
is balanced if and only if all closed walks in
G
are of even length. This holds precisely when
G
is bipartite. Thus (b) follows from (a).
∎

One of the main results of
Jiménez González and Mróz [2026]
is a description of incidence quadratic forms that we summarize as follows.

Theorem 4.18
.

Let
(G,\sigma)
be a bidirected graph. Then the incidence form
q_{{G,\sigma}}:\mathbb{Z}^{E(G)}\to\mathbb{Z}
is a semi-Cox-regular non-negative integral quadratic form of rank
\mathrm{rk}(q_{{G,\sigma}})=|V(G)|-c_{{G,\sigma}}
and corank
\mathrm{crk}(q_{{G,\sigma}})=|E(G)|-|V(G)|+c_{{G,\sigma}}
.
The following additional properties are expressed in terms of the canonical basis.

(a)

A connected unit form
q:\mathbb{Z}^{n}\to\mathbb{Z}
is non-negative of Dynkin type
\mathbb{A}
or
\mathbb{D}
if and only if
q=q_{G^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}
for some
(
connected
)
bidirected graph
(G^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime})
without loops. Moreover, in this case
\mathrm{Dyn}(q)=\mathbb{D}
if and only if
|V(G^{\prime})|\geq 4
and
{(G^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime})}
is unbalanced.

(b)

A connected irreducible integral quadratic form
q:\mathbb{Z}^{n}\to\mathbb{Z}
is non-negative of Dynkin type
\mathbb{C}
if and only if
q=q_{G^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}
for some
(
connected
)
bidirected graph
(G^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime})
having a bidirected loop.

For a more detailed discussion on the basic properties (connectedness, coefficients) of the incidence forms and some examples we refer to
[
Jiménez González and Mróz, 2026
, Section 2]
.

4.4.
Proof of Theorem
4.8

Fix a gentle bound quiver
(Q,I)
, and let
(G,\leq)=\triangle(Q,I)
be the associated marked ribbon graph and
C_{A}
, the Cartan matrix of the gentle algebra
A:=kQ/I
. Note that since
I
is a monomial ideal, there is an isomorphism of
k
-vector spaces

A\cong\bigoplus_{\text{$\eta$ a permitted path}}k\eta.

(4.19)

Recall that for a vertex
\alpha\in V(G)
, the corresponding permitted thread
\eta^{\alpha}=\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{\ell^{\alpha}}\in\mathcal{P}_{Q,I}
is a string in the sense of Butler-Ringel
Butler and Ringel [1987]
. Consider the set

R_{\eta^{\alpha}}:=\{\text{permitted paths of $(Q,I)$}\}\,\setminus\,\{{}_{[t]}\eta^{\alpha}\}_{t=0}^{\ell_{\alpha}}
.
It is clear that the
k
-linear space
\langle R_{\eta^{\alpha}}\rangle
generated by
R_{\eta^{\alpha}}
is a left ideal of
A
and for the left
A
-module
M_{\alpha}:=A/\langle R_{\eta^{\alpha}}\rangle
there is an isomorphism of
k
-vector spaces

M_{\alpha}\cong\bigoplus_{t=0}^{\ell^{\alpha}}k\,{}_{[t]}\eta^{\alpha}=k\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{\ell^{\alpha}}\oplus k\alpha_{2}\cdots\alpha_{\ell^{\alpha}}\oplus\ldots\oplus k\alpha_{\ell^{\alpha}}\oplus k\mathbf{1}_{\mathbf{s}(\eta^{\alpha})}

by (
4.19
).
Note that
M_{\alpha}
is a left
A
-module isomorphic to the string module associated to the string
\eta^{\alpha}
as defined in
Butler and Ringel [1987]
. In particular, the dimension vector of
M_{\alpha}
encodes the (half-)edges of
G
that are incident to vertex
\alpha
, that is,
\alpha
-th column of the incidence matrix
\mathrm{Inc}(G)
of
G
has the shape:

\mathrm{Inc}(G)\mathbf{e}_{\alpha}=\underline{\mathrm{dim}}(M_{\alpha}),\qquad\text{for every vertex $\alpha\in V(G)$}

(4.20)

(see Definition
2.7
).
Considering a vertex
i
of
Q
as an edge of
G
, then its end-vertices
\alpha,\beta\in V(G)
correspond to permitted threads
\eta^{\alpha},\eta^{\beta}
that intersect at vertex
i
(to be precise, the two split permitted threads with center
i
are
(\eta^{\alpha}_{[r]},{}_{[r]}\eta^{\alpha})
and
(\eta^{\beta}_{[s]},{}_{[s]}\eta^{\beta})
for some integers
r
and
s
, see Lemma
3.2
(b)).
One can directly show that the following is a short exact sequence of left
A
-modules,

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{P_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\left[\begin{smallmatrix}{}_{[r]}\eta^{\alpha}\\
{}_{[s]}\eta^{\beta}\end{smallmatrix}\right]}
\textstyle{M_{\alpha}\oplus M_{\beta}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\left[\begin{smallmatrix}a&-b\end{smallmatrix}\right]}
\textstyle{E_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0,}

(4.21)

where
{}_{[r]}\eta^{\alpha}
denotes the right multiplication by the path
{}_{[r]}\eta^{\alpha}
, and similarly for
{}_{[s]}\eta^{\beta}
,
E_{i}=D(\mathbf{1}_{i}A)=D(\bigoplus k\eta)
is the corresponding left indecomposable injective module, and
a:M_{\alpha}\to E_{i}
is given by
{}_{[t]}\eta^{\alpha}\mapsto 0
if
t<r
and
{}_{[t]}\eta^{\alpha}\mapsto({}_{[r]}\eta^{\alpha})_{[t-r]}^{*}
if
t\geq r
(where the direct sum in the description of
E_{i}
is taken over all permitted paths
\eta
with
\mathbf{t}(\eta)=i
and
\eta^{*}
denotes the usual dual basic element), and similarly for
b
. Together with the definition of incidence matrix, equations (
4.20
) and (
4.21
) imply

\mathrm{Inc}(G)\mathrm{Inc}(G)^{{\rm{tr}}}\mathbf{e}_{i}=\mathrm{Inc}(G)(\mathbf{e}_{\alpha}+\mathbf{e}_{\beta})=\underline{\mathrm{dim}}(M_{\alpha})+\underline{\mathrm{dim}}(M_{\beta})=\underline{\mathrm{dim}}(P_{i})+\underline{\mathrm{dim}}(E_{i})=(C_{A}+C_{A}^{\rm{tr}})\mathbf{e}_{i}.

(4.22)

This happens for every
i\in Q_{0}
which means that the first equality
\mathrm{Inc}(G)\mathrm{Inc}(G)^{{\rm{tr}}}=C_{A}+C_{A}^{\rm{tr}}
of (
4.9
) in Theorem
4.8
holds. Moreover, this shows that the Euler form
q_{A}
in the basis of indecomposable projectives is the incidence form
q_{G}
of
G
and hence it is non-negative (see Definition
4.15
and Subsection
4.1
).

To show the second equality of (
4.9
) we apply the following observation.

Claim 4.23
.

If
\omega
is an anti-walk of
(G,\leq)
having as source the vertex
\mathbf{s}(\omega)=\beta
, then the string complex
X^{\bullet}_{0,{\omega}^{-1}}
is a projective resolution of the string module
M_{\beta}
. In particular, if
\omega=i_{1}\ldots i_{L}i_{L+1}
then the resolution
X^{\bullet}_{0,{\omega}^{-1}}=X^{\bullet}_{-L,\omega}
induces the following exact sequence of
A
-modules

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{P_{i_{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{P_{i_{2}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{P_{i_{L}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{P_{i_{L+1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M_{\beta}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}

This result was noted in
[
Bobiński, 2017
, Lemma 2.4]
and its proof, see also
[
Jiménez González and Mróz, 2026
, Lemma 7.21]
, cf. (
3.26
), (
3.27
) and Subsection
3.3
. Similarly, the anti-walk
\omega
with target vertex
\mathbf{t}(\omega)=\alpha
contains the information of an injective resolution of the string module
M_{\alpha}
(see Claim
6.14
below).

As consequence of Claim
4.23
, for an anti-walk
\omega=i_{1}\ldots i_{L}i_{L+1}
with
\mathbf{s}(\omega)=\beta
we have

\underline{\mathrm{dim}}(M_{\beta})=\sum_{t=1}^{L+1}(-1)^{L+1-t}\underline{\mathrm{dim}}(P_{i_{t}})=C_{A}\sum_{t=1}^{L+1}(-1)^{L+1-t}\mathbf{e}_{\overline{i_{t}}},

(4.24)

cf. Remark
4.4
(recall that
Q_{0}=E(G)
).
Assume now that the gentle bound quiver
(Q,I)
has finite global dimension and let

(\tilde{G},\tilde{\leq},\tilde{\sigma}):=\blacktriangleup(Q,I)
be the forbidden marked ribbon graph as in Definition
3.18
. Observe that by construction, the
\theta
-th column of the incidence matrix of the bidirected graph
(\tilde{G},\tilde{\sigma})
has the shape

\mathrm{Inc}(\tilde{G},\tilde{\sigma})\mathbf{e}_{\theta}=\sum_{t=1}^{L+1}(-1)^{L+1-t}\mathbf{e}_{\overline{i_{t}}},

(4.25)

where
\theta:={\triangledown}(\omega)\in\mathcal{F}_{Q,I}
is the corresponding forbidden thread, see (
3.27
) and Definition
2.7
(recall that
V(\tilde{G})=\mathcal{F}_{Q,I}
and
E(\tilde{G})=Q_{0}
).
Now combining the formulae (
4.20
), (
4.24
) and (
4.25
) we get

\mathrm{Inc}(G)=C_{A}\cdot\mathrm{Inc}(\tilde{G},\tilde{\sigma}),

(4.26)

via the bijection
\Phi_{2}=\mathbf{s}\circ\triangledown^{-1}:\mathcal{F}_{Q,I}\to\mathcal{P}_{Q,I}
of (
3.27
).
Thus, since
C_{A}
is
\mathbb{Z}
-invertible in this case, we have

\mathrm{Inc}(\tilde{G},\tilde{\sigma})\mathrm{Inc}(\tilde{G},\tilde{\sigma})^{{\rm{tr}}}=C_{A}^{-1}\mathrm{Inc}(G)\mathrm{Inc}(G)^{{\rm{tr}}}C_{A}^{-{\rm{tr}}}=C_{A}^{-1}(C_{A}+C_{A}^{{\rm{tr}}})C_{A}^{-{\rm{tr}}}=C_{A}^{-1}+C_{A}^{-{\rm{tr}}}.

(4.27)

This shows the second equality of (
4.9
).
Note that this means that the Euler form
q_{A}
in the basis of simples is the incidence form
q_{\tilde{G},\tilde{\sigma}}
of the bidirected graph
(\tilde{G},\tilde{\sigma})
in the case of
{\rm gl.dim}(A)<\infty
.

(a) By Lemma
3.14
we can assume that
(Q,I)=\triangledown(G,\leq)
. To show the equivalence of (a2) and (a3) let
S,T
be the string functions associated to a direction
\sigma:H(G)\to\{\pm 1\}
of
G
as in Lemma
3.15

and observe that

(i)

S=-T
if and only if
\sigma
is constant on all half-edges incident to a vertex of
G
, that is,
\sigma(h)=\sigma(h^{\prime})
if
\mathrm{z}(h)=\mathrm{z}(h^{\prime})
.

Indeed, assume first that
S=-T
and recall that any arrow of
Q
is contained in a permitted thread
\eta^{\alpha}=\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{\ell^{\alpha}}
with
\ell^{\alpha}\geq 1
, for some
\alpha\in V(G)
with
\mathrm{z}^{-1}(\alpha)=\{h^{\alpha}_{0}>h^{\alpha}_{1}>\ldots>h^{\alpha}_{\ell^{\alpha}}\}
, where
\alpha_{t}=(h^{\alpha}_{t-1},h^{\alpha}_{t})
, see Remark
3.12
and Lemma
3.2
(a).
By definition of
S,T
and the condition
S=-T
we have
S(\alpha_{t})=-T(\alpha_{t+1})=S(\alpha_{t+1})
for
t=1,\ldots,\ell^{\alpha}-1
. Using the definition of
\sigma=\sigma^{S,T}
, see (
3.16
), we have
\sigma(h^{\alpha}_{t})=S(\alpha_{t})=S(\alpha_{t+1})=\sigma(h^{\alpha}_{t+1})
for
t=1,\ldots,\ell^{\alpha}-1
, and
\sigma(h^{\alpha}_{0})=-T(\alpha_{1})=S(\alpha_{1})=\sigma(h^{\alpha}_{1})
, that is,
\sigma
is constant on
\mathrm{z}^{-1}(\alpha)
. Conversely, if
\sigma
is constant on every
\mathrm{z}^{-1}(\alpha)
, by the definition of
S=S^{\sigma}
and
T=T^{\sigma}
, see (
3.17
), we have
S(\alpha_{t})=-T(\alpha_{t})
for
t=1,\ldots,\ell^{\alpha}
. Since every arrow of
(Q,I)=\triangledown(G,\leq)
is of the form
\alpha_{t}=(h^{\alpha}_{t-1},h^{\alpha}_{t})
as above, this shows that
S=-T
, as wanted.

If
G
is bipartite with partition
V(G)=V^{\prime}\cup V^{\prime\prime}
we can define a direction
\sigma
by taking
\sigma(h^{\prime})=1
for all
h^{\prime}\in\mathrm{z}^{-1}(V^{\prime})
and
\sigma(h^{\prime\prime})=-1
for all
h^{\prime\prime}\in\mathrm{z}^{-1}(V^{\prime\prime})
; then by (i) the corresponding string functions satisfy
S^{\sigma}=-T^{\sigma}
. Conversely, if there are string functions
S=-T
of
(Q,I)
then by (i) the associated direction
\sigma^{S,T}
of
G
is constant around each vertex, which defines a partition
V(G)=V^{+}\cup V^{-}
where
V^{\epsilon}:=\{\alpha\in V(G)\mid\text{$\sigma^{S,T}(h)=\epsilon$ for any half-edge $h$ with $\mathrm{z}(h)=\alpha$}\}
. Since
\sigma^{S,T}
is a direction, the end-vertices of each edge of
G
belong to different parts of this partition, which shows that
G
is bipartite.

To show the equivalence of (a1) and (a3), note that any closed walk
\omega
of
Q
without repeated edges is a homotopy string of
(Q,I)
. Since we are interested in
\omega
up to cyclic permutations, we may assume that
\omega=\sigma_{1}(\omega)\cdots\sigma_{L}(\omega)
is a homotopy presentation such that:

If both
\sigma_{1}(\omega),\sigma_{L}(\omega)
are direct (resp. inverse) homotopy letters, then
\sigma_{L}(\omega)\sigma_{1}(\omega)\in I
(resp.
{(\sigma_{L}(\omega)\sigma_{1}(\omega))}^{-1}\in I
).

In this situation we have

(ii)

\deg(\omega)={\rm nr}_{+}(\omega)-{\rm nr}_{-}(\omega)
, where
{\rm nr}(\omega)={\rm nr}_{+}(\omega)+{\rm nr}_{-}(\omega)
is the number of relations in
\omega
as in Definition
4.6
.

Recall that
\deg(\omega)
is the number of direct minus the number of inverse homotopy letters in the decomposition
\omega=\sigma_{1}(\omega)\cdots\sigma_{L}(\omega)
. Considering the letters
\sigma_{t}(\omega)
as arrows in a cycle, say
\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}_{L}
, a simple combinatorial argument shows that the number of clockwise oriented arrows minus the number of counterclockwise oriented arrows of the cycle (that is,
\deg(\omega)
) coincides with the number of clockwise oriented paths of length two minus the number of counterclockwise oriented paths of length two of the cycle (that is,
{\rm nr}_{+}(\omega)-{\rm nr}_{-}(\omega)
by the above condition
(*)
on the homotopy presentation of the closed walk
\omega
).

Moreover, note that for any reduced walk
\widetilde{\omega}
of
G
we have that

(iii)

the integers
\deg(\widetilde{\omega})
and
\ell(\widetilde{\omega})
have different parity.

Since
|\deg(ij)|=1
for any reduced walk
ij
of length two, if
\widetilde{\omega}=i_{1}\cdots i_{L}i_{L+1}
we have
\deg(\widetilde{\omega})=\sum_{t=1}^{L}\deg(i_{t}i_{t+1})\equiv L(\mathrm{mod}\,2)
, that is,
\deg(\widetilde{\omega})-\ell(\widetilde{\omega})\equiv 1(\mathrm{mod}\,2)
.

Assume first that
(Q,I)
satisfies the multi-clock condition, let
i_{1}\cdots i_{L}
be a cycle of
G
and take
\widetilde{\omega}:=i_{1}\cdots i_{L}i_{L+1}
with
i_{L+1}:=i_{1}
. It is easy to see that
\widetilde{\omega}
is a reduced walk and
\omega:=\triangledown(\widetilde{\omega})
is a closed walk without repeated edges, see Lemma
3.22
, satisfying the condition
(*)
above. Since
{\rm nr}(\omega)={\rm nr}_{+}(\omega)+{\rm nr}_{-}(\omega)
is even by assumption, then so is
\deg(\omega)=\deg(\widetilde{\omega})
by (ii), see Lemma
3.22
(c). Using (iii), the length
L+1
of
\widetilde{\omega}
is odd, that is, the length of original cycle
i_{1}\cdots i_{L}
is even. Since this holds for any cycle then
G
is a bipartite graph. Conversely, assume that
G
is a bipartite graph, let
\omega
be a closed walk of
Q
without repeated edges satisfying condition
(*)
above, and take
\widetilde{\omega}:=\triangle(\omega)=i_{1}\cdots i_{L}i_{L+1}
. Since
i_{1}\cdots i_{L}
is a closed walk (by Lemma
3.22
(a) and
(*)
) and
G
is bipartite, then
L
is even and by (iii) the degree
\deg(\widetilde{\omega})=\deg(\omega)
is also even (since
\ell(\widetilde{\omega})=L+1
is odd). By (ii) the difference
\deg(\omega)={\rm nr}_{+}(\omega)-{\rm nr}_{-}(\omega)
and the sum
{\rm nr}(\omega)={\rm nr}_{+}(\omega)+{\rm nr}_{-}(\omega)
are even integers. This shows that
(Q,I)
satisfies the multi-clock condition.

To show the equivalence of (a3) and (a4), assume that
{\rm gl.dim}(A)<\infty
and observe using equation (
4.26
) that the coranks of the incidence matrices
\mathrm{Inc}(G)
and
\mathrm{Inc}(\tilde{G},\tilde{\sigma})
coincide (since in this case
C_{A}
is
\mathbb{Z}
-invertible). By Lemma
4.17
, it follows that
G
is bipartite if and only if
(\tilde{G},\tilde{\sigma})
is balanced, as claimed.

(b) Recall that the Euler form
q_{A}
of
A
in the basis of indecomposable projectives is the incidence form
q_{G}
of (the underlying graph of) the associated marked ribbon graph
\triangle(Q,I)=(G,\leq)
, see (
4.22
). So by Theorem
4.18

the corank of
q_{A}
is given by
\mathrm{crk}(q_{A})=|E(G)|-|V(G)|+c_{{G}}
, where
c_{G}=c_{G,\sigma^{+}}=\mathrm{crk}(\mathrm{Inc}(G))
. Moreover, by Lemma
4.17
(b) and the equivalence “(a1)
\Leftrightarrow
(a3)” proved above, the
value of
\mathrm{crk}(\mathrm{Inc}(G))\in\{0,1\}
coincides with the “multi-clock characteristic” parameter
{\nabla}_{A}
of Definition
4.6
. Thus we have proved the first equality
\mathrm{crk}(q_{A})=|E(G)|-|V(G)|+{\nabla}_{A}
of (
4.10
) for the corank. This gives us also the last equality

\mathrm{crk}(q_{A})=|E(\tilde{G})|-|V(\tilde{G})|+{\nabla}_{A}
in the finite global dimension case, since

|E(G)|=|Q_{0}|=|E(\tilde{G})|\quad\text{and}\quad|V(G)|=|\mathcal{P}_{Q,I}|=|\mathcal{F}_{Q,I}|=|V(\tilde{G})|,

(4.28)

see Definitions
3.9
and
3.18
and the bijections of the diagram (
3.27
), cf. Lemmas
3.2
(b) and
3.6
(b). (Note that one can alternatively apply Theorem
4.18
and the fact that
q_{A}
in the basis of simples is the incidence form
q_{\tilde{G},\tilde{\sigma}}
, cf. Lemma
4.17
(a)). To show the remaining equality
\mathrm{crk}(q_{A})=|Q_{1}|-|Q_{0}|+{\nabla}_{A}
first note that given a vertex
\alpha\in V(G)
, its degree
\deg(\alpha)
(that is, the number of incident half-edges) equals to
\ell(\eta^{\alpha})+1
, where
\ell(\eta^{\alpha})
is the length of the corresponding permitted thread
\eta^{\alpha}\in\mathcal{P}_{Q,I}
, cf. Definition
3.9
. Hence applying (
4.28
) and elementary graph theoretic property we get

2|Q_{0}|=2|E(G)|=\sum_{\alpha\in V(G)}\deg(\alpha)=\sum_{\alpha\in V(G)}(\ell(\eta^{\alpha})+1)=\sum_{\eta^{\alpha}\in\mathcal{P}_{Q,I}}\ell(\eta^{\alpha})+|V(G)|=|Q_{1}|+|V(G)|

(4.29)

(the latter equality follows since every arrow of
Q
is contained in exactly one non-trivial permitted thread, by Lemma
3.2
(a)). In particular, we have
|V(G)|=2|Q_{0}|-|Q_{1}|
and this gives us

\mathrm{crk}(q_{A})=|E(G)|-|V(G)|+{\nabla}_{A}=|Q_{0}|-(2|Q_{0}|-|Q_{1}|)+{\nabla}_{A}=|Q_{1}|-|Q_{0}|+{\nabla}_{A}.

(4.30)

The formulae for the rank
\mathrm{rk}(q_{A})
follow from those for
\mathrm{crk}(q_{A})
since
\mathrm{rk}(q_{A})=|Q_{0}|-\mathrm{crk}(q_{A})
, cf. (
4.28
) and Subsection
2.1
.
To finish the proof of claim (b) it remains to conclude that the binary parameter
{\nabla}_{A}
is a derived equivalence invariant. But this follows from the proved formula for the corank of
q_{A}
. Indeed, by (
4.30
) we have that
{\nabla}_{A}=|Q_{0}|-|Q_{1}|+\mathrm{crk}(q_{A})
and every summand of the right-hand side of this equality is a derived invariant, see Remark
4.4
, and
[
Avella-Alaminos and Geiss, 2008
, Proposition B]
for the invariance of
|Q_{1}|
.

(c) First observe that given an arbitrary bidirected graph
(G^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime})
, the diagonal coefficients of its incidence form
q^{\prime}=q_{G^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}:\mathbb{Z}^{E(G^{\prime})}\to\mathbb{Z}
satisfy:

q^{\prime}_{i}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l l}2,&\text{if $i$ is a negative (i.e., bidirected) loop},\\
1,&\text{if $i$ is not a loop},\\
0,&\text{if $i$ is a positive loop},\end{array}\right.

(4.31)

for
i\in E(G^{\prime})
, see Definitions
2.7
,
4.15
, cf.
[
Jiménez González and Mróz, 2026
, Lemma 2.11]
. In the case of our marked ribbon graph
\triangle(Q,I)=(G,\leq)
, every edge of
G

is negative (with respect to the constant bidirection
\sigma^{+}\equiv+1
). So since the Euler form
q=q_{A}
in the basis
P=\{[P_{i}]\}_{i\in Q_{0}}
is the incidence form
q_{G}=q_{G,\sigma^{+}}
of
G
, by (
4.31
)
we have
q_{i}=q_{i}^{P}\in\{1,2\}
for each
i\in E(G)=Q_{0}
. In particular,
q
is Cox-regular by the first statement of Theorem
4.18
, and
q
is connected by Lemma
4.5
. Moreover, by Definition
3.9
it follows that
G
has a (negative) loop
i\in E(G)
at vertex
\alpha\in V(G)
if and only if the corresponding permitted thread
\eta^{\alpha}
self-crosses at
i
as a vertex in
Q_{0}
. This proves the formula (
4.11
) of (c1), cf. Lemma
3.2
.

Assume now that
q
is unitary. By the arguments above, this holds precisely when
G
has no loops, equivalently, when
(Q,I)
has no self-crossing permitted path. Then by Theorem
4.18
(a),
q
as the incidence form of
G
, has Dynkin type
\mathbb{A}
or
\mathbb{D}
, and
\mathrm{Dyn}(q)=\mathbb{D}
if and only if
|V(G)|\geq 4
and
(G,\sigma^{+})
is unbalanced. Note that
(G,\sigma^{+})
is unbalanced precisely when
G
is not bipartite, equivalently, when
{\nabla}_{A}=0
by the equivalence “(a1)
\Leftrightarrow
(a3)” shown before. This proves (c2), since
|V(G)|=2|Q_{0}|-|Q_{1}|
by (
4.29
) (note that if
{\nabla}_{A}=0
then
r_{A}=2|Q_{0}|-|Q_{1}|
, cf. (
4.10
)).

If
q
is not unitary, then by the arguments above
G
has a loop, which means that
G
is not bipartite. Thus
{\nabla}_{A}=0
. Assume first that
2|Q_{0}|-|Q_{1}|\geq 2
. It means that
r_{A}=|V(G)|\geq 2
, so
q
as the incidence form of
G
having at least 2 vertices is irreducible by
[
Jiménez González and Mróz, 2026
, Lemma 2.14(b)]
. So we can apply Theorem
4.18
(b) which implies that
\mathrm{Dyn}(q)=\mathbb{C}_{r_{A}}
. In the remaining case
r_{A}=|V(G)|=2|Q_{0}|-|Q_{1}|=1
the graph
G
consists of one vertex and
n:=|E(G)|
two-tail loops, thus

\mathrm{Inc}(G)=[2,2,2,\ldots,2]^{\rm{tr}}\in\mathbb{M}_{n,1}(\mathbb{Z})\quad\text{and}\quad C_{A}+C_{A}^{\rm{tr}}=\mathrm{Inc}(G)\mathrm{Inc}(G)^{{\rm{tr}}}=4\cdot J\in\mathbb{M}_{n,n}(\mathbb{Z}),

(4.32)

where
J
denotes the
n\times n
matrix whose all entries equal 1, cf. Definitions
2.7
,
4.15
and (
4.22
). This means that the Euler form
q=q_{A}
in the basis of projectives is given as
q(x)=2x^{\rm{tr}}Jx
, for
x\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}\cong K_{0}({\rm per}\,A)
. In particular,

\frac{1}{2}q(x)=x^{\rm{tr}}Jx
is a non-negative unit form of corank
n-1
and Dynkin type
\mathbb{A}_{1}
, cf. (D1) in Subsection
2.1
and
[
Jiménez González and Mróz, 2026
, Example 6.9]
.

(d) Recall that in the case
{\rm gl.dim}(A)<\infty
the Euler form
q=q_{A}
in the basis
S=\{[S_{i}]\}_{i\in Q_{0}}
of simples is the incidence form
q_{\tilde{G},\tilde{\sigma}}
of the bidirected graph
(\tilde{G},\tilde{\sigma})
, see
(
4.27
). In particular,
q
is semi-Cox-regular by Theorem
4.18
. Note that by construction,
\tilde{G}
has a loop
i\in E(\tilde{G})
at vertex
\alpha\in V(\tilde{G})
if and only if the forbidden thread
\theta^{\alpha}
corresponding to
\alpha
self-crosses at
i
as a vertex in
Q_{0}
, see Definition
3.18
. Note that the latter is equivalent with the existence of a (unique) almost-full cycle
\omega
at
i
in
Q
, cf. Lemmas
3.5
and
3.6
(take
\omega
as the unique subpath of
\theta^{\alpha}
with
\mathbf{s}(\omega)=\mathbf{t}(\omega)=i
). Moreover, by the definition of the bidirection
\tilde{\sigma}
in Definition
3.18
it follows that if
i=\{h,\iota h\}
is a loop in
\tilde{G}
then
\tilde{\sigma}(h)=-\tilde{\sigma}(\iota h)
(resp.
\tilde{\sigma}(h)=\tilde{\sigma}(\iota h)=+1
), that is,
i
is a positive (resp. negative) loop, provided the corresponding almost-full cycle
\omega
has odd (resp. even) length, cf. (
2.9
). Combining these observations with the formula (
4.31
) we get the proof of (
4.14
). In particular,
q
is a unit form if and only if
(Q,I)
has no
almost-full cycles. Moreover, if there is an almost-full cycle of odd length at
i\in Q_{0}
then by the above we have
0=q_{i}=q([S_{i}])
. Since
q
is non-negative then
\mathrm{rad}(q)=\mathcal{R}_{q}(0)=q^{-1}(0)
thus
q_{ij}=q([S_{i}],[S_{j}])=0
for each
j\in Q_{0}
, which means that
q
is disconnected, see Subsection
2.1
. Finally, assume that there is no almost-full cycle of odd length and
2|Q_{0}|-|Q_{1}|\geq 3
. This means that
(\tilde{G},\tilde{\sigma})
has no positive loop and
|V(\tilde{G})|=2|Q_{0}|-|Q_{1}|\geq 3
, cf. (
4.29
) and (
4.28
). This implies that
q
, as the incidence form of
(\tilde{G},\tilde{\sigma})
, is connected by
[
Jiménez González and Mróz, 2026
, Lemma 2.14(c)]
.
This finishes the proof of (d1).

To show (d2)-(d3), assume that
q
is connected. We proceed similarly as in (c2)-(c3). If
q
is unitary then
(\tilde{G},\tilde{\sigma})
has no loops and by Theorem
4.18
(a),
q
has Dynkin type
\mathbb{A}
or
\mathbb{D}
. Moreover,
\mathrm{Dyn}(q)=\mathbb{D}
if and only if
|V(\tilde{G})|\geq 4
and
(\tilde{G},\tilde{\sigma})
is unbalanced. Equivalently, when
{\nabla}_{A}=0
and
r_{A}=2|Q_{0}|-|Q_{1}|\geq 4
by “(a1)
\Leftrightarrow
(a4)” and (
4.10
) shown before. This proves (d2). If
q
is not unitary, then
(\tilde{G},\tilde{\sigma})
has a negative (bidirected) loop, which means it is unbalanced, that is,
{\nabla}_{A}=0
. Now, if
2|Q_{0}|-|Q_{1}|\geq 2
then
r_{A}=|V(\tilde{G})|\geq 2
, so
q
is irreducible by
[
Jiménez González and Mróz, 2026
, Lemma 2.14(b)]
. Thus
\mathrm{Dyn}(q)=\mathbb{C}_{r_{A}}
by Theorem
4.18
(b). In the remaining case
r_{A}=|V(\tilde{G})|=2|Q_{0}|-|Q_{1}|=1
the graph
\tilde{G}
consists of one vertex and
n:=|E(\tilde{G})|
bidirected loops, with
s
two-tail and
n-s
two-head loops for some
0\leq s\leq n
.
Thus, up to renumbering, we have:

\mathrm{Inc}(\tilde{G},\tilde{\sigma})=[2,\ldots,2,-2,\ldots,-2]^{\rm{tr}}\in\mathbb{M}_{n,1}(\mathbb{Z})\quad\text{and}\quad C_{A}^{-{\rm{tr}}}+C_{A}^{-1}=\mathrm{Inc}(\tilde{G},\tilde{\sigma})\mathrm{Inc}(\tilde{G},\tilde{\sigma})^{{\rm{tr}}}=4\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}\!J_{s,s}&\!-J_{s,n-s}\\
\!-J_{n-s,s}&\!J_{n-s,n-s}\end{array}\right]\in\mathbb{M}_{n,n}(\mathbb{Z}),

(4.33)

where
\mathrm{Inc}(\tilde{G},\tilde{\sigma})
has
s
entries
2
and the remaining entries are
-2
, and

J_{t,t^{\prime}}\in\mathbb{M}_{t,t^{\prime}}(\mathbb{Z})
denotes the matrix whose all entries equal 1, cf.(
4.32
). Similarly as in (c3), this means that

\frac{1}{2}q
is a non-negative unit form of rank
1
and corank
n-1
, hence it has Dynkin type
\mathbb{A}_{1}
, cf.
[
Jiménez González and Mróz, 2026
, Example 6.9]
. This finishes the proof of (d3) and of the theorem.

\Box

4.5. Applications

In this subsection we show some consequences of Theorem
4.8
and its proof. We start with the following immediate conclusion which provides a collection of graph theoretic invariants for gentle algebras in the spirit of recent results for Brauer graph algebras
[
Antipov and Zvonareva, 2022
, Theorem 1.2]
and
[
Opper and Zvonareva, 2022
, Theorem A]
.

Corollary 4.34
.

Let
(Q,I)
and
(Q^{\prime},I^{\prime})
be two gentle bound quivers with marked ribbon graphs
(G,\leq)=\triangle(Q,I)
and
(G^{\prime},\leq^{\prime})=\triangle(Q^{\prime},I^{\prime})
. If the gentle algebras
A=kQ/I
and
A^{\prime}=kQ^{\prime}/I^{\prime}
are derived equivalent then:

(a)

G
and
G^{\prime}
have the same number of vertices, edges and faces,

(b)

the multi-sets of lengths and closed degrees of faces
\{(\ell(\omega),\deg^{\circ}(\omega))\mid\ \omega\in\mathrm{Fa}(G,\leq)\}
and
\{(\ell(\omega),\deg^{\circ}(\omega))\mid\ \omega\in\mathrm{Fa}(G^{\prime},\leq^{\prime})\}
coincide,

(c)

either both or none of
G
and
G^{\prime}
are bipartite.

Proof.

Recall that
|E(G)|=|Q_{0}|
hence derived invariance of
|E(G)|
is clear. For
|V(G)|
observe that if
A\cong_{\rm der}A^{\prime}
then by Theorem
4.8
(b) we have
{\nabla}_{A}={\nabla}_{A^{\prime}}
and
|V(G)|-{\nabla}_{A}=\mathrm{rk}(q_{A})=\mathrm{rk}(q_{A^{\prime}})=|V(G)|-{\nabla}_{A^{\prime}}
thus
|V(G)|=|V(G^{\prime})|
. Claim (b) is a direct consequence of Theorem
3.32
and the formula (
3.33
). In particular, (b) implies that
|\mathrm{Fa}(G,\leq)|=|\mathrm{Fa}(G^{\prime},\leq^{\prime})|
. The remaining claim (c) follows from derived invariance of the multi-clock condition (see Theorem
4.8
(b)) and the equivalences in Theorem
4.8
(a).
∎

The following example shows that sometimes we can easily disprove derived equivalences by a quick glance at the quiver and relations.

Example 4.35
.

Consider the three gentle bound quivers
(Q^{(i)},I^{(i)})
,
i=0,1,2
, from Example
3.4
(b). Let
A^{(i)}:=kQ^{(i)}/I^{(i)}
be the corresponding gentle algebras. Observe that all three algebras have the same AAG invariant
\{(4,6)\}
and the Coxeter polynomial
z^{5}-z^{4}-z+1
(cf. Subsection
3.4
and Example
6.19
). However, we easily see that
\nabla_{A^{(0)}}=\nabla_{A^{(2)}}=1
(that is,
A^{(0)}
and
A^{(2)}
satisfy the multi-clock condition) and
\nabla_{A^{(1)}}=0
, thus
A^{(0)}\not\cong_{\rm der}A^{(1)}\not\cong_{\rm der}A^{(2)}
by Theorem
4.8
(b). Recall that Amiot showed that also
A^{(0)}\not\cong_{\rm der}A^{(2)}
by using non-trivial geometric arguments, see
[
Amiot, 2016
, 3.6]
. Moreover, the coranks and Dynkin types of the Euler forms are as follows:
\mathrm{crk}(q_{A^{(0)}})=2=\mathrm{crk}(q_{A^{(2)}})
,
\mathrm{crk}(q_{A^{(1)}})=1
and
\mathrm{Dyn}(q_{A^{(0)}})=\mathbb{A}_{3}=\mathrm{Dyn}(q_{A^{(2)}})
,
\mathrm{Dyn}(q_{A^{(1)}})=\mathbb{D}_{4}
, cf. Theorem
4.8
(c). On the other hand, we verify directly that each of the three marked ribbon graphs
(G^{(i)},\leq^{(i)}):=\triangle(Q^{(i)},I^{(i)})
for
i=1,2,3
, has 4 vertices, 5 edges and 1 face of length 10 and closed degree 2 (cf. example on p.
3.2
). But
G^{(0)}
and
G^{(2)}
are bipartite and
G^{(1)}
is not, cf. Theorem
4.8
(a). This also shows that the collection of graph theoretic parameters in Corollary
4.34
is not a complete derived invariant.

The subsequent conclusion of Theorem
4.8
may be viewed as a refinement of
[
Bobiński et al., 2004
, Theorem C(ii)]
.

Corollary 4.36
.

Let
A=kQ/I
be a 1-cycle gentle algebra
(
of arbitrary
{\rm gl.dim}(A))
. Then
q_{A}
is non-negative of
\mathrm{crk}(q_{A})={\nabla}_{A}\in\{0,1\}
.

Proof.

By Theorem
4.8
,
q_{A}
is non-negative and
\mathrm{crk}(q_{A})=|Q_{1}|-|Q_{0}|+{\nabla}_{A}={\nabla}_{A}
.
∎

Recall that an algebra
A
over an algebraically closed field is
derived discrete
if for each
v=(v_{i})\in\mathbb{N}^{(\mathbb{Z})}
there are only finitely many isoclasses of indecomposable objects in
{\mathcal{D}}^{b}(A)
of a given homology dimension vector
v
, see
Vossieck [2001]
. In particular, if
A
is
derived finite
, that is,
{\mathcal{D}}^{b}(A)
has only finitely many indecomposables up to shift and isomorphism, then
A
is derived discrete.

Corollary 4.37
.

Let
A
be a derived discrete algebra over an algebraically closed field. Then the Euler form
q_{A}
is non-negative of

\mathrm{crk}(q_{A})\in\{0,1\}
. If
A
is derived finite then
q_{A}
is positive.

Proof.

Recall from
Vossieck [2001]

A
is derived discrete if and only if
A
is derived hereditary of Dynkin type, or
A
is a gentle 1-cycle algebra not satisfying the clock condition (see Remark
4.7
). Note that
A
is derived hereditary of Dynkin type if and only if
A
is derived finite. Now the hypothesis follows easily by Corollary
4.36
