and the known fact that the Euler form of a derived hereditary algebra of Dynkin type is positive, cf.
Happel [1988]
. ∎

The classification of Dynkin types of the Euler forms of derived discrete algebras follows by applying Theorem
4.8
(c). In particular, we can recover also some other claims on the Euler form from
Bobiński et al. [2004]
.

Yet another conclusion of Theorem
4.8
(and its proof) provides few nice combinatorial limitations for gentle quivers and the associated graphs.

Corollary 4.38
.

Let
(Q,I)
be a gentle bound quiver and take
\triangle(Q,I)=(G,\leq)
and
\blacktriangleup(Q,I)=(\tilde{G},\tilde{\leq},\tilde{\sigma})

(
the latter in case
{\rm gl.dim}(Q,I)<\infty)
. Then

(a)

{\rm cyc}(Q)={\rm cyc}(G)={\rm cyc}(\tilde{G})
, cf. (
2.5
),

(b)

|Q_{0}|-1\,\leq\,|Q_{1}|\,\leq\,2|Q_{0}|-1
,

(c)

0\,\leq\,{\rm cyc}(Q)\,\leq\,|Q_{0}|
,

(d)

the following four equalities hold simultanously:
|Q_{1}|=2|Q_{0}|-1
,
{\rm cyc}(Q)=|Q_{0}|
,
|V(G)|=1
, and
|V(\tilde{G})|=1
.

Proof.

Claim (a) is a direct consequence of the corank formula (
4.10
) of Theorem
4.8
(b). First inequality in (b) is obvious, since
|Q_{0}|-1=|Q_{1}|
if and only if
Q
is a tree, cf. (
2.5
). To see the second one, recall that
V(G)=2|Q_{0}|-|Q_{1}|
(see (
4.29
)). Thus
1\leq 2|Q_{0}|-|Q_{1}|
, so
|Q_{1}|\,\leq\,2|Q_{0}|-1
. Inequalities in (c) are a simple reformulation of these in (b), cf. (
2.5
). The remaining claim (d) follows from the arguments above and (
4.28
).
∎

Let us consider another interesting class of algebras, playing an important role in modular representation theory of finite groups. Recall that a
Brauer graph
is a triple
\Gamma=(G,\mathrm{p},\mathrm{M})
, where
(G,\mathrm{p})
is a ribbon graph (cf. Subsection
3.2
) and
\mathrm{M}:V(G)\to\mathbb{N}
is a function, called the multiplicity function. We say that
\Gamma
has trivial multiplicities if
\mathrm{M}\equiv 1
. With a Brauer graph
\Gamma
one associates a finite dimensional (bound quiver)
k
-algebra
B=B(\Gamma)
called the
Brauer graph algebra
. We recall that these algebras are symmetric, thus self-injective, so they typically have infinite global dimension, cf.
Schroll [2015]
. We skip the precise definition, for the details as well as some historical remarks we refer to
Schroll [2017]
,
Schroll [2015]
, cf.
Antipov and Zvonareva [2022]
.

Theorem 4.39
.

Let
\Gamma
be a connected Brauer graph with trivial multiplicities and let
B=B(\Gamma)
be the associated Brauer graph algebra over an algebraically closed field
k
. Then the Euler form
q_{B}
is non-negative. Moreover, the following conditions are equivalent:

(a)

The Euler form
q_{B}
is positive.

(b)

The Cartan matrix
C_{B}
of
B
is non-singular.

(c)

\Gamma
is a tree or
\Gamma
is a 1-cycle graph and the unique cycle has odd length.

Proof.

First assume that
\Gamma
consists of a single vertex (resp. a single non-loop edge). Then
B\cong k
(resp.
B\cong k[x]/(x^{2})
), cf.
[
Schroll, 2015
, p. 189]
. In these cases
C_{B}=[1]

(resp.
C_{B}=[2]
) and all the statements hold easily. Hence since now we may assume that
\Gamma
has a vertex of degree at least two as in Definition
3.7
.

By
[
Schroll, 2015
, Theorem 1.3]
there exists a gentle algebra
A
such that
B\cong T(A)=A\lJoin D(A)
is the trivial extension of
A
by the
A
-
A
-bimodule
D(A)={\rm Hom}_{k}(A,k)
. Recall that
T(A)=A\oplus D(A)
as a vector space and the multiplication is given by
(a,f)\cdot(b,g)=(ab,ag+fb)
. In particular,
1_{T(A)}=(1_{A},0)
is the identity of
T(A)
, and if
1_{A}=\varepsilon_{1}+\ldots+\varepsilon_{n}
is a decomposition of
1_{A}
into the sum of primitive orthogonal idempotents then so is
1_{T(A)}=(\varepsilon_{1},0)+\ldots+(\varepsilon_{n},0)
for
1_{T(A)}
. Moreover, for any
1\leq i,j\leq n
we have
(\varepsilon_{j},0)T(A)(\varepsilon_{i},0)\cong\varepsilon_{j}A\varepsilon_{i}\oplus D(\varepsilon_{i}A\varepsilon_{j})
. Thus

(C_{T(A)})_{i,j}=\mathrm{dim}_{k}((\varepsilon_{j},0)T(A)(\varepsilon_{i},0))=\mathrm{dim}_{k}(\varepsilon_{j}A\varepsilon_{i})+\mathrm{dim}_{k}(\varepsilon_{i}A\varepsilon_{j})=(C_{A})_{i,j}+(C_{A})_{j,i}
. This means that

C_{T(A)}=C_{A}+C_{A}^{\rm{tr}}
. In particular, the Euler forms of
T(A)
and
A
are related as follows

q_{T(A)}=2q_{A},

(4.40)

under the identifications
K_{0}({\rm per}\,T(A))\cong\mathbb{Z}^{n}\cong K_{0}({\rm per}\,A)
(via the bases of corresponding indecomposable projectives). Thus
q_{B}
is non-negative by Theorem
4.8
.

Equivalence of (a) and (b): it was shown above that
C_{B}
is a symmetric positive semidefinite (integer) matrix. Therefore, if
C_{B}
is not positive definite then
C_{B}
has eigenvalue 0. This means that
C_{B}
is singular. The opposite implication follows obviously from Sylvester’s criterion.

Equivalence of (a) and (c): if
\Gamma=(G^{\prime},\mathrm{p},\mathrm{M})
with
\mathrm{M}\equiv 1
, then by
[
Schroll, 2015
, Theorems 1.2, 1.3]
we may assume that the gentle algebra
A
with
T(A)\cong B
as above, has the form
A=kQ/I
, where the associated marked ribbon graph
\triangle(Q,I)=(G,\leq)
has its underlying graph
G
isomorphic to
G^{\prime}
; more precisely,
(G,\leq)
arises, up to isomorphism, from
(G^{\prime},\mathrm{p})
by choosing a marking
\mathrm{m}:V(G^{\prime})\to H(G^{\prime})
corresponding to so-called admissible cut, cf. Remark
3.8
and
[
Schroll, 2015
, pp.195-196]
(note that since
\Gamma
has trivial multiplicities then
\Gamma
is unique for the isoclass of
B
, see
[
Antipov and Zvonareva, 2022
, Lemma 3.1]
). In particular,
n=|E(G)|=|E(G^{\prime})|
.
Now observe that
q_{B}
is positive if and only if so is
q_{A}
by (
4.40
). And the latter is positive if and only if

\mathrm{crk}(q_{A})=0
. On the other hand, by Theorem
4.8
(b) we get that
\mathrm{crk}(q_{A})=n-m+{\nabla}_{A}
, where
m=|V(G)|=|V(G^{\prime})|
, so
\mathrm{crk}(q_{A})=0
is equivalent with
n=m-{\nabla}_{A}
.
The latter equality holds precisely when
G^{\prime}
is a tree or
G^{\prime}
is not bipartite 1-cycle graph by Theorem
4.8
(a). Equivalently, (c) holds.
∎

As a consequence we obtain the following analogue of classical results for algebras of finite global dimension interrelating the definiteness of the Euler (or Tits) form with the representation type, cf.
Gabriel [1972]
,
Dlab and Ringel [1976]
,
Brüstle et al. [2011]
. Recall that Brauer graph algebras are of tame representation type. For more details as well as brief definitions of representation types we refer to
Schroll [2017]
.

Corollary 4.41
.

Let
\Gamma
be a connected Brauer graph with trivial multiplicities and let
B=B(\Gamma)
be the associated Brauer graph algebra over an algebraically closed field
k
. Then the Euler form
q_{B}
is positive if and only if
B
is of finite or 1-domestic representation type.

Proof.

It is well-known that
B
is representation-finite if and only if
\Gamma
is a tree, see
[
Schroll, 2017
, Corollary 2.9]
. Moreover, from the characterization of domestic Brauer graph algebras due to Bocian-Skowroński
Bocian and Skowroński [2003]
it follows that, in case of trivial multiplicities,
B
is 1-domestic if and only if
\Gamma
a 1-cycle graph and the unique cycle has odd length, cf.
[
Schroll, 2017
, Theorem 5.1]
. Hence the claim follows by Theorem
4.39
.
∎

Recall that the (non)singularity of the Cartan matrix may reflect certain interesting properties of an algebra, cf.
Białkowski et al. [2008]
,
Bessenrodt and Holm [2008]
.
As an exemplary consequence of Theorem
4.39
we present the following observation.

Corollary 4.42
.

Let
\Gamma
be a connected Brauer graph with trivial multiplicities and let
B=B(\Gamma)
be the associated Brauer graph algebra over an algebraically closed field. Assume that
\Gamma
is a tree or
\Gamma
is a 1-cycle graph and the unique cycle has odd length. Then the Auslander-Reiten quiver of
\mathrm{mod}\,B
does not admit a generalized standard tube.

Proof.

Recall that
B
is a symmetric algebra. So in case the AR quiver of
\mathrm{mod}\,B
contains a generalized standard tube then by
[
Białkowski et al., 2008
, Theorem 2.1]
the Cartan matrix
C_{B}
is singular, a contradiction with Theorem
4.39
(b).
∎

At the final stages of preparing the manuscript, discussions with Bobiński and Zwara pointed us to the following observations.

Remark 4.43
.

(a) Generalization of Theorem
4.39
to Brauer graph algebras with arbitrary multiplicities may be retrieved from the results of Antipov
Antipov [2006]
. Namely, by
[
Antipov, 2006
, Proposition 1]
the Cartan matrix
C_{B}
of the algebra
B=B(\Gamma)
of a Brauer graph
\Gamma=(G,\mathrm{p},\mathrm{M})
(with
G
connected and having at least one edge) decomposes as a sum of certain symmetric matrices, which may be reformulated using our conventions as

C_{B}=\sum_{\alpha\in V(G)}\mathrm{M}(\alpha)c_{\alpha}c_{\alpha}^{\rm{tr}}

(4.44)

where
c_{\alpha}:=\mathrm{Inc}(G)\mathbf{e}_{\alpha}
is the
\alpha
-th column of the incidence matrix
\mathrm{Inc}(G)
of
G
. This shows that
C_{B}
is positive semidefinite (thus the Euler form
q_{B}
is non-negative). Moreover, it follows that the positive definiteness of
C_{B}
does not depend on the multiplicity function
\mathrm{M}
(since, by (
4.44
),
C_{B}
is positive definite if and only if for every
0\neq x\in\mathbb{Z}^{E(G)}
there exists
\alpha\in V(G)
such that
x^{\rm{tr}}c_{\alpha}c_{\alpha}^{\rm{tr}}x>0
). In particular, the equivalence of points (a,b,c) in Theorem
4.39
holds also in the general case. However, since the representation type of a Brauer graph algebra heavily depends on multiplicities, one cannot extend Corollary
4.41
for
B
with non-trivial
\mathrm{M}
. Actually, only one implication of the corollary holds in this case:
if
B
is of finite or 1-domestic representation type, then
q_{B}
is positive
(for instance, if
\Gamma
is a tree with at least three non-trivial multiplicities then
q_{B}
is positive but
B
is not domestic, cf.
Bocian and Skowroński [2003]
,
Schroll [2017]
).
We note that the characterization of Brauer graph algebras having non-singular Cartan matrices, as in Theorem
4.39
(b,c),
may be deduced from
[
Antipov, 2006
, Main Theorem]
, which was proved by different techniques.

(b) Recall that for the algebra
B=B(\Gamma)
of a Brauer graph
\Gamma=(G,\mathrm{p},\mathrm{M})
with
\mathrm{M}\equiv 1
we have
C_{B}=\mathrm{Inc}(G)\mathrm{Inc}(G)^{\rm{tr}}
(cf. Theorem
4.8
and the proof of Theorem
4.39
). The formula (
4.44
) implies that for
B=B(\Gamma)
with arbitrary
\mathrm{M}
we have:

C_{B}=\mathrm{Inc}(G,\mathrm{M})\mathrm{Inc}(G,\mathrm{M})^{\rm{tr}}

(4.45)

where
\mathrm{Inc}(G,\mathrm{M})\in\mathbb{M}_{E(G),V(G)}(\mathbb{Z})
is the matrix defined by
\mathrm{Inc}(G,\mathrm{M})\mathbf{e}_{\alpha}=\sqrt{\mathrm{M}(\alpha)}\mathrm{Inc}(G)\mathbf{e}_{\alpha}
for each
\alpha\in V(G)
. One may view
\mathrm{Inc}(G,\mathrm{M})
as the “weighted” incidence matrix of
(G,\mathrm{M})
. It is interesting to note that
C_{B}
does not depend on the permutation
\mathrm{p}
.

5  Roots and indecomposable complexes

The following main result of this section is a more precise version of Theorem B from the introduction. We use the notation and the terminology of Subsections
2.1
and
3.3
.

Theorem 5.1
.

Let
(Q,I)
be a gentle bound quiver and let
q=q_{A}:K_{0}({\rm per}\,A)\to\mathbb{Z}
denote the Euler form of the gentle algebra
A=kQ/I
. Then the following inclusions hold:

\mathcal{R}_{q}(0)\cup\mathcal{R}_{q}(1)\ \subseteq\ \mathrm{P}(A)\ \subseteq\ \mathcal{R}_{q}(0)\cup\mathcal{R}_{q}(1)\cup\mathcal{R}_{q}(2),

(5.2)

where
\mathrm{P}=\mathrm{P}(A):=[{\rm ind}({\rm per}\,A)]\subseteq K_{0}({\rm per}\,A)
denotes the subset of all the classes of indecomposable perfect complexes in the Grothendieck group
K_{0}({\rm per}\,A)
of all perfect complexes. Moreover, if
(G,\leq)=\triangle(Q,I)
is the associated marked ribbon graph then

(a)

\mathcal{R}_{q}(0)=\{[X^{\bullet}_{m,\omega}]\mid\ m\in\mathbb{Z}\ \text{\,and\,}\,\omega\ \text{is a reduced closed walk in $G$ of even length}\}
,

(b)

\mathcal{R}_{q}(1)=\{[X^{\bullet}_{m,\omega}]\mid\ m\in\mathbb{Z}\ \text{\,and\,}\,\omega\ \text{is a reduced open walk in $G$}\}
,

(c)

\mathrm{P}\cap\mathcal{R}_{q}(2)=\{[X^{\bullet}_{m,\omega}]\mid\ m\in\mathbb{Z}\ \text{\,and\,}\,\omega\ \text{is a reduced closed walk in $G$ of odd length}\}
,

(d)

\mathcal{R}_{q}(0)\cup\mathcal{R}_{q}(1)=\mathrm{P}
if and only if
(Q,I)
satisfies the multi-clock condition,

(e)

[Y^{\bullet}]\in\mathcal{R}_{q}(0)
for every indecomposable band complex
Y^{\bullet}
.

Slightly abusing our conventions, in this section any trivial walk
\omega
is considered as a reduced closed walk of even length and in this case the associated zero complex
X^{\bullet}_{m,\omega}
is treated as an indecomposable string complex. Observe that in contrast to the classical results providing a classification of indecomposable modules in terms of positive roots of the Euler (or Tits) form (cf.
Gabriel [1972]
,
Bongartz [1983]
,
Dlab and Ringel [1976]
) we do not restrict in Theorem
5.1
to “positive” roots since we deal with objects of the derived category. The proof of the theorem will be given in Subsection
5.2
. In the next subsection we discuss some preparatory facts required for the proof.

5.1. Incidence vectors

In
[
Jiménez González and Mróz, 2026
, Section 6]
we studied so-called incidence vectors associated to walks in an arbitrary bidirected graph. Here we recall the special case of this construction, for walks in a graph
G
(viewed as the bidirected graph
(G,\sigma^{+})
with constant bidirection
\sigma^{+}\equiv+1
, cf. Definition
2.7
).

Definition 5.3
.

Given a graph
G
and a walk
\omega=i_{1}\cdots i_{L}i_{L+1}
of
G
, for
L\geq 0
and
i_{t}\in\vec{E}(G)
,
t=1,\ldots,L+1
, we set

\underline{{\mathrm{inc}}}(\omega)=\underline{{\mathrm{inc}}}_{G}(\omega):=\sum_{t=0}^{L}(-1)^{t}\mathbf{e}_{i_{t+1}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{E(G)}

(5.4)

where
\mathbf{e}_{i}:=\mathbf{e}_{\overline{i}}
for
i\in\vec{E}(G)
, and we call
\underline{{\mathrm{inc}}}(\omega)
the
incidence vector
of
\omega
. For a trivial walk
\omega
we set
\underline{{\mathrm{inc}}}(\omega):=0
.

We recall from
[
Jiménez González and Mróz, 2026
, Lemmas 6.2-6.3]
the following basic properties of incidence vectors.

Lemma 5.5
.

Let
G
be a graph and
\omega
,
\omega^{\prime}
a pair of concatenable walks in
G
. Then

(a)

\underline{{\mathrm{inc}}}(\omega\omega^{\prime})=\underline{{\mathrm{inc}}}(\omega)+(-1)^{\ell(\omega)}\underline{{\mathrm{inc}}}(\omega^{\prime})
.

(b)

\underline{{\mathrm{inc}}}({\omega}^{-1})=(-1)^{\ell(\omega)+1}\underline{{\mathrm{inc}}}(\omega)
,

(c)

if
\omega
is a closed walk of even length, then
\underline{{\mathrm{inc}}}(\omega^{k})=k\,\underline{{\mathrm{inc}}}(\omega)
for each
k\in\mathbb{Z}
,

(d)

if
\omega
is a closed walk of odd length, then
\underline{{\mathrm{inc}}}(\omega^{k})=(k\,{\rm mod}\,2)\underline{{\mathrm{inc}}}(\omega)
for each
k\in\mathbb{Z}
,

(e)

\mathrm{Inc}(G)^{{\rm{tr}}}\underline{{\mathrm{inc}}}(\omega)=\mathbf{e}_{\mathbf{t}(\omega)}-(-1)^{\ell(\omega)}\mathbf{e}_{\mathbf{s}(\omega)}\in\mathbb{Z}^{V(G)}
, where
\mathrm{Inc}(G)\in\mathbb{M}_{E(G),V(G)}(\mathbb{Z})
is the incidence matrix of
G
as in Definition
2.7
.

The following one of the main results of
Jiménez González and Mróz [2026]
is crucial in our study.

Theorem 5.6

(
[
Jiménez González and Mróz, 2026
, Theorem C]
)
.

Given a connected graph
G
, the set of vectors

\mathcal{R}^{\rm inc}_{G}:=\{\pm\underline{{\mathrm{inc}}}(\omega)\mid\ \omega\text{ a walk of }G\}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}^{E(G)}

(5.7)

satisfies

\mathcal{R}_{q}(0)\cup\mathcal{R}_{q}(1)\ \subseteq\ \mathcal{R}^{\rm inc}_{G}\ \subseteq\ \mathcal{R}_{q}(0)\cup\mathcal{R}_{q}(1)\cup\mathcal{R}_{q}(2),

(5.8)

where
q=q_{G}=q_{G,\sigma^{+}}:\mathbb{Z}^{E(G)}\to\mathbb{Z}
is the incidence form of
G
as in Definition
4.15
. Moreover,

(a)

\mathcal{R}_{q}(0)=\{\pm\underline{{\mathrm{inc}}}(\omega)\mid\ \,\omega\ \text{is a closed walk of even length}\}
,

(b)

\mathcal{R}_{q}(1)=\{\pm\underline{{\mathrm{inc}}}(\omega)\mid\ \,\omega\ \text{is an open walk}\}
,

(c)

\mathcal{R}^{\rm inc}_{G}\cap\mathcal{R}_{q}(2)=\{\pm\underline{{\mathrm{inc}}}(\omega)\mid\ \,\omega\ \text{is a closed walk of odd length}\}
.

Vectors from
\mathcal{R}^{\rm inc}_{G}
are called
incidence roots
of
G
(or of
q_{G}
). Observe that by Lemma
5.5
(a)-(b) we may restrict to reduced walks in the definition of
\mathcal{R}^{\rm inc}_{G}
, that is,

\mathcal{R}^{\rm inc}_{G}=\{\pm\underline{{\mathrm{inc}}}(\omega)\mid\ \omega\text{ a reduced walk of }G\}
, and similarly for the sets in claims (a)-(c) of Theorem
5.6
, cf.
[
Jiménez González and Mróz, 2026
, Remark 7.1]
. The following observations provide a bridge from incidence roots to the study of the present paper. We use the notation and terminology of Subsections
3.3
and
4.1
.

Lemma 5.9
.

Let
(Q,I)
be a gentle bound quiver and
(G,\leq)=\triangle(Q,I)
the associated marked ribbon graph. Then

(a)

\underline{\mathrm{dim}}^{\rm p}(X^{\bullet}_{m,\omega})=(-1)^{m}\underline{{\mathrm{inc}}}_{G}(\omega)
for each
m\in\mathbb{Z}
and a non-trivial reduced walk
\omega
of
G
,

(b)

\underline{\mathrm{dim}}^{\rm p}(X^{\bullet}_{m,\omega,\mu})=(-1)^{m}d\,\underline{{\mathrm{inc}}}_{G}(\omega_{[\ell(\omega)-1]})
for each
m\in\mathbb{Z}
, a belt
\omega
of
(G,\leq)
and an indecomposable automorphism
\mu
of a
k
-vector space
K
of dimension
d={\rm dim}_{k}(K)\geq 1
.

Proof.

We show (a) by induction on
L\geq 0
, for reduced walks
\omega=i_{1}\cdots i_{L}i_{L+1}
. If
L=0
then
\omega=i_{1}
and

X^{\bullet}_{m,\omega}=\ldots 0\to P_{i_{1}}\to 0\ldots
is a stalk complex concentrated in cohomological degree
m
, so
\underline{\mathrm{dim}}^{\rm p}(X^{\bullet}_{m,\omega})=(-1)^{m}\mathbf{e}_{i_{1}}
, cf. the formula (
4.1
). Since
\underline{{\mathrm{inc}}}(i_{1})=\mathbf{e}_{i_{1}}
we get the claim. Now, given a reduced walk
\omega^{\prime}=i_{1}\cdots i_{L}i_{L+1}i_{L+2}
, by construction in Subsection
3.3
it is clear that
\underline{\mathrm{dim}}^{\rm p}(X^{\bullet}_{m,\omega^{\prime}})=\underline{\mathrm{dim}}^{\rm p}(X^{\bullet}_{m,\omega})+(-1)^{s+1}\mathbf{e}_{i_{L+2}}
, independently on the degree
\deg(i_{L+1}i_{L+2})
, where
\omega=i_{1}\cdots i_{L}i_{L+1}
and
s
is the cohomological degree of the summand
P_{i_{L+1}}
in
X^{\bullet}_{m,\omega}
, cf. (
4.1
). Thus by the inductive assumption we get that
(-1)^{s}=(-1)^{m+L}
and

\underline{\mathrm{dim}}^{\rm p}(X^{\bullet}_{m,\omega^{\prime}})=(-1)^{m}\underline{{\mathrm{inc}}}(\omega)+(-1)^{m+L+1}\mathbf{e}_{i_{L+2}}=(-1)^{m}\left(\sum_{t=0}^{L}(-1)^{t}\mathbf{e}_{i_{t+1}}+(-1)^{L+1}\mathbf{e}_{i_{L+2}}\right)=(-1)^{m}\underline{{\mathrm{inc}}}(\omega^{\prime}),

which shows (a). Now take
m\in\mathbb{Z}
, a belt
\omega
and an automorphism
\mu:K\to K
as in (b). Then directly from Proposition
3.21
it follows that
\underline{\mathrm{dim}}^{\rm p}(X^{\bullet}_{m,\omega,\mu})=d\,\underline{\mathrm{dim}}^{\rm p}(X^{\bullet}_{m,\omega_{[\ell(\omega)-1]}})
for
d={\rm dim}_{k}(K)
. Thus by (a) we get
\underline{\mathrm{dim}}^{\rm p}(X^{\bullet}_{m,\omega,\mu})=(-1)^{m}d\,\underline{{\mathrm{inc}}}_{G}(\omega_{[\ell(\omega)-1]})
.
∎

5.2.
Proof of Theorem
5.1

Fix a gentle bound quiver
(Q,I)
, and let
(G,\leq)=\triangle(Q,I)
be the associated marked ribbon graph and
q=q_{A}:K_{0}({\rm per}\,A)\to\mathbb{Z}
, the Euler form of the gentle algebra
A:=kQ/I
. Recall that by Theorem
4.8
the form
q_{A}
in the basis of indecomposable projectives
P:=\{[P_{i}]\}_{i\in Q_{0}}
is the incidence form
q_{G}:\mathbb{Z}^{E(G)}\to\mathbb{Z}
of
G
(see Definition
4.15
). In particular,

q_{A}([X^{\bullet}])=q_{G}(\underline{\mathrm{dim}}^{\rm p}(X^{\bullet}))

(5.10)

for every perfect complex
X^{\bullet}
(the set of edges
E(G)
of
G
is identified with the set of vertices
Q_{0}
of
Q
).
By Proposition
3.21
the set
\mathrm{P}=\mathrm{P}(A)=[{\rm ind}({\rm per}\,A)]\subseteq K_{0}({\rm per}\,A)
is the sum

\mathrm{P}=\mathrm{P}_{\rm st}\cup\mathrm{P}_{\rm bd}\quad\text{for}\quad\begin{array}[]{l}\mathrm{P}_{\rm st}:=\{[X^{\bullet}_{m,\omega}]\mid\ m\in\mathbb{Z}\ \text{\,and\,}\,\omega\ \text{is a reduced walk in $G$}\,\},\\
\mathrm{P}_{\rm bd}:=\{[X^{\bullet}_{m,\omega,\mu}]\mid\ m\in\mathbb{Z},\,\omega\ \text{is
a belt of $(G,\leq)$ and $\mu$ is an indecomposable}\\
\,\hskip 71.13188pt\text{ automorphism of a finite dimensional $k$-vector space $K$}\},\end{array}

(5.11)

cf. (
3.24
). By transferring the inclusions (
5.8
) of Theorem
5.6
via (
5.10
) and Lemma
5.9
(a) we obtain

\mathcal{R}_{q_{A}}(0)\cup\mathcal{R}_{q_{A}}(1)\ \subseteq\ \mathrm{P}_{\rm st}\ \subseteq\ \mathcal{R}_{q_{A}}(0)\cup\mathcal{R}_{q_{A}}(1)\cup\mathcal{R}_{q_{A}}(2),

(5.12)

and in the same way the equalities (a) and (b) of Theorem
5.1
follow by Theorem
5.6
(a)-(b) (note that
[X^{\bullet}_{m+1,\omega}]=-[X^{\bullet}_{m,\omega}]
).
To show the remaining claims take an indecomposable band complex
Y^{\bullet}
. By Proposition
3.21
there exists
m\in\mathbb{Z}
,
a belt
\omega=i_{1}i_{2}\cdots i_{L}i_{L+1}
of
(G,\leq)
and an indecomposable automorphism
\mu
of a finite dimensional
k
-vector space
K
such that
Y^{\bullet}\cong X^{\bullet}_{m,\omega,\mu}
. By the definition of
a belt it follows that
\omega_{1}:=\omega_{[L]}=i_{1}i_{2}\cdots i_{L}
is a closed walk and
\deg(\omega)=0
. The latter condition implies that
L=\ell(\omega_{1})>0
is even. Hence by Lemma
5.9
and Lemma
5.5
(c) we get that

\underline{\mathrm{dim}}^{\rm p}(Y^{\bullet})=\underline{\mathrm{dim}}^{\rm p}(X^{\bullet}_{m,\omega,\mu})=(-1)^{m}d\,\underline{{\mathrm{inc}}}_{G}(\omega_{1})=(-1)^{m}\,\underline{{\mathrm{inc}}}_{G}(\omega_{1}^{d})=\underline{\mathrm{dim}}^{\rm p}(X^{\bullet}_{m,\omega_{1}^{d}})

(5.13)

where
d={\rm dim}_{k}(K)
. In particular,
[Y^{\bullet}]=[X^{\bullet}_{m,\omega_{1}^{d}}]
belongs to
\mathcal{R}_{q_{A}}(0)
by (a), since
\omega_{1}^{d}
is a closed walk of even length (note that
\omega_{1}^{d}
is reduced since
i_{L}i_{1}=i_{L}i_{L+1}
is reduced by the definition of a belt). This shows claim (e). This also shows that
\mathrm{P}_{\rm bd}\subseteq\mathrm{P}_{\rm st}
hence
\mathrm{P}=\mathrm{P}_{\rm st}
. Thus by applying (
5.12
) we obtain the inclusions (
5.2
). Similarly, we get claim (c) by applying Theorem
5.6
(c) via Lemma
5.9
(a). To see the remaining claim (d) observe that by (
5.2
), the equality
\mathcal{R}_{q}(0)\cup\mathcal{R}_{q}(1)=\mathrm{P}
is equivalent with
\mathrm{P}\cap\mathcal{R}_{q}(2)=\emptyset
. By (c), the latter holds exactly if
G
has no closed walks of odd length, that is, if
G
is bipartite. And this holds if and only if
(Q,I)
satisfies the multi-clock condition by Theorem
4.8
(a).
\Box

5.3. Root systems and applications

In this subsection we show some exemplary applications and consequences of Theorem
5.1
.

Corollary 5.14
.

Let
(Q,I)
be a gentle bound quiver and let
q=q_{A}:K_{0}({\rm per}\,A)\to\mathbb{Z}
be the Euler form of the gentle algebra
A=kQ/I
. Let
\mathrm{P}=\mathrm{P}(A)=[{\rm ind}({\rm per}\,A)]\subseteq K_{0}({\rm per}\,A)
be the subset as in Theorem
5.1
.
Then for any
x\in\mathrm{P}\cap\mathcal{R}_{q}(2)
there exist infinitely many
(
up to shift and isomorphism
)
indecomposable string complexes
X^{\bullet}
in
{\mathcal{D}}^{b}(A)
such that
[X^{\bullet}]=x
. In particular, if
(Q,I)
does not satisfy the multi-clock condition, then
A
is of derived infinite type.

Proof.

Take
x\in\mathrm{P}\cap\mathcal{R}_{q}(2)
. By Theorem
5.1
(c) there exists
m\in\mathbb{Z}
and a reduced closed walk
\omega
in
G
of odd length such that
x=[X^{\bullet}_{m,\omega}]
, where
(G,\leq)=\triangle(Q,I)
. So by Lemma
5.9
(a) and Lemma
5.5
(d) for every odd
t\in\mathbb{Z}
we have

\underline{\mathrm{dim}}^{\rm p}(X^{\bullet}_{m,\omega^{t}})=(-1)^{m}\underline{{\mathrm{inc}}}_{G}(\omega^{t})=(-1)^{m}\underline{{\mathrm{inc}}}_{G}(\omega)=\underline{\mathrm{dim}}^{\rm p}(X^{\bullet}_{m,\omega}),

thus
[X^{\bullet}_{m,\omega^{t}}]=[X^{\bullet}_{m,\omega}]=x
. Observe that the string complexes
\{X^{\bullet}_{m,\omega^{t}}\}_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
are pairwise not isomorphic (even up to shift), see (
3.25
), cf.
Bekkert and Merklen [2003]
. Thus the first assertion holds. Now, if
(Q,I)
does not satisfy the multi-clock condition then by Theorem
5.1
(d) the set
\mathrm{P}\cap\mathcal{R}_{q}(2)
is non-empty. And by the arguments above, every
x\in\mathrm{P}\cap\mathcal{R}_{q}(2)
induces an infinite family of pairwise not isomorphic (up to shift) indecomposable complexes in
{\mathcal{D}}^{b}(A)
.
∎

Example 5.15
.

Let
(Q,I)
be a gentle bound quiver satisfying the equivalent properties of Corollary
4.38
(d). In particular, the associated marked ribbon graph
(G,\leq)=\triangle(Q,I)
has one vertex and
n:=|Q_{0}|=|E(G)|\geq 1
loops, and
(Q,I)
does not satisfy the multi-clock condition. Observe that the condition
|V(G)|=1
is precisely the case when the Euler form
q=q_{A}:K_{0}({\rm per}\,A)\to\mathbb{Z}
of
A=kQ/I
is not irreducible, and in this case
\mathrm{Dyn}(\frac{1}{2}q)=\mathbb{A}_{1}
, see Theorem
4.8
(c), cf. (
4.32
). Therefore the set
\mathcal{R}_{q}(1)\subseteq K_{0}({\rm per}\,A)
of
1
-roots of
q
is empty. Alternatively, this follows by Theorem
5.1
(b) since there are no open walks in
G
. Moreover, the sets
\mathcal{R}_{q}(0)
and
\mathrm{P}(A)\cap\mathcal{R}_{q}(2)
are non-empty by Theorem
5.1
(a,c).

Let us consider the case
n=1
in more detail. In this case
(Q,I)
is the one loop gentle bound quiver
1
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{1}}
of Example
3.10
,
A=kQ/I
is the algebra of “dual numbers” (cf.
Vossieck [2001]
), and
G={\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 29.07803pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-6.1985pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{{\alpha}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}{{}{{}{{}{{}}{}{{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}{{}}}}}}}}}{}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}{}}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}{{}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-29.07803pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.25555pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{1}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}{{}}{}{{}{{}}{}{{}}}}}{}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}{}\lx@xy@spline@}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}{}}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces}
. Note that the Grothendieck group
K_{0}({\rm per}\,A)
has rank 1 and it is generated by the class
[P_{1}]
of the only indecomposable projective
A
-module
P_{1}=A
.
Moreover, every non-trivial reduced walk of
G
is the
\ell
-fold concatenation
1^{\ell}=11\ldots 1
or
1^{-\ell}:=(1^{-1})^{\ell}=1^{-1}1^{-1}\ldots 1^{-1}
, for
\ell\geq 1
(here without loss of generality we treat the loop
1
as an ordered edge such that
\deg(11)=1
, cf. Subsection
3.3
). Observe that
\deg(1^{\ell})=\ell-1
and
\deg(1^{-\ell})=1-\ell
for every
\ell\geq 1
, hence
G
has no belts. Therefore by Proposition
3.21
, up to isomorphism every (non-zero) indecomposable perfect complex is the string complex
X^{\bullet}_{m,1^{\ell}}=X^{\bullet}_{m+l-1,1^{-\ell}}
of the form

\textstyle{...\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{P_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{1}}
\textstyle{P_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{1}}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{1}}
\textstyle{P_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{...}

with
\ell
copies of
P_{1}
starting at cohomological degree
m
, for some
m\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\ell\geq 1
. To get the non-perfect complexes observe that the walk
1
is a full face and
11
is the unique primitive left resolvable walk of
(G,\leq)
. Thus by Proposition
3.37
every indecomposable non-perfect complex has the form
X^{\bullet}_{m,-,11}
which is the shifted (infinite) projective resolution of the simple module
S_{1}
. In this way we easily recovered the known
classification of indecomposable objects in
{\mathcal{D}}^{b}(A)
, cf.
Vossieck [2001]
, see also Example
6.10
. Moreover, since
1^{\ell}
is a closed walk, by Theorem
5.1
(a,c) we infer that
[X^{\bullet}_{m,1^{\ell}}]=0\in\mathcal{R}_{q}(0)
if
\ell
is even, and
[X^{\bullet}_{m,1^{\ell}}]=(-1)^{m}[P_{1}]\in\mathcal{R}_{q}(2)
if
\ell
is odd, cf. (
4.1
). Note that here
\mathcal{R}_{q}(2)=\{[P_{1}],-[P_{1}]\}
since the Cartan matrix has the form
C_{A}=[2]\in\mathbb{M}_{1,1}(\mathbb{Z})
. Thus
\mathrm{P}(A)=\mathcal{R}_{q}(0)\cup\mathcal{R}_{q}(1)\cup\mathcal{R}_{q}(2)=\mathcal{R}_{q}(0)\cup\mathcal{R}_{q}(2)=\{0,[P_{1}],-[P_{1}]\}
, cf. (
5.2
).

In the theorem below we show that in case the Euler form of a gentle algebra
A
is positive, the set
\mathrm{P}=[{\rm ind}({\rm per}\,A)]
is finite and it carries an additional structure of a root system in the sense of Bourbaki
Bourbaki [1968]
. Recall that a root system is a finite system of vectors in a Euclidean space satisfying axioms defined in
[
Humphreys, 1980
, III.9]
. Root systems are classified via their
Cartan-Dynkin types

\mathbb{A}_{n},\mathbb{B}_{n},\mathbb{C}_{n},\mathbb{D}_{n},\mathbb{E}_{6},\mathbb{E}_{7},\mathbb{E}_{8},

\mathbb{F}_{4},\mathbb{G}_{2}
, see
[
Humphreys, 1980
, Chapter III]
for the details. Recall also that this classification played a fundamental role in the well-known classification of complex semisimple Lie algebras, see
Bourbaki [1968]
,
Humphreys [1980]
.

Theorem 5.16
.

Let
(Q,I)
be a gentle bound quiver and let
q=q_{A}:K_{0}({\rm per}\,A)\to\mathbb{Z}
be the Euler form of the gentle algebra
A=kQ/I
. Let
n:=|Q_{0}|\geq 1
and let
\mathrm{P}=\mathrm{P}(A)=[{\rm ind}({\rm per}\,A)]\subseteq K_{0}({\rm per}\,A)
be the subset as in Theorem
5.1
.
If
q_{A}
is positive then the set
\mathrm{P}^{\star}:=\mathrm{P}\setminus\{0\}
is an irreducible
(
finite
)
root system in the Euclidean space
(K_{0}({\rm per}\,A)\otimes\mathbb{R},q(-,-))
. Moreover,

(a)

if
(Q,I)
satisfies the multi-clock condition then the root system
\mathrm{P}^{\star}
has
n^{2}+n
elements and its Cartan-Dynkin type is
\mathbb{A}_{n}
,

(b)

if
(Q,I)
does not satisfy the multi-clock condition then the root system
\mathrm{P}^{\star}
has
2n^{2}
elements and its Cartan-Dynkin type is
\mathbb{C}_{n}

(
we set
\mathbb{C}_{1}:=\mathbb{A}_{1}
here
)
;
moreover, the set
\mathcal{R}_{q}(1)\subsetneq\mathrm{P}^{\star}
has
2(n^{2}-n)
elements and for
n\geq 4
it is an irreducible root system of the Cartan-Dynkin type
\mathbb{D}_{n}
.

Proof.

Let
(G,\leq)=\triangle(Q,I)
be the associated marked ribbon graph. Assume that
q_{A}
is positive, equivalently, that
\mathrm{crk}(q_{A})=0
. Recall that
\mathrm{crk}(q_{A})=|E(G)|-|V(G)|+{\nabla}_{A}\ =\ |Q_{1}|-|Q_{0}|+{\nabla}_{A}
and
|E(G)|=|Q_{0}|
, see Theorem
4.8
(b) and (
4.28
). Thus positivity of
q_{A}
implies that one of the following conditions holds (cf. the arguments in the proof of Theorem
4.39
):

(i)

G
is a tree (equivalently,
Q
is a tree),

(ii)

G
is not bipartite 1-cycle graph (equivalently,
(Q,I)
is 1-cycle not satisfying the multi-clock condition),

cf. Theorem
4.8
(a) and Corollary
4.38
(a).

Assume first that
n=|E(G)|=1
. Since by our conventions
G
has a vertex of degree at least two (see Definitions
3.7
and
3.9
), the assumption
n=1
implies that the case (ii) holds and
G
consists of one vertex and one loop. Thus
(Q,I)
is a one loop gentle bound quiver. As we showed in Example
5.15
, in this case
\mathcal{R}_{q_{A}}(1)=\emptyset
and
\mathrm{P}^{\star}=\mathcal{R}_{q_{A}}(2)=\{[P_{1}],-[P_{1}]\}\subseteq K_{0}({\rm per}\,A)\cong\mathbb{Z}[P_{1}]
hence
\mathrm{P}^{\star}
is an irreducible root system in
K_{0}({\rm per}\,A)\otimes\mathbb{R}\cong\mathbb{R}
of Cartan-Dynkin type
\mathbb{A}_{1}(=\mathbb{C}_{1})
, see
[
Humphreys, 1980
, 9.3]
.

Now assume that
n=|E(G)|\geq 2
. For this case we may apply
[
Jiménez González and Mróz, 2026
, Proposition 7.15 and Remark 7.16]
, where by a direct verification of the root system axioms and a careful counting of walks in
G
it was shown that the incidence roots of the incidence form
q_{G}
of
G
satisfy the following properties.

•

In both cases (i) and (ii), the set
\mathcal{R}^{\star}:=\mathcal{R}^{\rm inc}_{G}\setminus\{0\}
is an irreducible root system in the Euclidean space
(\mathbb{R}^{n},q_{G}(-,-))
.

•

In case (i) we have
|\mathcal{R}^{\star}|=n^{2}+n
and
\mathrm{CarDyn}(\mathcal{R}^{\star})=\mathbb{A}_{n}
.

•

In case (ii) we have
|\mathcal{R}^{\star}|=2n^{2}
and
\mathrm{CarDyn}(\mathcal{R}^{\star})=\mathbb{C}_{n}
.

•

In case (ii) we have
|\mathcal{R}_{q_{G}}(1)|=2(n^{2}-n)
and
\mathcal{R}_{q_{G}}(1)\subsetneq\mathcal{R}^{\star}
. Moreover, the set
\mathcal{R}_{q_{G}}(1)
is an irreducible root system with
\mathrm{CarDyn}(\mathcal{R}_{q_{G}}(1))=\mathbb{D}_{n}
, provided
n\geq 4
.

(Here
\mathrm{CarDyn}(-)
denotes the Cartan-Dynkin type of a root system.) Now recall that by Theorem
4.8
the Euler form
q_{A}
in the basis of indecomposable projectives
P:=\{[P_{i}]\}_{i\in Q_{0}}
is the incidence form
q_{G}:\mathbb{Z}^{E(G)}\to\mathbb{Z}
of
G
, and the set
\mathrm{P}=\mathrm{P}_{\rm st}\subseteq K_{0}({\rm per}\,A)
corresponds bijectively via

\underline{\mathrm{dim}}^{\rm p}(-)
to the set
\mathcal{R}^{\rm inc}_{G}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}^{n}
(see Lemma
5.9
(a) and the proof of Theorem
5.1
). Thus the properties of the root systems
\mathcal{R}^{\star}
and
\mathcal{R}_{q_{G}}(1)
listed above transfer to the sets
\mathrm{P}^{\star}
and
\mathcal{R}_{q_{A}}(1)
, respectively, and in this way we get the claims (a) and (b).
∎

Remark 5.17
.

Let
(Q,I)
be a gentle bound quiver
(Q,I)
with positive Euler form
q=q_{A}
.

(a) Since by Theorem
5.16
, the set
\mathrm{P}^{\star}\subseteq K_{0}({\rm per}\,A)
is an irreducible root system, there is a simple complex Lie algebra having
\mathrm{P}^{\star}
as its root system, cf.
[
Humphreys, 1980
, 18.4]
. It is interesting to ask what happens if
q
is not positive. In this case the set
\mathrm{P}^{\star}
is infinite and it seems to us that (at least in some cases)
\mathrm{P}^{\star}
or its subsets may be viewed as infinite root systems like these related to Kac-Moody Lie algebras, cf.
Kac [1990]
,
Dlab and Ringel [1976]
,
Kac [1980]
, compare also with recent results on Lie algebra structures related with gentle algebras in
Chaparro et al. [2020]
,
Chen and Yang [2023]
.

(b) If
n=|Q_{0}|\geq 4
and
(Q,I)
does not satisfy the multi-clock condition, then by Theorem
5.16
(b) there are two root systems:
\mathcal{R}_{q}(1)\subsetneq\mathrm{P}^{\star}
and
\mathrm{P}^{\star}
in
(K_{0}({\rm per}\,A)\otimes\mathbb{R},q(-,-))
of Cartan-Dynkin types
\mathbb{D}_{n}
and
\mathbb{C}_{n}
, respectively. Observe that the set
\mathrm{P}^{\prime}:=\mathrm{P}^{\star}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{q}(1)
has the cardinality
2n
and it is contained in
\mathcal{R}_{q}(2)
(cf. Theorem
5.1
). Thus, using the terminology of
Humphreys [1980]
, the elements of
\mathcal{R}_{q}(1)
(resp. of
\mathrm{P}^{\prime}
) are “short” (resp. “long”) roots in the root system
\mathrm{P}^{\star}
. For more detailed discussion on root systems induced by positive Cox-regular quadratic forms (of arbitrary Dynkin type) and their interrelations with Weyl groups and Weyl roots we refer to
Mróz and Zając [2022]
, cf.
Roiter [1978]
.

6  Auslander-Reiten triangles and the Coxeter transformation

Let
A
be a finite dimensional
k
-algebra with bounded derived category
{\mathcal{D}}^{b}(A)
. It was shown in
Happel [1991]
that if
Z^{\bullet}
is an indecomposable complex in
{\mathcal{D}}^{b}(A)
then there is an Auslander-Reiten triangle
X^{\bullet}\to Y^{\bullet}\to Z^{\bullet}\to X^{\bullet}[1]
in
{\mathcal{D}}^{b}(A)
if and only if
Z^{\bullet}
is a perfect complex, in which case
X^{\bullet}
is indecomposable and the triangle is unique up to isomorphism, see also
Happel [1988]
. Moreover, if
A
is a Gorenstein algebra, then the whole triangle is Auslander-Reiten in the category
{\rm per}\,A
, see, for instance,
Happel et al. [2008]
. Since gentle algebras are Gorenstein (see
Geiss and Reiten [2005]
and
Amiot et al. [2023]
), using Proposition
3.21
and following
Bobiński [2011]
we produce all Auslander-Reiten triangles in the derived category of a gentle algebra in terms of walks of the associated marked ribbon graph (Proposition
6.4
and Remark
6.8
). Afterwards, we apply this construction to describe the Coxeter transformation (Corollary
6.16
) and the Coxeter polynomial of a gentle algebra (Theorem
6.17
, which is Theorem C from the introduction).

The main construction of Auslander-Reiten triangles involves the concatenation of walks followed by an appropriate “reduction”, which we briefly analyze below.

6.1. Reduced concatenation

For any concatenable walks
\omega_{1},\omega_{2}
of a graph
G
there is a maximal
\ell\geq 0
such that
\omega_{1}=\omega^{\prime}_{1}\omega
and
\omega_{2}={\omega}^{-1}\omega^{\prime\prime}_{2}
for a walk
\omega
of length
\ell
. Note that
\mathbf{s}(\omega^{\prime}_{1})=\mathbf{t}(\omega)=\mathbf{s}({\omega}^{-1})=\mathbf{t}(\omega^{\prime\prime}_{2})
, and that if
\omega_{1}
and
\omega_{2}
are reduced walks then
\omega^{\prime}_{1}\omega^{\prime\prime}_{2}
is reduced by the maximality of
\ell
. In this situation, in what follows we use the notation,

\ell(\omega_{1},\omega_{2}):=\ell\qquad\text{and}\qquad\omega_{1}\cdot\omega_{2}:=\omega^{\prime}_{1}\omega^{\prime\prime}_{2},

and call
\omega_{1}\cdot\omega_{2}
the
reduced concatenation
of the (reduced) walks
\omega_{1}
and
\omega_{2}
.

Lemma 6.1
.

If
\omega_{1}
,
\omega_{2}
,
\omega_{3}
are concatenable reduced walks of a graph
G
then
\omega_{1}\cdot(\omega_{2}\cdot\omega_{3})=(\omega_{1}\cdot\omega_{2})\cdot\omega_{3}
.

Proof.

We distinguish three cases taking
\ell:=\ell(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})
and
\ell^{\prime}:=\ell(\omega_{2},\omega_{3})
:

Case 1.
Assume that
\ell+\ell^{\prime}<\ell(\omega_{2})
and take the following decompositions with
\ell=\ell(\omega^{\prime\prime}_{1})=\ell(\omega^{\prime}_{2})
and
\ell^{\prime}=\ell(\omega^{\prime\prime\prime}_{2})=\ell(\omega^{\prime}_{3})
,

\textstyle{{\Bigl(}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\omega_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\omega^{\prime}_{1}}
\textstyle{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}[}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\omega^{\prime\prime}_{1}}
\textstyle{{\Bigr)\Bigl(}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\omega_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\omega^{\prime}_{2}}
\textstyle{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}]}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\omega^{\prime\prime}_{2}}
\textstyle{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\omega^{\prime\prime\prime}_{2}}
\textstyle{{\Bigr)\Bigl(}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\omega_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\omega^{\prime}_{3}}
\textstyle{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0})}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\omega^{\prime\prime}_{3}}
\textstyle{{\Bigr)}.}

Then
\omega_{1}\cdot(\omega_{2}\cdot\omega_{3})=(\omega^{\prime}_{1}\omega^{\prime\prime}_{1})\cdot(\omega^{\prime}_{2}\omega^{\prime\prime}_{2}\omega^{\prime\prime}_{3})=(\omega^{\prime}_{1}\omega^{\prime\prime}_{2}\omega^{\prime\prime}_{3})
and
(\omega_{1}\cdot\omega_{2})\cdot\omega_{3}=(\omega^{\prime}_{1}\omega^{\prime\prime}_{2}\omega^{\prime\prime\prime}_{2})\cdot(\omega^{\prime}_{3}\omega^{\prime\prime}_{3})=(\omega^{\prime}_{1}\omega^{\prime\prime}_{2}\omega^{\prime\prime}_{3})
.

Case 2.
Assume that
\ell+\ell^{\prime}=\ell(\omega_{2})
and take the following decompositions with
\ell=\ell(\omega^{\prime\prime\prime}_{1})=\ell(\omega^{\prime}_{2})
and
\ell^{\prime}=\ell(\omega^{\prime\prime}_{2})=\ell(\omega^{\prime}_{3})
,

\textstyle{{\Bigl(}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\omega_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\omega^{\prime}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\omega^{\prime\prime}_{1}}
\textstyle{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}[}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\omega^{\prime\prime\prime}_{1}}
\textstyle{{\Bigr)\Bigl(}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\omega_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\omega^{\prime}_{2}}
\textstyle{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}](}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\omega^{\prime\prime}_{2}}
\textstyle{{\Bigr)\Bigl(}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\omega_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\omega^{\prime}_{3}}
\textstyle{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0})}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\omega^{\prime\prime}_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\omega^{\prime\prime\prime}_{3}}
\textstyle{{\Bigr)}.}

Note that the pair of walks
\omega^{\prime}:=\omega^{\prime}_{1}\omega^{\prime\prime}_{1}
and
\omega^{\prime\prime}:=\omega^{\prime\prime}_{3}\omega^{\prime\prime\prime}_{3}
is concatenable, decomposed in such a way that
\ell(\omega^{\prime},\omega^{\prime\prime})=\ell(\omega^{\prime\prime}_{1})=\ell(\omega^{\prime\prime}_{3})
, that is,
\omega^{\prime\prime}_{3}={(\omega^{\prime\prime}_{1})}^{-1}
and
\omega^{\prime}_{1}\omega^{\prime\prime\prime}_{3}
is a reduced walk. Then we have
\omega_{1}\cdot(\omega_{2}\cdot\omega_{3})=(\omega^{\prime}_{1}\omega^{\prime\prime}_{1}\omega^{\prime\prime\prime}_{1})\cdot(\omega^{\prime}_{2}\omega^{\prime\prime}_{3}\omega^{\prime\prime\prime}_{3})=\omega^{\prime}_{1}\omega^{\prime\prime\prime}_{3}
since
\omega^{\prime\prime}_{1}\omega^{\prime\prime\prime}_{1}={(\omega^{\prime}_{2}\omega^{\prime\prime}_{3})}^{-1}
, and
(\omega_{1}\cdot\omega_{2})\cdot\omega_{3}=(\omega^{\prime}_{1}\omega^{\prime\prime}_{1}\omega^{\prime\prime}_{2})\cdot(\omega^{\prime}_{3}\omega^{\prime\prime}_{3}\omega^{\prime\prime\prime}_{3})=\omega^{\prime}_{1}\omega^{\prime\prime\prime}_{3}
since
\omega^{\prime\prime}_{1}\omega^{\prime\prime}_{2}={(\omega^{\prime}_{3}\omega^{\prime\prime}_{3})}^{-1}
.

Case 3.
Assume that
\ell+\ell^{\prime}>\ell(\omega_{2})
and take the next decompositions with
\ell=\ell(\omega^{\prime\prime}_{1}\omega^{\prime\prime\prime}_{1})=\ell(\omega^{\prime}_{2}\omega^{\prime\prime}_{2})
and
\ell^{\prime}=\ell(\omega^{\prime\prime}_{2}\omega^{\prime\prime\prime}_{2})=\ell(\omega^{\prime}_{3}\omega^{\prime\prime}_{3})
,

\textstyle{{\Bigl(}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\omega_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\omega^{\prime}_{1}}
\textstyle{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}[}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\omega^{\prime\prime}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\omega^{\prime\prime\prime}_{1}}
\textstyle{{\Bigr)\Bigl(}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\omega_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\omega^{\prime}_{2}}
\textstyle{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\omega^{\prime\prime}_{2}}
\textstyle{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}]}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\omega^{\prime\prime\prime}_{2}}
\textstyle{{\Bigr)\Bigl(}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\omega_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\omega^{\prime}_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\omega^{\prime\prime}_{3}}
\textstyle{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0})}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\omega^{\prime\prime\prime}_{3}}
\textstyle{{\Bigr)}.}

We take the decompositions
\omega^{\prime\prime}_{1}\omega^{\prime\prime\prime}_{1}
and
\omega^{\prime}_{3}\omega^{\prime\prime}_{3}
satisfying
\ell(\omega^{\prime\prime}_{1})=\ell(\omega^{\prime\prime}_{2})=\ell(\omega^{\prime\prime}_{3})
, which implies that
\omega^{\prime\prime}_{1}={(\omega^{\prime\prime}_{2})}^{-1}=\omega^{\prime\prime}_{3}
. Similarly, it follows that
\omega_{1}^{\prime\prime\prime}=(\omega_{2}^{\prime})^{-1}
and
\omega_{2}^{\prime\prime\prime}=(\omega_{3}^{\prime})^{-1}
.
Moreover, observe that the compositions
\omega^{\prime\prime}_{1}\omega^{\prime\prime\prime}_{3}
and
\omega^{\prime}_{1}\omega^{\prime\prime}_{3}
are reduced, since
\omega^{\prime\prime}_{1}=\omega^{\prime\prime}_{3}
and the walks
\omega_{1}
and
\omega_{3}
are reduced. Then we have

\omega_{1}\cdot(\omega_{2}\cdot\omega_{3})=(\omega^{\prime}_{1}\omega^{\prime\prime}_{1}\omega^{\prime\prime\prime}_{1})\cdot(\omega^{\prime}_{2}\omega^{\prime\prime\prime}_{3})=\omega^{\prime}_{1}\omega^{\prime\prime}_{1}\omega^{\prime\prime\prime}_{3}\qquad\text{and}\qquad(\omega_{1}\cdot\omega_{2})\cdot\omega_{3}=(\omega^{\prime}_{1}\omega^{\prime\prime\prime}_{2})\cdot(\omega^{\prime}_{3}\omega^{\prime\prime}_{3}\omega^{\prime\prime\prime}_{3})=\omega^{\prime}_{1}\omega^{\prime\prime}_{3}\omega^{\prime\prime\prime}_{3}.

In every case
\omega_{1}\cdot(\omega_{2}\cdot\omega_{3})=(\omega_{1}\cdot\omega_{2})\cdot\omega_{3}
, as claimed.
∎

Recall that for a marked ribbon graph
(G,\leq)
we denote by
\overleftarrow{\alpha}
the (unique) anti-walk with target vertex
\alpha
, see Subsection
3.4
, and take for simplicity
\overrightarrow{\alpha}:={(\overleftarrow{\alpha})}^{-1}
. For a reduced walk
\omega
of
G
with
\mathbf{s}(\omega)=\alpha
and
\mathbf{t}(\omega)=\beta
we take

{}_{+}\omega:=\overrightarrow{\beta}\cdot\omega,\qquad\omega_{+}:=\omega\cdot\overleftarrow{\alpha},\qquad\text{and}\qquad{}_{+}\omega_{+}:=({}_{+}\omega)_{+}={}_{+}(\omega_{+})=\overrightarrow{\beta}\cdot\omega\cdot\overleftarrow{\alpha}.

(6.2)

Observe that
{}_{+}\omega_{+}
is well-defined by Lemma
6.1
. Compare construction (
6.2
) with the analogous construction at the level of homotopy strings as in
[
Bobiński, 2011
, pp. 652-3]
. We may say that (
6.2
) unifies the several cases considered in
Bobiński [2011]
into one formula.

Lemma 6.3
.

Let
(G,\leq)
be a marked ribbon graph, and
\omega
be a non-trivial reduced walk of
G
. Then
{}_{+}\omega_{+}
is a non-trivial reduced walk, and at most one of the walks
{}_{+}\omega
or
\omega_{+}
is trivial.

Proof.

Observe that a reduced concatenation
\omega\cdot\omega^{\prime}
is trivial if and only if
\omega^{\prime}={\omega}^{-1}
. Moreover, we have
\mathbf{s}(\omega\cdot\omega^{\prime})=\mathbf{s}(\omega^{\prime})
and
\mathbf{t}(\omega\cdot\omega^{\prime})=\mathbf{t}(\omega)
. Take
\mathbf{s}(\omega)=\alpha
and
\mathbf{t}(\omega)=\beta
. Supposing that
{}_{+}\omega_{+}
is trivial, since
{}_{+}\omega_{+}=\overrightarrow{\beta}\cdot(\omega\cdot\overleftarrow{\alpha})
, we have
\overleftarrow{\beta}=\omega\cdot\overleftarrow{\alpha}
. In particular,
\mathbf{s}(\overleftarrow{\beta})=\mathbf{s}(\overleftarrow{\alpha})
, and by the uniqueness of the anti-walk starting at a vertex (cf. (
3.27
)) we have
\overleftarrow{\beta}=\overleftarrow{\alpha}
. Since
\overleftarrow{\alpha}=\omega\cdot\overleftarrow{\alpha}
, then necessarily
\omega
is trivial, a contradiction. Therefore,
{}_{+}\omega_{+}
is non-trivial. Supposing that both walks
{}_{+}\omega
and
\omega_{+}
are trivial, we have
\omega=\overrightarrow{\alpha}=\overleftarrow{\beta}
. That is,
\omega
is at the same time an anti-walk and the inverse of an anti-walk, which is impossible.
∎

Note that either
{}_{+}\omega
or
\omega_{+}
may be a trivial walk, and that this happens exactly when
\omega
or its inverse is an anti-walk (compare to
[
Bobiński, 2011
, Corollary 6.3]
). Recall that we treat
X^{\bullet}_{m,\omega}
as the zero complex for any
m\in\mathbb{Z}
and a trivial walk
\omega
, cf. Subsection
3.3
.

6.2. Auslander-Reiten triangles

The following result describes, up to isomorphism, all almost split (or Auslander-Reiten) triangles of string complexes in the derived category of a gentle algebra, cf.
Bobiński [2011]
,
Happel [1991]
. It may be viewed as a translation (and simplification, see (
6.2
)) of the construction of Bobiński
Bobiński [2011]
into the language of walks in the marked ribbon graph.

Proposition 6.4
.

Let
(Q,I)
be a gentle bound quiver with associated marked ribbon graph
(G,\leq)
. Then for any non-trivial reduced walk
\widetilde{\omega}
of
G
and any
m\in\mathbb{Z}
, there is an almost split triangle in
{\rm per}\,kQ/I
of the form

\textstyle{X^{\bullet}_{m,\widetilde{\omega}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X^{\bullet}_{m+m(\widetilde{\omega}),{}_{+}\widetilde{\omega}}\oplus X^{\bullet}_{m,\widetilde{\omega}_{+}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X^{\bullet}_{m+m(\widetilde{\omega}),{}_{+}\widetilde{\omega}_{+}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X^{\bullet}_{m-1,\widetilde{\omega}}}

(6.5)

where
m(\widetilde{\omega}):=\ell(\overrightarrow{\beta})-2
for
\beta:=\mathbf{t}(\widetilde{\omega})
, cf. (
6.2
).

Proof.

Take
\widetilde{\omega}=i_{1}\cdots i_{L+1}
(
L\geq 0
) with
\mathbf{s}(\widetilde{\omega})=\alpha
and
\mathbf{t}(\widetilde{\omega})=\beta
, and consider the corresponding homotopy string
\omega:=\triangledown(\widetilde{\omega})
of
(Q,I)
, see Lemma
3.22
and the proof of Proposition
3.21
(here we set
\triangledown(\mathbf{1}_{\alpha}):=\emptyset
for every trivial walk
\mathbf{1}_{\alpha}
of
G
, where
\emptyset
denotes the “empty” homotopy string as in
Bobiński [2011]
). We want to translate to our graphical model Bobiński’s description of Auslander-Reiten triangles
Bobiński [2011]
in terms of homotopy strings (cf. (
3.23
)),

\textstyle{\widehat{X}^{\bullet}_{m,\omega}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\widehat{X}^{\bullet}_{m+m^{\prime}(\omega),{}_{+}\omega}\oplus\widehat{X}^{\bullet}_{m,\omega_{+}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\widehat{X}^{\bullet}_{m+m^{\prime\prime}(\omega),{}_{+}\omega_{+}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\widehat{X}^{\bullet}_{m-1,\omega},}

(6.6)

for which our goal is to show that
\triangledown({}_{+}\widetilde{\omega})={}_{+}\omega
and
m(\widetilde{\omega})=m^{\prime}(\omega)
where
(m,\omega)\mapsto(m+m^{\prime}(\omega),{}_{+}\omega)
is the main construction of
Bobiński [2011]
. We briefly recall such construction following the terminology and notation of that paper.

The first step is to consider the permitted path
\sigma_{\omega}
of maximal length such that
\sigma_{\omega}\omega
is a homotopy presentation. We claim that
\ell(\sigma_{\omega})>0
if and only if the (plain) concatenation
\overrightarrow{\beta}\widetilde{\omega}
is a reduced walk, in which case
\sigma_{\omega}=\langle j^{\prime},i_{1}\rangle
where
j^{\prime}
and
i_{1}
are the last and first ordered edges of
\overrightarrow{\beta}
and
\widetilde{\omega}
respectively. Indeed, recall that
\mathbf{t}(\widetilde{\omega})=\mathbf{t}(i_{1})=\beta
means that
i_{1}=(h^{\beta}_{t},\iota h^{\beta}_{t})
for some
0\leq t\leq\ell^{\beta}
, where

\mathrm{z}^{-1}(\beta)=\{h^{\beta}_{0}>h^{\beta}_{1}>\ldots>h^{\beta}_{t-1}>h^{\beta}_{t}>\ldots>h^{\beta}_{\ell^{\beta}-1}>h^{\beta}_{\ell^{\beta}}\},

see Remark
3.12
, and that by definition of
\overrightarrow{\beta}
we have
j^{\prime}=(\iota h^{\beta}_{0},h^{\beta}_{0})
. Note that
\sigma_{\omega}=\beta_{1}\cdots\beta_{t}=(h^{\beta}_{0},h^{\beta}_{1})\cdots(h^{\beta}_{t-1},h^{\beta}_{t})
which shows that
\ell(\sigma_{\omega})=t
, and our claim follows since
t=0
if and only if
j^{\prime}={i_{1}}^{-1}
.

•

Assume that
\ell(\sigma_{\omega})>0
.
In this case
{}_{+}\omega:={\theta}^{-1}\sigma\omega
where
\sigma=\sigma_{\omega}
and
\theta=\theta_{\mathbf{t}(\sigma),-T(\sigma)}
is the forbidden path of maximal length with
\mathbf{t}(\theta)=\mathbf{t}(\sigma)
and
T(\theta)=-T(\sigma)
(cf.
[
Bobiński, 2011
, Lemma 6.1]
). It is easy to see that by the maximality of
\sigma
the path
\theta
is a forbidden thread, and actually
\theta=\triangledown(\overleftarrow{\beta})
. Since
\sigma=\langle j^{\prime},i_{1}\rangle
we have

\triangledown({}_{+}\widetilde{\omega})=\triangledown(\overrightarrow{\beta}\cdot\widetilde{\omega})=\triangledown({(\overleftarrow{\beta})}^{-1}\widetilde{\omega})={\triangledown(\overleftarrow{\beta})}^{-1}\langle j^{\prime},i_{1}\rangle\triangledown(\widetilde{\omega})={\theta}^{-1}\sigma\omega={}_{+}\omega.

(6.7)

Moreover,
m^{\prime}(\omega):=\ell(\theta)-1=\ell(\overrightarrow{\beta})-2=m(\widetilde{\omega})
, see (
3.26
).

•

Assume that
\ell(\sigma_{\omega})=0
.
By the claim above, this means that there is a reduced walk
\widetilde{\theta}
of maximal (positive) length such that

\overleftarrow{\beta}=\widetilde{\theta}\widetilde{\theta}^{\prime}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\widetilde{\omega}=\widetilde{\theta}\widetilde{\omega}^{\prime},

for some reduced walks
\widetilde{\theta}^{\prime}
and
\widetilde{\omega}^{\prime}
of
G
, so that
\overrightarrow{\beta}\cdot\widetilde{\omega}={(\widetilde{\theta}^{\prime})}^{-1}\widetilde{\omega}^{\prime}
. Similarly, Bobiński takes decompositions

\triangledown(\overleftarrow{\beta})=\theta\theta^{\prime}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\omega=\theta\omega^{\prime},

for a forbidden path
\theta
of maximal length
r(\omega)\geq 0
, and some walks
\theta^{\prime}
and
\omega^{\prime}
of
Q
(where
\theta^{\prime}=\theta_{\mathbf{t}\omega^{\prime},-T\omega^{\prime}}
in the notation of
Bobiński [2011]
). The definition of
{}_{+}\omega
together with the remaining verification
\triangledown(\overrightarrow{\beta}\cdot\widetilde{\omega})=\triangledown({(\widetilde{\theta}^{\prime})}^{-1}\widetilde{\omega}^{\prime})={}_{+}\omega
, which include several border cases, are shown in Table
1
. In this case, the integer
m^{\prime}(\omega)
is given by
m^{\prime}(\omega):=\ell(\theta^{\prime})+r(\omega)-1=\ell(\theta\theta^{\prime})-1=\ell(\overrightarrow{\beta})-2=m(\widetilde{\omega})
.

In any case, the homotopy string
\omega_{+}
is defined in
Bobiński [2011]
by
\omega_{+}:={({}_{+}({\omega}^{-1}))}^{-1}
, then using Lemma
3.22
(c) we have

\triangledown(\widetilde{\omega}_{+})=\triangledown(\widetilde{\omega}\cdot\overleftarrow{\alpha})={\triangledown(\overrightarrow{\alpha}\cdot{\widetilde{\omega}}^{-1})}^{-1}={\triangledown(_{+}({\widetilde{\omega}}^{-1}))}^{-1}={({}_{+}({\omega}^{-1}))}^{-1}=\omega_{+},

since clearly
{(\widetilde{\omega}_{1}\cdot\widetilde{\omega}_{2})}^{-1}={\widetilde{\omega}_{2}}^{-1}\cdot{\widetilde{\omega}_{1}}^{-1}
for any pair of reduced walks
\widetilde{\omega}_{1},\widetilde{\omega}_{2}
of
G
. The homotopy string
{}_{+}\omega_{+}
is given by
{}_{+}\omega_{+}:=({}_{+}\omega)_{+}
if
{}_{+}\omega\neq\emptyset
, and
{}_{+}\omega_{+}:={}_{+}(\omega_{+})
if
\omega_{+}\neq\emptyset
, and the integer
m^{\prime\prime}(\omega)
is given by
m^{\prime\prime}(\omega):=m^{\prime}(\omega)
and
m^{\prime\prime}(\omega):=m^{\prime}(\omega_{+})
respectively. By Lemma
6.1
we have, in any case,
\triangledown(_{+}\widetilde{\omega}_{+})={}_{+}\omega_{+}
, and clearly
m^{\prime\prime}(\omega)=m(\widetilde{\omega})
. Using Proposition
3.21
, this shows that the complexes of the triangle (
6.5
) in the main claim coincide with the complexes of the triangle (
6.6
), which completes the proof.
∎

\begin{array}[]{l l l l l l}&\text{Conditions}&\text{Definition of ${}_{+}\omega$}&\text{Walks of $G$}&\text{H. strings of $(Q,I)$}&\text{Example}\\[4.30554pt]
&\ell(\theta^{\prime})>0&{}_{+}\omega:={(\theta^{\prime})}^{-1}\omega^{\prime}&\overleftarrow{\beta}=\widetilde{\theta}\widetilde{\theta}^{\prime},\quad\text{\footnotesize(with $\ell(\widetilde{\theta}^{\prime})\geq 1$)}&\triangledown(\widetilde{\theta}^{\prime})=\theta^{\prime}&\widetilde{\omega}={2}^{-1}35\\
&\ell(\omega^{\prime})>0&&\widetilde{\omega}=\widetilde{\theta}\widetilde{\omega}^{\prime},\quad\text{\footnotesize(with $\ell(\widetilde{\omega}^{\prime})\geq 1$)}&\triangledown(\widetilde{\omega}^{\prime})={}^{[1]}\omega^{\prime}&\omega=\alpha_{1}(\alpha_{2}{\gamma}^{-1}_{1})\\
&\text{$\sigma_{1}(\omega^{\prime})$ direct}&&{}_{+}\widetilde{\omega}={(\widetilde{\theta}^{\prime})}^{-1}\widetilde{\omega}^{\prime}&\langle j,i\rangle=\sigma_{1}(\omega^{\prime})&{}_{+}\widetilde{\omega}=4{1}^{-1}35\\
\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}&\lx@intercol\text{Then $\triangledown({}_{+}\widetilde{\omega})={\triangledown(\widetilde{\theta}^{\prime})}^{-1}\langle j,i\rangle\triangledown(\widetilde{\omega}^{\prime})={(\theta^{\prime})}^{-1}\sigma_{1}(\omega^{\prime})({}^{[1]}\omega^{\prime})={(\theta^{\prime})}^{-1}\omega^{\prime}={}_{+}\omega$;}\hfil\lx@intercol&{}_{+}\omega={\beta}^{-1}_{2}(\alpha_{2}{\gamma}^{-1}_{1}).\\[12.91663pt]
&\ell(\theta^{\prime})>0&{}_{+}\omega:={(\theta^{\prime})}^{-1}\omega^{\prime}&\overleftarrow{\beta}=\widetilde{\theta}\widetilde{\theta}^{\prime},\quad\text{\footnotesize(with $\ell(\widetilde{\theta}^{\prime})>1$)}&\triangledown(\widetilde{\theta}^{\prime})={}^{[1]}\theta^{\prime}&\widetilde{\omega}=4{1}^{-1}3\\
&\ell(\omega^{\prime})>0&&\widetilde{\omega}=\widetilde{\theta}\widetilde{\omega}^{\prime},\quad\text{\footnotesize(with $\ell(\widetilde{\omega}^{\prime})\geq 1$)}&\triangledown(\widetilde{\omega}^{\prime})={}^{[1]}\omega^{\prime}&\omega=({\beta}^{-1}_{2}\alpha_{2})\\
&\text{$\sigma_{1}(\omega^{\prime})$ inverse}&&{}_{+}\widetilde{\omega}={(\widetilde{\theta}^{\prime})}^{-1}\widetilde{\omega}^{\prime}&\langle j,i\rangle={\sigma_{1}(\theta^{\prime})}^{-1}\sigma_{1}(\omega^{\prime})&{}_{+}\widetilde{\omega}=35{1}^{-1}3\\
\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}&\lx@intercol\text{Then $\triangledown({}_{+}\widetilde{\omega})={\triangledown(\widetilde{\theta}^{\prime})}^{-1}\langle j,i\rangle\triangledown(\widetilde{\omega}^{\prime})={(^{[1]}\theta^{\prime})}^{-1}{\sigma_{1}(\theta^{\prime})}^{-1}\sigma_{1}(\omega^{\prime})({}^{[1]}\omega^{\prime})={(\theta^{\prime})}^{-1}\omega^{\prime}={}_{+}\omega$;}\hfil\lx@intercol&{}_{+}\omega={\gamma}^{-1}_{1}{\beta}^{-1}_{3}({\beta}^{-1}_{2}\alpha_{2}).\\[12.91663pt]
&\ell(\theta^{\prime})>0&{}_{+}\omega:={(^{[1]}\theta^{\prime})}^{-1}&\overleftarrow{\beta}=\widetilde{\theta}\widetilde{\theta}^{\prime},\quad\text{\footnotesize(with $\ell(\widetilde{\theta}^{\prime})\geq 1$)}&\triangledown(\widetilde{\theta}^{\prime})={}^{[1]}\theta^{\prime}&\widetilde{\omega}={2}^{-1}1\\
&\ell(\omega^{\prime})=0&&\widetilde{\omega}=\widetilde{\theta},\quad\text{\footnotesize(with $\ell(\widetilde{\omega}^{\prime})=0$)}&\triangledown(\widetilde{\omega}^{\prime})=\emptyset&\omega=\alpha_{1}\\
&\text{---------------}&&{}_{+}\widetilde{\omega}={(\widetilde{\theta}^{\prime})}^{-1}&\text{---------------}&{}_{+}\widetilde{\omega}=4\\
\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}&\lx@intercol\text{Then $\triangledown({}_{+}\widetilde{\omega})={\triangledown(\widetilde{\theta}^{\prime})}^{-1}={(^{[1]}\theta^{\prime})}^{-1}={}_{+}\omega$;}\hfil\lx@intercol&{}_{+}\omega=\mathbf{1}_{\overline{4},-1}.\\[12.91663pt]
&\ell(\theta^{\prime})=0&{}_{+}\omega:=\omega^{\prime}&\overleftarrow{\beta}=\widetilde{\theta}{j}^{-1},\quad\text{\footnotesize(with $\ell(\widetilde{\theta}^{\prime})=1$)}&\triangledown({j}^{-1})=\mathbf{1}_{\overline{j}}&\widetilde{\omega}={2}^{-1}1{5}^{-1}\\
&\ell(\omega^{\prime})>0&&\widetilde{\omega}=\widetilde{\theta}\widetilde{\omega}^{\prime},\quad\text{\footnotesize(with $\ell(\widetilde{\omega}^{\prime})\geq 1$)}&\triangledown(\widetilde{\omega}^{\prime})={}^{[1]}\omega^{\prime}&\omega=\alpha_{1}\beta_{2}(\beta_{3})\\
&\text{$\sigma_{1}(\omega^{\prime})$ direct}&&{}_{+}\widetilde{\omega}=j\widetilde{\omega}^{\prime}&\langle j,i\rangle=\sigma_{1}(\omega^{\prime})&{}_{+}\widetilde{\omega}=4{5}^{-1}\\
\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}&\lx@intercol\text{Then $\triangledown({}_{+}\widetilde{\omega})=\triangledown(j\widetilde{\omega}^{\prime})=\langle j,i\rangle\triangledown(\widetilde{\omega}^{\prime})=\sigma_{1}(\omega^{\prime})({}^{[1]}\omega^{\prime})=\omega^{\prime}={}_{+}\omega$;}\hfil\lx@intercol&{}_{+}\omega=(\beta_{3}).\\[12.91663pt]
&\ell(\theta^{\prime})=0&{}_{+}\omega:={}^{[1]}\omega^{\prime}&\overleftarrow{\beta}=\widetilde{\theta},\quad\text{\footnotesize(with $\ell(\widetilde{\theta}^{\prime})=0$)}&\triangledown(\widetilde{\theta}^{\prime})=\emptyset&\widetilde{\omega}=4{5}^{-1}{3}^{-1}1{5}^{-1}\\
&\ell(\omega^{\prime})>0&&\widetilde{\omega}=\widetilde{\theta}\widetilde{\omega}^{\prime},\quad\text{\footnotesize(with $\ell(\widetilde{\omega}^{\prime})\geq 1$)}&\triangledown(\widetilde{\omega}^{\prime})={}^{[1]}\omega^{\prime}&\omega=\beta_{3}\gamma_{1}({\alpha}^{-1}_{2}\beta_{2}\beta_{3})\\
&\text{$\sigma_{1}(\omega^{\prime})$ inverse}&&{}_{+}\widetilde{\omega}=\widetilde{\omega}^{\prime}&\text{---------------}&{}_{+}\widetilde{\omega}=1{5}^{-1}\\
\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}&\lx@intercol\text{Then $\triangledown({}_{+}\widetilde{\omega})=\triangledown(\widetilde{\omega}^{\prime})={}^{[1]}\omega^{\prime}={}_{+}\omega$;}\hfil\lx@intercol&{}_{+}\omega=\beta_{2}\beta_{3}.\\[12.91663pt]
&\ell(\theta^{\prime})=0&{}_{+}\omega:=\emptyset&\overleftarrow{\beta}=\widetilde{\theta},\quad\text{\footnotesize(with $\ell(\widetilde{\theta}^{\prime})=0$)}&\triangledown(\widetilde{\theta}^{\prime})=\emptyset&\widetilde{\omega}={2}^{-1}1{4}^{-1}\\
&\ell(\omega^{\prime})=0&&\widetilde{\omega}=\widetilde{\theta},\quad\text{\footnotesize(with $\ell(\widetilde{\omega}^{\prime})=0$)}&\triangledown(\widetilde{\omega}^{\prime})=\emptyset&\omega=\alpha_{1}\beta_{2}\\
&\text{---------------}&&{}_{+}\widetilde{\omega}=\mathbf{1}_{\alpha},\quad\text{\footnotesize(with $\alpha:=\mathbf{s}(\widetilde{\omega})$)}&\text{---------------}&{}_{+}\widetilde{\omega}=\mathbf{1}_{\delta}\\
\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}&\lx@intercol\text{Then $\triangledown({}_{+}\widetilde{\omega})=\triangledown(\mathbf{1}_{\alpha})=\emptyset={}_{+}\omega$;}\hfil\lx@intercol&{}_{+}\omega=\emptyset.\end{array}

Table 1:
Verification of the identity
\triangledown({}_{+}\widetilde{\omega})={}_{+}\triangledown(\widetilde{\omega})
for the case
\ell(\sigma_{\triangledown(\widetilde{\omega})})=0
. We use notation of
Bobiński [2011]
. When possible,
j
and
i
denote the last and first ordered edge of
{(\widetilde{\theta}^{\prime})}^{-1}
and
\widetilde{\omega}^{\prime}
respectively. The given example refers to the gentle bound quiver
(Q^{(1)},I^{(1)})
and marked ribbon graph
(G^{(1)},\leq^{(1)})
of Example
6.9
.

In the following observation, whose proof follows from Proposition
3.21
(b) and
[
Bobiński, 2011
, Main Theorem Part I]
, we describe those almost split triangles of perfect complexes over gentle algebras which are not included in Proposition
6.4
.

Remark 6.8
.

Let
(Q,I)
be a gentle bound quiver with associated marked ribbon graph
(G,\leq)
. Then for any belt
\omega
of
(G,\leq)
, any
m\in\mathbb{Z}
and any almost split sequence
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mu^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\bigoplus_{t=1}^{n}\mu_{t}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mu^{\prime\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
in the category of automorphisms of finite dimensional vector spaces where
\mu^{\prime},\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{n},\mu^{\prime\prime}
are indecomposable automorphisms, there is an almost split triangle in
{\rm per}\,kQ/I
of the form

\textstyle{X^{\bullet}_{m,\omega,\mu^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\bigoplus_{t=1}^{n}X^{\bullet}_{m,\omega,\mu_{t}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X^{\bullet}_{m,\omega,\mu^{\prime\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X^{\bullet}_{m-1,\omega,\mu^{\prime}}.}
