Example 6.10
.

Take
A=kQ/I
for
(Q,I)
being the one loop gentle bound quiver
1
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{1}}
. Recall that then
\triangle(Q,I)=(G,\leq)
with
G={\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 29.07803pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-6.1985pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{{\alpha}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}{{}{{}{{}{{}}{}{{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}{{}}}}}}}}}{}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}{}}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}{{}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-29.07803pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.25555pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{1}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}{{}}{}{{}{{}}{}{{}}}}}{}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}{}\lx@xy@spline@}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}{}}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces}
and every (non-zero) indecomposable perfect complex is the string complex
X^{\bullet}_{m,1^{\ell}}
for some
m\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\ell\geq 1
, see Example
5.15
. Then
\overleftarrow{\alpha}={1}^{-1}
,
\overrightarrow{\alpha}=1
, thus

{}_{+}1^{\ell}=1^{\ell+1}
,
1^{\ell}_{+}=1^{\ell}\cdot{1}^{-1}=1^{\ell-1}
,
{}_{+}1^{\ell}_{+}=1\cdot 1^{\ell}\cdot{1}^{-1}=1^{\ell}
,
m(1^{\ell})=\ell(\overrightarrow{\alpha})-2=-1
. Therefore, every string complex
X^{\bullet}_{m,1^{\ell}}
induces Auslander-Reiten triangle of the form

\textstyle{X^{\bullet}_{m,1^{\ell}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X^{\bullet}_{m-1,1^{\ell+1}}\oplus X^{\bullet}_{m,1^{\ell-1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X^{\bullet}_{m-1,1^{\ell}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X^{\bullet}_{m-1,1^{\ell}}.}

(6.11)

In particular, for
\ell=1
the walk
1^{\ell-1}
is the trivial walk
\mathbf{1}_{\alpha}
so the triangle (
6.11
) has the form
X^{\bullet}_{m,1}\to X^{\bullet}_{m-1,11}\to X^{\bullet}_{m-1,1}\to X^{\bullet}_{m-1,1}
. This shows that the Auslander-Reiten quiver of the category
{\rm per}\,A
consists of one connected component of the shape
\mathbb{Z}\mathbb{A}_{\infty}
having the complexes
\{X^{\bullet}_{m,1}\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}
on the boundary, and the Auslander-Reiten translation on
{\rm per}\,A
is given by
\tau_{A}X^{\bullet}=X^{\bullet}[-1]
, compare with
[
Bobiński et al., 2004
, Theorem B]
.

6.3. The Coxeter transformation of a gentle algebra

If
A
is a Gorenstein algebra we have at our disposal a triangle autoequivalence
\tau_{A}:{\rm per}\,A\to{\rm per}\,A
, called the
Auslander-Reiten translation
of
{\rm per}\,A
, such that for any indecomposable perfect complex
Z^{\bullet}
the Auslander-Reiten triangle in
{\rm per}\,A
ending at
Z^{\bullet}
has the form
X^{\bullet}\to Y^{\bullet}\to Z^{\bullet}\to X^{\bullet}[1]
with
X^{\bullet}\cong\tau_{A}Z^{\bullet}
, see for instance
Geiss and Reiten [2005]
and
Ladkani [2020]
, cf. introduction to Section
6
. The induced automorphism
\Psi_{A}:K_{0}({\rm per}\,A)\to K_{0}({\rm per}\,A)
given by
\Psi_{A}([Z^{\bullet}]):=[\tau_{A}Z^{\bullet}]
is called the
Coxeter transformation
of
A
, cf.
Happel [1988]
and
Ladkani [2020]
, and its characteristic polynomial
\psi_{A}(z)\in\mathbb{Z}[z]
is called the
Coxeter polynomial
, which is a derived equivalence invariant of
A
, see
Ladkani [2020]
. Moreover, if
A
has finite global dimension, then the categories
{\mathcal{D}}^{b}(A)
and
{\rm per}\,A
coincide and therefore
{\mathcal{D}}^{b}(A)
has Auslander-Reiten triangles. In this case, the Coxeter transformation and the Coxeter polynomial of
A
were widely studied, for instance in
Lenzing and de la Peña [2008]
,
Happel [1997]
,
Ringel [1994]
,
de la Peña [2015]
.

For a gentle bound quiver
(Q,I)
with marked ribbon graph
(G,\leq)
, we collect in a
E(G)\times V(G)
-matrix
\widehat{J}
the incidence vectors of the anti-walks of
(G,\leq)
,

\widehat{J}:=[\underline{{\mathrm{inc}}}(\overleftarrow{\alpha})]_{\alpha\in V(G)},\quad\text{where $\underline{{\mathrm{inc}}}(i_{1}\cdots i_{L}i_{L+1})=\sum_{t=0}^{L}(-1)^{t}\mathbf{e}_{i_{t+1}}$ as in~\eqref{eq:defincvect}},

(6.12)

and use this matrix to give an expression for the Coxeter transformation
\Psi_{A}
of the gentle algebra
A=kQ/I
as a matrix in the basis of projectives, and to compute the Coxeter polynomial
\psi_{A}(z)
in terms of the AAG invariant of
(Q,I)
, see (
3.31
). Observe that if
(Q,I)
has finite global dimension, then the matrix
\widehat{J}
coincides with the incidence matrix
\mathrm{Inc}(\tilde{G},\tilde{\sigma})
of the forbidden marked ribbon graph
\blacktriangleup(Q,I)=(\tilde{G},\tilde{\leq},\tilde{\sigma})
of
(Q,I)
, see Definition
3.18
, up to a permutation and appropriate change of sign of its columns. Indeed, for an anti-walk
\overleftarrow{\alpha}
of
(G,\leq)
with source vertex
\beta:=\mathbf{s}(\overleftarrow{\alpha})
, via the bijection
\Phi_{2}=\mathbf{s}\circ\triangledown^{-1}
of (
3.27
), the
\beta
-th column of
\mathrm{Inc}(\tilde{G},\tilde{\sigma})
is given by
\mathrm{Inc}(\tilde{G},\tilde{\sigma})\mathbf{e}_{\beta}=\mathrm{Inc}(\tilde{G},\tilde{\sigma})\mathbf{e}_{\triangledown(\overleftarrow{\alpha})}=(-1)^{\ell(\overleftarrow{\alpha})+1}\underline{{\mathrm{inc}}}(\overleftarrow{\alpha})
, cf. (
4.25
). Since the
\alpha
-th column of
\widehat{J}
is
\widehat{J}\mathbf{e}_{\alpha}=\underline{{\mathrm{inc}}}(\overleftarrow{\alpha})
, and
\beta=\xi(\alpha)
where
\xi
is the permutation (
3.28
), then
\mathrm{Inc}(\tilde{G},\tilde{\sigma})\mathbf{e}_{\xi(\alpha)}(-1)^{\ell(\overleftarrow{\alpha})}=-\underline{{\mathrm{inc}}}(\overleftarrow{\alpha})=-\widehat{J}\mathbf{e}_{\alpha}
. This shows that

\mathrm{Inc}(\tilde{G},\tilde{\sigma})\Lambda=-\widehat{J},\qquad\text{with the orthogonal matrix $\Lambda:=P(\xi)S$,}

(6.13)

where
P(\xi)
is the permutation matrix given by
P(\xi)\mathbf{e}_{\alpha}=\mathbf{e}_{\xi(\alpha)}
, and
S
is the diagonal matrix
\mathrm{diag}[(-1)^{\ell(\overleftarrow{\alpha})}]
with
\alpha
running over the set of vertices of
G
. We will use matrix
\Lambda
in the proof of Theorem
6.17
below.

By applying arguments analogous to those for Claim
4.23
we obtain its dual variant as follows.

Claim 6.14
.

If
\overleftarrow{\alpha}=i_{1}\ldots i_{L}i_{L+1}
is the anti-walk of
(G,\leq)
having as target the vertex
\mathbf{t}(\overleftarrow{\alpha})=\alpha
, then the injective resolution of the string module
M_{\alpha}
induces the following exact sequence of
A
-modules

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M_{\alpha}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E_{i_{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E_{i_{2}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E_{i_{L}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E_{i_{L+1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}

In particular, we have (cf. Remark
4.4
and (
4.20
))

C_{A}^{{\rm{tr}}}\widehat{J}=\mathrm{Inc}(G).

(6.15)

Corollary 6.16
.

The Coxeter transformation
\Psi_{A}:K_{0}({\rm per}\,A)\to K_{0}({\rm per}\,A)
associated to the perfect category of a gentle bound quiver
(Q,I)
in the basis of projectives of
K_{0}({\rm per}\,A)
(with
A:=kQ/I
) is given by the
n\times n
-matrix

\Psi_{A}=\mathbf{I}_{n}-\widehat{J}\widehat{J}^{{\rm{tr}}}C_{A}^{{\rm{tr}}},

where
n=|Q_{0}|
and
\mathbf{I}_{n},C_{A}\in\mathbb{M}_{n,n}(\mathbb{Z})
are the identity matrix and the Cartan matrix of
A
, respectively.

Observe that if
A
has finite global dimension, then the claim of Corollary
6.16
follows easily from Theorem
4.8
and the observation (
6.13
). Indeed, recall that in this case the Coxeter transformation
K_{0}(\mathrm{mod}\,A)\to K_{0}(\mathrm{mod}\,A)
of
A
, as a matrix in the basis determined by simple modules, has the form
-C_{A}^{{\rm{tr}}}C_{A}^{-1}
(cf.
Lenzing and de la Peña [2008]
,
Assem et al. [2006]
). Using Remark
4.4
(b) we have the following commutative diagram,

\textstyle{K_{0}({\rm proj}\,A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{-C_{A}^{-1}C_{A}^{\rm{tr}}}
\scriptstyle{C_{A}}
\textstyle{K_{0}(\mathrm{mod}\,A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{-C_{A}^{\rm{tr}}C_{A}^{-1}}
\textstyle{K_{0}({\rm proj}\,A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{C_{A}}
\textstyle{K_{0}(\mathrm{mod}\,A),}

where we consider the basis
\{[P_{i}]\}_{i}
of the group
K_{0}({\rm proj}\,A)
. Recalling that the groups
K_{0}({\rm proj}\,A)
and
K_{0}({\rm per}\,A)
can be identified through the corresponding bases determined by indecomposable projective modules, the Coxeter transformation on
K_{0}({\rm per}\,A)
with respect to such basis is given by
-C_{A}^{-1}C_{A}^{\rm{tr}}
. Using Theorem
4.8
and equation (
6.13
), we have
\widehat{J}\widehat{J}^{\rm{tr}}=\mathrm{Inc}(\tilde{G},\tilde{\sigma})\Lambda\Lambda^{\rm{tr}}\mathrm{Inc}(\tilde{G},\tilde{\sigma})^{\rm{tr}}=\mathrm{Inc}(\tilde{G},\tilde{\sigma})\mathrm{Inc}(\tilde{G},\tilde{\sigma})^{\rm{tr}}=C_{A}^{-1}+C_{A}^{-{\rm{tr}}}
, that is,

\mathbf{I}_{n}-\widehat{J}\widehat{J}^{\rm{tr}}C_{A}^{\rm{tr}}=\mathbf{I}_{n}-(C_{A}^{-1}+C_{A}^{-{\rm{tr}}})C_{A}^{\rm{tr}}=-C_{A}^{-1}C_{A}^{\rm{tr}}=\Psi_{A}.

The following alternative proof includes the case of infinite global dimension.

Proof.

Note first that the matrix
\Psi_{A}
is
\mathbb{Z}
invertible with the inverse given by
\Psi_{A}^{-1}=\mathbf{I}_{n}-\widehat{J}\widehat{J}^{{\rm{tr}}}C_{A}
. Indeed, we have

\displaystyle\Psi_{A}\Psi_{A}^{-1}

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{I}_{n}-\widehat{J}\widehat{J}^{{\rm{tr}}}C_{A}^{{\rm{tr}}})(\mathbf{I}_{n}-\widehat{J}\widehat{J}^{{\rm{tr}}}C_{A})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{I}_{n}-\widehat{J}\widehat{J}^{{\rm{tr}}}C_{A}-\widehat{J}\widehat{J}^{{\rm{tr}}}C_{A}^{{\rm{tr}}}+\widehat{J}\widehat{J}^{{\rm{tr}}}C_{A}^{{\rm{tr}}}\widehat{J}\widehat{J}^{{\rm{tr}}}C_{A}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{I}_{n}-\widehat{J}\widehat{J}^{{\rm{tr}}}(C_{A}+C_{A}^{{\rm{tr}}})+\widehat{J}\widehat{J}^{{\rm{tr}}}\mathrm{Inc}(G)\mathrm{Inc}(G)^{{\rm{tr}}}

by (
6.15
)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{I}_{n}-\widehat{J}\widehat{J}^{{\rm{tr}}}(C_{A}+C_{A}^{{\rm{tr}}})+\widehat{J}\widehat{J}^{{\rm{tr}}}(C_{A}+C_{A}^{{\rm{tr}}})=\mathbf{I}_{n},

by Theorem
4.8
.

Assume now that
X^{\bullet}
is an indecomposable perfect complex of the gentle algebra
A
with Auslander-Reiten translation
\tau_{A}X^{\bullet}
, which is again a perfect complex since
A
is Gorenstein. Moreover, if
X^{\bullet}
is a string complex, then so is
\tau_{A}X^{\bullet}
by Proposition
6.4
. In this case, by Proposition
3.21
there are a reduced walk
\omega
of the associated marked ribbon graph
(G,\leq)
and
m\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
\tau_{A}X^{\bullet}
is isomorphic to
X^{\bullet}_{m,\omega}
. Taking
\alpha=\mathbf{t}(\omega)
and
\beta=\mathbf{s}(\omega)
we have

\displaystyle\Psi_{A}^{-1}[\underline{\mathrm{dim}}^{\rm p}(X^{\bullet}_{m,\omega})]

\displaystyle=(-1)^{m}[\mathbf{I}_{n}-\widehat{J}\mathrm{Inc}(G)^{{\rm{tr}}}]\underline{{\mathrm{inc}}}(\omega)

by Lemma
5.9
(a) and (
6.15
)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{m}[\underline{{\mathrm{inc}}}(\omega)-\widehat{J}(\mathbf{e}_{\alpha}-(-1)^{\ell(\omega)}\mathbf{e}_{\beta})]

by Lemma
5.5
(e)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{m}[\underline{{\mathrm{inc}}}(\omega)-\underline{{\mathrm{inc}}}(\overleftarrow{\alpha})+(-1)^{\ell(\omega)}\underline{{\mathrm{inc}}}(\overleftarrow{\beta})]

by definition of
\widehat{J}
.

On the other hand, using Lemma
5.5
(a,b) it is easy to show that
\underline{{\mathrm{inc}}}(\omega\cdot\omega^{\prime})=\underline{{\mathrm{inc}}}(\omega\omega^{\prime})
for any pair of reduced concatenable walks
\omega
and
\omega^{\prime}
. Then we have

\displaystyle\underline{{\mathrm{inc}}}({}_{+}\omega_{+})

\displaystyle=\underline{{\mathrm{inc}}}(\overrightarrow{\alpha}\cdot\omega\cdot\overleftarrow{\beta})=\underline{{\mathrm{inc}}}(\overrightarrow{\alpha}\omega\overleftarrow{\beta})=\underline{{\mathrm{inc}}}(\overrightarrow{\alpha})+(-1)^{\ell(\overrightarrow{\alpha})}\underline{{\mathrm{inc}}}(\omega\overleftarrow{\beta})

by Lemma
5.5
(a)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\ell(\overrightarrow{\alpha})+1}\underline{{\mathrm{inc}}}(\overleftarrow{\alpha})+(-1)^{\ell(\overrightarrow{\alpha})}(\underline{{\mathrm{inc}}}(\omega)+(-1)^{\ell(\omega)}\underline{{\mathrm{inc}}}(\overleftarrow{\beta}))

by Lemma
5.5
(a,b)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\ell(\overrightarrow{\alpha})}[\underline{{\mathrm{inc}}}(\omega)-\underline{{\mathrm{inc}}}(\overleftarrow{\alpha})+(-1)^{\ell(\omega)}\underline{{\mathrm{inc}}}(\overleftarrow{\beta})].

Using Lemma
5.9
(a), the definition
m(\omega):=\ell(\overrightarrow{\alpha})-2
and the above identities we get

\underline{\mathrm{dim}}^{\rm p}(X^{\bullet}_{m+m(\omega),{}_{+}\omega_{+}})=(-1)^{m+m(\omega)}\underline{{\mathrm{inc}}}({}_{+}\omega_{+})=(-1)^{m}[\underline{{\mathrm{inc}}}(\omega)-\underline{{\mathrm{inc}}}(\overleftarrow{\alpha})+(-1)^{\ell(\omega)}\underline{{\mathrm{inc}}}(\overleftarrow{\beta})]=\Psi_{A}^{-1}[\underline{\mathrm{dim}}^{\rm p}(X^{\bullet}_{m,\omega})].

By Proposition
6.4
, the complex
X^{\bullet}
is isomorphic to
X^{\bullet}_{m+m(\omega),{}_{+}\omega_{+}}
which shows that
\Psi_{A}[\underline{\mathrm{dim}}^{\rm p}(X^{\bullet})]=\underline{\mathrm{dim}}^{\rm p}(\tau_{A}X^{\bullet})
. This completes the proof, since
K_{0}({\rm per}\,A)
contains a basis consisting of classes of string complexes.
∎

Denote by
\mathrm{char}[M](z):=\det(z\mathbf{I}_{n}-M)
the characteristic polynomial of a
n\times n
matrix
M
and by
\psi_{A}(z):=\mathrm{char}[\Psi_{A}](z)
the Coxeter polynomial of an algebra
A
(when defined). We can now prove Theorem C from the introduction, which we restate here for convenience.

Theorem 6.17
.

Let
(Q,I)
be a gentle bound quiver. The Coxeter polynomial
\psi_{A}(z)
of the gentle algebra
A=kQ/I
with AAG invariant
\varphi_{A}
is given by

\psi_{A}(z)=(z-1)^{|Q_{1}|-|Q_{0}|}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}n>0\\
m\geq 0\end{subarray}}(z^{n}-(-1)^{n+m})^{\varphi_{A}(n,m)}.

(6.18)

Observe that the theorem shows that AAG invariant (more precisely, its “finite part”:
\varphi_{A}(n,m)
for
n>0
) completely determines the shape of the Coxeter polynomial of a gentle algebra.

Proof.

Let
(G,\leq)
be the marked ribbon graph associated with
(Q,I)
. We set
V=V(G)
and
E=E(G)
.
Consider the orthogonal matrix
\Lambda:=P(\xi)S
with
\xi
as in (
3.28
) and
S:=\mathrm{diag}[(-1)^{\ell(\overleftarrow{\alpha})}]_{\alpha\in V}
, see (
6.13
), and observe that
\Lambda=\mathbf{I}_{|V|}-\widehat{J}^{{\rm{tr}}}C_{A}\widehat{J}
. Indeed, using (
6.15
) and Lemma
5.5
(e), for any vertex
\alpha\in V
we have

(\mathbf{I}_{|V|}-\widehat{J}^{{\rm{tr}}}C_{A}\widehat{J})\mathbf{e}_{\alpha}=\mathbf{e}_{\alpha}-\mathrm{Inc}(G)^{{\rm{tr}}}\underline{{\mathrm{inc}}}(\overleftarrow{\alpha})=\mathbf{e}_{\alpha}-[\mathbf{e}_{\mathbf{t}(\overleftarrow{\alpha})}-(-1)^{\ell(\overleftarrow{\alpha})}\mathbf{e}_{\mathbf{s}(\overleftarrow{\alpha})}]=(-1)^{\ell(\overleftarrow{\alpha})}\mathbf{e}_{\xi(\alpha)}=P(\xi)S\mathbf{e}_{\alpha}=\Lambda\mathbf{e}_{\alpha}.

Using that for a
a\times b
matrix
M
and a
b\times a
matrix
N
we have
\mathrm{char}[MN](z)=z^{a-b}\mathrm{char}[NM](z)
, see for instance
[
Zhang, 1999
, 2.4]
, and that the characteristic polynomials of a square matrix and its transpose coincide, we get from Corollary
6.16

\displaystyle\psi_{A}(z)

\displaystyle=\det[z\mathbf{I}_{|E|}-(\mathbf{I}_{|E|}-\widehat{J}\widehat{J}^{\rm{tr}}C_{A}^{\rm{tr}})]=\mathrm{char}[-\widehat{J}\widehat{J}^{\rm{tr}}C_{A}^{\rm{tr}}](z-1)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{char}[-C_{A}\widehat{J}\widehat{J}^{\rm{tr}}](z-1)=(z-1)^{|E|-|V|}\mathrm{char}[-\widehat{J}^{\rm{tr}}C_{A}\widehat{J}](z-1)

\displaystyle=(z-1)^{|E|-|V|}\det[z\mathbf{I}_{|V|}-\mathbf{I}_{|V|}+\widehat{J}^{\rm{tr}}C_{A}\widehat{J}]=(z-1)^{|E|-|V|}\mathrm{char}[\Lambda](z).

Recall that the characteristic polynomial of an orthogonal matrix
\Lambda=P(\xi)S
with integer coefficients, where
\xi
is a permutation of
V
and
S=\mathrm{diag}(s_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in V}
with
s_{\alpha}\in\{\pm 1\}
for
\alpha\in V
, is given by

\mathrm{char}[\Lambda](z)=\prod_{\mathcal{O}\in V/\xi}[z^{|\mathcal{O}|}-\mathrm{sgn}(\mathcal{O})],\qquad\text{where $\mathrm{sgn}(\mathcal{O}):=\prod_{\alpha\in\mathcal{O}}s_{\alpha}$.}

By definition of the AAG invariant
\varphi_{A}
(
3.31
), and using the bijections
\xi=\mathbf{s}\circ\mathbf{t}^{-1}
and
\Phi=\Phi_{1}\Phi_{2}
defined in Subsection
3.4
, see (
3.27
), for fixed
n>0
and
\epsilon\in\{0,1\}
we have

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m\geq 0\\
n+m\equiv\epsilon(\mathrm{mod}\,2)\end{subarray}}\varphi_{A}(n,m)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|\{\mathcal{O}\in\mathcal{F}_{Q,I}/\Phi\mid\text{$n=n_{\mathcal{O}}=|\mathcal{O}|$ and $n_{\mathcal{O}}+m_{\mathcal{O}}\equiv\epsilon(\mathrm{mod}\,2)$}\}|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|\{\mathcal{O}^{\prime}\in V/\xi\mid\text{$n=|\mathcal{O}^{\prime}|$ and $\mathrm{sgn}(\mathcal{O}^{\prime})=(-1)^{\epsilon}$}\}|.

Note that for the last identity, if
\omega=\overleftarrow{\alpha^{0}}\overleftarrow{\alpha^{1}}\cdots\overleftarrow{\alpha^{a}}
is the face of
(G,\leq)
corresponding to an orbit
\mathcal{O}\in\mathcal{F}_{Q,I}/\Phi
(correspondingly, to an orbit
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}\in V/\xi
), then we have
n_{\mathcal{O}}+m_{\mathcal{O}}=\ell(\omega)
by (
3.29
), and

(-1)^{\ell(\omega)}=\prod_{t=0}^{a}(-1)^{\ell(\overleftarrow{\alpha^{t}})}=\prod_{\alpha\in\mathcal{O}^{\prime}}(-1)^{\ell(\overleftarrow{\alpha})}=\mathrm{sgn}(\mathcal{O}^{\prime}),

since
\Lambda:=P(\xi)S
with
S:=\mathrm{diag}[(-1)^{\ell(\overleftarrow{\alpha})}]
. This shows that
\mathrm{char}[\Lambda](z)=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}n>0\\
m\geq 0\end{subarray}}[z^{n}-(-1)^{n+m}]^{\varphi_{A}(n,m)}
, and the claim follows since
|Q_{1}|-|Q_{0}|=|E|-|V|
by (
4.10
).
∎

Example 6.19
.

Consider the three gentle algebras
A^{(i)}:=kQ^{(i)}/I^{(i)}
from Examples
3.4
(b) and
4.35
. Recall that all three algebras have the same AAG invariant
\{(4,6)\}
(that is,
\varphi_{A^{(i)}}(4,6)=1
). Then by applying Theorem
6.17
we find that the Coxeter polynomial in each case has the shape

\psi_{A^{(i)}}(z)=(z-1)^{6-5}(z^{4}-(-1)^{4+6})^{1}=z^{5}-z^{4}-z+1.

Recall that for an algebra
A
of finite global dimension the matrix formula
-C_{A}^{{\rm{tr}}}C_{A}^{-1}
for the Coxeter transformation implies that the Coxeter polynomial is palindromic (self-reciprocal), see
Lenzing and de la Peña [2008]
. The final example shows that Theorem
6.17
provides a method to search for non-palindromic Coxeter polynomials, which seem to be new in the literature.

Example 6.20
.

Let
(Q,I)
be the gentle bound quiver

\scriptstyle 1

\scriptstyle{\alpha_{1}}

\scriptstyle 2

\scriptstyle{\beta_{1}}
from Example
3.10
. We verify directly that its AAG invariant is
\{(2,0),(0,2)\}
. By applying Theorem
6.17
we get that the algebra
A=kQ/I
has the following non-palindromic Coxeter polynomial

\psi_{A}(z)=(z-1)^{0}(z^{2}-(-1)^{2})=z^{2}-1.
